Spotify Shoutouts

Columbia PM Lab capstone · With Spotify

Course projectTeam capstone, Columbia PM Lab with Spotify
Problem

A creator tool built with Spotify letting independent artists record personalized shoutouts for fans.

Deck ↗

Spotify Shoutouts

A creator tool, built with Spotify, that turns listening into a personal connection and a new income line for independent artists.

The 30-second version. Shoutouts lets independent artists record short personalized video and audio messages for fans, the way a fan might gift one for a birthday or a graduation. It was scoped as a Columbia PM Lab capstone built directly with Spotify, grounded in four research-backed artist personas and Spotify's own audience economics (monthly active listeners drive 60% of streams and 80% of merch purchases, super listeners drive 50% of ticket sales). The PM work here is the hard part of any two-sided marketplace: defining a North Star around the loop closing rather than vanity signups, setting pass/fail Beta gates benchmarked against Cameo and Patreon, and sequencing supply, demand, and trust so problems surface early rather than at scale.

🔗 Try it live: Concept built with Spotify, not a public product.

📄 Build artifact: Spotify PM Lab Final Presentation · persona research · metrics logic

🛠 Approach: Product strategy, persona research, KPI design, and a launch plan delivered as a final presentation. Built on existing Spotify infrastructure (payments, Creator Partnerships, trust and safety) rather than net-new systems.

Timeline: Columbia PM Lab capstone, partnered with Spotify. Strategy and metrics complete, scoped for a curated Beta.

1. Problem statement

Independent career artists sit in a structural trap Spotify research calls the "Sponsorship Gap." They have released consistently for three or more years and are trying to make music full-time, but they remain undercapitalized: too little income, too inconsistently, to invest in growth, with no label advances and no brand sponsorship. The competition is brutal. Spotify reports over 100,000 new songs released daily, and the payout distribution is steep. In Spotify's 2024 transparency reporting the 100,000th-ranked artist earned almost $6,000 while the 10,000th earned $131,000.

The escape hatch is not more reach. It is converting reach into durable fandom. Spotify's own data shows monthly active listeners are roughly 33% of an artist's audience but drive 60% of streams and 80% of merch purchases, and super listeners (around 2% of listeners) drive over 18% of streams and 50% of ticket sales. Existing tools (Discovery Mode, Marquee, Countdown Pages) help artists get heard. None of them give a fan a reason to pay an artist directly for a moment of connection, and none of them give the artist a new income line they fully control. Cameo proved the personalized-shoutout model works, but it lives off-platform and off the artist's audience graph.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Give Sponsorship Gap artists a new, optional income line they control, without giving up ownership or royalties.
  • Convert a fraction of an artist's existing listeners into paying, returning fans by turning a Shoutout into a giftable occasion.
  • Prove a two-sided marketplace loop closes before spending to scale it (supply reliability, demand satisfaction, minimum economic viability).
  • Strengthen Spotify's artist value proposition by adding a monetization surface native to the audience graph artists already have.

Non-goals

  • Not a royalty replacement. Positioned as supplemental and optional, to avoid the perception that Spotify is shifting income risk onto artists.
  • Not open self-enrollment at launch. A curated cohort protects quality before scale, so we onboard through Creator Partnerships first.
  • Not a new payments stack. We extend existing Spotify rails rather than build, to reduce risk and time to Beta.
  • Not a celebrity marketplace. The thesis is mid-tier independent artists (5,000 to 100,000 monthly listeners), not A-list talent.
  • Not a general video platform. Shoutouts are short, occasion-driven messages, not a content feed.

3. The product

A fan opens an artist's Spotify profile, requests a personalized Shoutout (often as a gift, with an occasion and a short note), pays, the artist records and delivers within an SLA window, and both sides leave the loop satisfied or get a frictionless refund.

StepFan sideArtist side
1. DiscoverSees Shoutouts offered on the artist profileOpts in through curated onboarding
2. RequestPicks occasion, writes a note, paysReceives request with context
3. FulfillWaits inside the 7-day SLARecords and delivers the message
4. CloseReceives the Shoutout, rates itGets paid; rating builds social proof
5. ReturnComes back for the next gifting occasionEarns repeat, predictable supplemental income

The economic model is a single equation the whole team can reason about: revenue per artist = monthly listeners x Shoutout request rate x average Shoutout value. Every product decision must move at least one of those three levers.

4. User stories

Independent artist (supply)

  • As an undercapitalized career artist, I want a new income line I fully control, so that I can reinvest in releases without signing away ownership.
  • As an artist with a real but modest fanbase, I want to turn my most dedicated listeners into paying fans, so that my income reflects loyalty and not just raw streams.
  • As a busy artist, I want clear delivery expectations and reminders, so that I can fulfill reliably without it taking over my week.

Fan (demand)

  • As a superfan, I want a personal message from an artist I love, so that I feel a real connection beyond passive streaming.
  • As a gift-giver, I want to buy a Shoutout for a friend's birthday or graduation, so that I give something memorable and specific.
  • As a first-time buyer, I want clear pricing and a visible rating, so that I trust the experience before I pay.

Edge and trust states

  • As a fan whose Shoutout is not delivered within 7 days, I want an automatic refund, so that a missed deadline never costs me money or trust.
  • As Spotify, I want baseline moderation on requests and deliverables, so that an abusive or off-brand message never reaches a fan.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Artist opt-in and onboarding through a curated cohort. Given an invited artist, when they complete onboarding, then they are activated and discoverable for Shoutouts.
  • Fan request and payment flow on existing Spotify rails. Given a fan on an opted-in artist's profile, when they submit a request and pay, then the artist receives the request and funds are held.
  • 7-day SLA with reminders and automatic refund on miss. Given an undelivered Shoutout, when 7 days elapse, then the fan is refunded automatically.
  • Delivery, rating, and payout. Given a delivered Shoutout, when the fan confirms or the window closes, then the artist is paid and a rating is recorded.
  • Baseline trust and safety filtering on requests and deliverables.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Gifting flow with occasion presets (birthday, anniversary, graduation) and scheduled delivery.
  • Pricing guidance for artists, anchored to a $40 midpoint for the emerging tier.
  • In-app prompts timed to high-intent moments (post-stream, profile visit).

Future (P2)

  • Self-enrollment at scale once curated quality is proven, modeled on Cameo's CameoX.
  • Bundles (Shoutout plus merch, Shoutout plus ticket presale).
  • Deeper trust and safety layering beyond keyword filtering.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Decision: North Star is monthly completed Shoutouts per active artist, not requests or signups.

Alternatives considered: total signups (rejected, vanity, signups do not equal value), gross revenue (rejected, masks a broken loop with a few whales). Why this won: in a two-sided marketplace, value only exists when the loop closes. An incomplete Shoutout means a refund, a frustrated fan, and churn on both sides. Measuring completions forces the team to defend the whole loop.

Decision: Curated cohort first, open enrollment never at launch.

Alternatives considered: open marketplace day one (rejected, quality and fulfillment risk would poison trust before the model is proven). Why this won: supply reliability is the single point of failure. Cameo's own move to a premium 24-hour expedited tier shows fulfillment is hard even at scale, so we optimize for reliability over reach first.

Decision: Revise the GA earnings target from $500 down to $300 per artist per month.

This is the unflattering one. The first draft set $500, which implied a 5x jump from the $100 Beta floor that the data did not support. Benchmarked against Cameo micro-influencer pricing ($20 to $50, $40 midpoint) and Patreon's sub-$200 median, $300 is defensible because it is explained entirely by volume (2 to 3 Shoutouts in Beta rising to 8 at GA) at a stable price. Honesty over an impressive-looking slide.

Decision: Repeat rate measured at 15% to 20% within 6 months, not 25% within 90 days.

Alternatives considered: a 90-day window (rejected, too short for occasion-based gifting). Why this won: gifting follows natural cycles. A January birthday buyer may return for a June anniversary. The 6-month window matches real behavior, and the 15% to 20% band is anchored to the superfan segment that drives outsized spending.

Decision: Position as supplemental income, not a royalty fix.

Alternatives considered: market it as the answer to streaming economics (rejected, creates PR risk and artist backlash). Why this won: framing it as optional and on the artist's terms protects both artist trust and Spotify's reputation.

7. UX

The core surface is the artist profile, which already carries fan intent. Shoutouts appears there as an offer, not a separate destination, so the request starts where attention already is. The fan flow is occasion-first (pick the moment, write the note, pay) to lean into gifting, which converts better than self-purchase. The artist flow is reliability-first: a clear request with context, a visible SLA countdown, reminders, and a one-tap deliver. Ratings surface prominently to build the social proof a new marketplace needs. Refund flows are designed to be frictionless on purpose, because trust is built by resolving problems well, not by pretending they never happen.

8. Success metrics and validation

The framework is two phases. Beta proves the loop closes. GA proves it scales into income and habit.

Beta gates (all pass/fail, all must pass to reach GA):

  • Completion rate: 70% of Shoutouts completed within 7 days. Below this, refund requests spike and trust erodes. Benchmarked to Cameo's 7-day SLA.
  • Artist opt-in: 40% of invited artists activate. Midpoint of the 30% to 50% range for curated outreach.
  • Artist NPS at least 30 and Listener NPS at least 30. The threshold where promoters outnumber detractors meaningfully; supply refers supply, demand refers demand.
  • Beta floor: $100 per artist per month. A proof-of-concept floor, roughly 2 to 3 Shoutouts at $40. If a curated, high-intent cohort cannot clear this, it is a product problem, not a scale problem.

GA targets (all due by month 6):

  • 8 completed Shoutouts per active artist per month (habit cadence, roughly 2 per week).
  • $300 per artist per month (volume-driven, defensible against comps).
  • 15% to 20% repeat rate within 6 months (anchored to superfan gifting behavior).

What didn't work, and what I'd change. The original GA economics were aspirational rather than benchmarked: $500 per artist and a 25% 90-day repeat rate both overstated what comparable platforms achieve, and I revised them down once I stress-tested against Cameo and Patreon data. I would also flag that all persona-level listener and income figures are clearly-labeled modeled assumptions, not validated actuals, because Spotify does not publish typical ratios by artist size. The honest gap in this work is that none of the Beta gates have been run with real artists yet. The next step is a live curated Beta to replace every benchmark with an observed number, and the framework is deliberately built to catch a failing loop early rather than amplify it.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • What is the real request rate from listener to buyer at the mid-tier? (data, after Beta)
  • Does gifting convert as strongly inside Spotify as Cameo's standalone context? (user, design)
  • What moderation depth is required before opening beyond a curated cohort? (legal, trust and safety)
  • How should FTC disclosure be handled if Shoutouts ever intersect with brand sponsorship? (legal)

v2 scope: open self-enrollment, bundles with merch and ticket presales, and richer trust and safety.

Shoutouts (on Spotify)CameoPatreon
Native to artist audience graphYesNoNo
Occasion / gifting focusYesYesNo
Recurring vs one-offOne-off, gifting cyclesOne-offRecurring
Target tierMid-tier independentsCelebritiesCreators broadly

GTM hypothesis: seed a curated cohort through Creator Partnerships, prove the loop in Beta, then scale enrollment. Why defensible: the audience graph is the moat. No standalone competitor can place a personalized monetization surface on the exact profile where a fan's intent already lives.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: named a structural, evidence-backed problem (the Sponsorship Gap) and tied a product to the specific audience segments Spotify's own data says drive value.
  • Trade-off reasoning: curated vs open, supplemental vs royalty-fix, and a willingness to revise headline targets down when the data did not support them.
  • Marketplace fluency: a North Star built on the loop closing, sequenced gates for supply, demand, then economics.
  • Validation discipline: every metric benchmarked to a named comp, every assumption labeled, no fabricated actuals.
  • Stakeholder communication: the whole strategy compresses to one revenue equation and a single takeaway slide, scaling from a 30-second skim to a full deck.
  • Partner credibility: scoped to build on existing Spotify infrastructure and teams, not net-new systems, which is how real platform features ship.

Built and documented by Juliana Cardona Mejia, MBA '26, Columbia Business School

Product requirements

Work Nooks

Barcelona MA capstone · Summa Cum Laude

DemoMaster's capstone, Universitat de Barcelona
Problem
Remote workers want a quiet place near home that is not a pricey coworking membership.
North star
8% of searches end in a paid reservation (target)

A marketplace turning quiet neighborhood spaces into bookable work nooks.

Live ↗Deck ↗

Work Nooks

A marketplace that turns quiet neighborhood cafés into bookable focus seats, so remote workers find a nearby place to work and hosts earn from empty tables.

The 30-second version. Work Nooks lets a remote worker in Barcelona find and reserve a verified café workspace for as little as 4 euros a day, while café owners list, manage, and earn from seats that would otherwise sit empty. It is a true two-sided marketplace: a mobile-first PWA for workers and a desktop SaaS dashboard for owners, with Stripe Connect payouts, a 90/10 split, geofenced check-in, and a five-axis review system. This was a Barcelona MA Digital Product Management capstone graded Summa Cum Laude, shipped from a 55-slide pitch to a live, browsable product seeded with 30 real cafés across 8 neighborhoods.

🔗 Try it live: Open Work Nooks

📄 Build spec: original build prompt

🛠 Stack: React, Supabase (auth, DB, RLS, storage, edge functions), Stripe Connect, Mapbox, Twilio. Mobile-first PWA for workers, desktop dashboard for owners.

Timeline: Barcelona MA capstone (Summa Cum Laude). Live prototype, seeded with 30 real Barcelona cafés.

Live prototype shows list view; map view pending.

Capstone evidence: A 55-slide pitch supports the capstone.

1. Problem statement

Remote and hybrid workers want a third place to work that is not their apartment and not a 300-euro-a-month coworking membership. Cafés are the obvious answer, but the experience is a gamble: you do not know if a place has wifi that holds, an outlet near a seat, or whether the staff will welcome a three-hour laptop session or shoot you looks after one coffee. On the other side, café owners have empty tables in the off-peak hours between breakfast and lunch and no clean way to monetize them, and no proof of who is actually coming in.

Existing options each fail one side. Coworking spaces (WeWork, local equivalents) are priced for daily commuters, not a worker who needs a seat twice a week. Café-finder apps and Google Maps tell you a café exists but not whether it works for a laptop day, and they do nothing for the owner. Work Nooks closes the gap with a verified, bookable seat at a café price (from 4 euros a day) and gives owners a dashboard, payouts, and foot-traffic proof.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Give remote workers a nearby, verified seat they can trust and book in under a minute, at a café price.
  • Give café owners a new revenue line from off-peak empty seats, with payouts and proof of foot traffic.
  • Make "verified" mean something: confirmed wifi, seating, and a genuine welcome to stay, not a self-claimed badge.
  • Build trust on both sides through honest reviews and frictionless refunds, so a bad experience never poisons the marketplace.

Non-goals

  • Not a coworking subscription. No monthly pass at v1, because the wedge is the pay-per-day café price, not a membership.
  • Not multi-city. Barcelona only, because density and verification quality beat breadth at launch.
  • Not pick-your-seat or order-from-app. Out of scope to keep the booking flow simple and the café relationship light.
  • Not a community feed or chat. The worker wants a seat, not a social network.
  • Cafés first; co-working spaces listed where verified. Bars and restaurants without verified workspace amenities remain out of scope.

3. The product

Two surfaces, one marketplace. Workers get a mobile PWA; owners get a desktop dashboard.

StepWorker (mobile PWA)Owner (desktop dashboard)
1. ListSearches by neighborhood, time, and filtersAdds a location with photos, hours, capacity, tier
2. VerifySees only verified cafés in resultsUploads business license; Work Nooks verifies within 48h
3. BookReserves a seat, pays via StripeSees the reservation on the day's timeline
4. ArriveOne-tap check-in, geofenced within 100mKnows who is expected and when
5. ReviewRates wifi, ambience, seating, comfortResponds to reviews; sub-4.0 over 30 auto-pauses listing
6. EarnBuilds a stamp card toward a free coffeeReceives weekly Stripe Connect payout (90% of fare)

The economic model is a 90/10 split: the owner keeps 90% of the fare, Work Nooks takes 10% as the marketplace fee, captured at confirmation, paid out weekly via Stripe Connect Express.

4. User stories

Remote worker

  • As a remote worker, I want to find a verified café near me with good wifi and outlets, so that I can have a focused work day without gambling on an unknown spot.
  • As a worker on a budget, I want to pay a café price (from 4 euros) rather than a coworking membership, so that I only pay for the days I actually need.
  • As a returning worker, I want a stamp card that rewards loyalty, so that working here regularly earns me a free coffee or a free day.

Café owner

  • As a café owner, I want to earn from empty off-peak seats, so that my quiet hours generate revenue instead of nothing.
  • As an owner, I want a dashboard showing reservations, ratings, and a foot-traffic uplift versus my organic baseline, so that I can see Work Nooks is actually working.
  • As an owner, I want weekly automatic payouts, so that I do not have to chase money.

Edge and trust states

  • As a worker who arrives but the café is not ready, I want an automatic full refund, so that a no-show on their side never costs me.
  • As a worker trying to check in from home, I want the geofence to block it, so that check-ins are honest and reviews are trustworthy.
  • As an owner whose rating drops below 4.0 over 30 reviews, I want my listing auto-paused with a clear message, so that I fix the issue before more workers have a bad day.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Role-aware auth and onboarding (worker, owner, or both). Given a new user, when they pick a role at signup, then they see the correct surface and can hold both roles on one account.
  • Verified-only search and discovery with filters in list view. Given a worker searching, when results render, then only verified cafés appear.
  • Reservation and Stripe payment. Given a worker on a café detail page, when they reserve and pay (Stripe test), then they get a confirmation and the owner sees the booking.
  • Geofenced check-in and five-axis review at checkout. Given a worker at the café, when they are within 100m, then check-in succeeds; when they check out, then they are prompted to rate wifi, ambience, seating, and comfort.
  • Owner verification flow within 48h. Given an owner who adds a location with a license, when ops verifies within 48h, then the status flips to verified and the listing goes live.
  • 90/10 fee capture and weekly Stripe Connect payouts.
  • Auto-pause on sub-4.0 average over 30 reviews.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Mapbox map: P1, not yet live.

  • Stamp card (5 visits to a free coffee, 10 to a free day).

  • Owner analytics: revenue over time vs organic baseline, foot-traffic heatmap, review trends.

  • Referral credit (5 euros split between sender and recipient).

Future (P2)

  • Veridas API for automated verification (replacing manual ops review).
  • Workspace types beyond cafés (bars, restos, coworking).
  • Additional cities and a monthly pass tier.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Decision: Verification is manual ops review, not self-attestation.

Alternatives considered: trust owner-claimed amenities (rejected, a wrong "has wifi" badge breaks trust on the worker's first try), full automation at launch (rejected, no verification API was good enough yet). Why this won: "verified" is the entire value proposition for the worker. A human confirming wifi and seating within 48 hours is slow but trustworthy, and an auto-pause trigger keeps quality honest over time. The cost is roughly 30 minutes of ops time per verification, around 200 in Q1, which is the price of trust.

Decision: Café price wedge (from 4 euros), no membership in v1.

Alternatives considered: a monthly coworking-style pass (rejected, that is the incumbent model and the reason workers are underserved). Why this won: the surprising, clickable fact is that a verified workspace costs the price of a coffee. Pricing per day removes the commitment that keeps occasional workers out of coworking entirely.

Decision: Geofenced check-in within 100m, even though it adds friction.

Alternatives considered: trust-based check-in (rejected, breaks review integrity and enables fare gaming). Why this won: honest check-ins are what make reviews and foot-traffic proof credible to owners. The friction is the point.

Decision: Two distinct surfaces (mobile worker, desktop owner) instead of one responsive app.

Alternatives considered: one shared responsive layout (rejected, a worker booking on the go and an owner managing analytics have opposite needs). Why this won: the worker flow is glanceable and one-handed; the owner flow is a data dashboard. Forcing them into one layout would have compromised both. The trade-off is more to build and maintain.

Decision: Barcelona only, 30 real cafés, not a thin national demo.

Alternatives considered: a broad but shallow multi-city seed (rejected, density and real verified supply matter more than a big map with empty pins). Why this won: a worker in Gràcia needs real options in Gràcia. 30 verified cafés across 8 neighborhoods reads as real coverage, not a demo.

7. UX

The worker surface is search-first and deliberately unfussy. The hero does the only real selling ("Find a place to work. In your neighborhood. From 4 euros."), and everything below is functional: filter chips, café cards with a verified-workspace badge and a star rating, and a planned Mapbox map toggle (P1, not yet live). The voice is practical, local, and Barcelona-fluent (neighborhood names, euros) without performing it; the capstone polish shows as clarity, not flourish. The café detail page is the conversion surface, so it leans on real café photos and a clear attribute row (wifi, outlets, window seats, quiet) over stock imagery. The owner dashboard is calm and metric-led: a revenue row, today's reservation timeline, and a sub-4-star alert. Copy never says "user" or "customer"; it says "you," "the shop," and "the worker."

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Search to reservation conversion. Target 8% of searches that end in a paid reservation, measured from the search_executed to reservation_completed event chain.
  • Check-in rate. Target at least 80% of reservations resulting in a geofenced check-in, measured from checked_in over reservation_completed.
  • Owner activation. Target 60% of signed-up owners completing Stripe Connect and getting a first location verified.

Lagging indicators

  • Worker repeat rate. Target 35% of workers booking a second day within 30 days (stamp-card progress is the proxy).
  • Verified supply quality. Target average rating at or above 4.2 across active cafés, with auto-pause keeping sub-4.0 (over 30 reviews) listings out of search.
  • Owner-reported foot-traffic uplift vs organic baseline, surfaced in the analytics tab.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. The honest limitation is that this is a seeded prototype, so the metrics above are targets, not observed results, and the conversion, activation and repeat figures are targets, not observed. Manual verification is the right call for trust but does not scale; the roadmap to a Veridas-style API exists precisely because 30 minutes per listing breaks past a few hundred cafés. The original copy also buried the single most clickable fact, the 4-euro price, inside the meta description rather than above the fold, and the verified badge floated without explaining what was verified. Both were sharpened (the price now anchors the hero, the badge now reads "verified workspace"), but the broader lesson holds: for a search-first utility, the most surprising concrete fact should lead, and trust signals must say what they actually guarantee.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • What is the real off-peak fill rate owners can expect, and is 10% the right take? (data, after pilot)
  • Does manual verification hold quality at 200-plus cafés, or is Veridas needed sooner? (ops, eng)
  • How do we handle a café that is verified but degrades (slow wifi creep) between reviews? (data, ops)
  • What insurance and liability framing do workers and owners need? (legal)

v2 scope: Veridas verification, a monthly pass tier, workspace types beyond cafés, and expansion to a second city.

Work NooksCoworking (WeWork-style)Café-finder apps / Maps
PriceFrom 4 euros/dayMembership / day pass premiumFree, but no booking
Verified for laptop workYes (wifi, outlets, welcome)YesNo
Bookable seatYesYesNo
Earns for the café ownerYesNoNo

GTM hypothesis: seed real verified supply in dense Barcelona neighborhoods, acquire workers through the surprising café-price hook, and let stamp cards and referrals drive repeat. Why defensible: verified supply is the moat. A café-finder cannot match a bookable, verified, owner-monetized seat, and coworking cannot match the café price.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: a real two-sided gap (workers gamble on cafés, owners waste off-peak seats) that neither coworking nor map apps solve.
  • Trade-off reasoning: manual verification over self-attestation, café price over membership, geofence friction in service of trust, two surfaces over one.
  • Technical fluency: a full marketplace stack (Stripe Connect splits and payouts, RLS-scoped data, geofencing, Mapbox) scoped and shipped as a working prototype.
  • Validation discipline: targets framed honestly as targets, real seeded supply rather than empty pins, explicit targets, not observed results.
  • Roadmap sense: a clear path from manual verification to an automated API, and from one city and pay-per-day to multi-city with a membership tier.
  • Communication: capstone-grade clarity (Summa Cum Laude) from a 55-slide pitch to a browsable live product with a search-first, local voice.

Built and documented by Juliana Cardona Mejia, MBA '26, Columbia Business School

Product requirements

Grüns Twin

Columbia marketing case competition

Course projectColumbia marketing case competition
Problem
People stop taking daily vitamins because motivation alone fades.
North star
50% of activated users keep a streak to day 30 (target)

An AI digital twin of your ideal self that evolves as you take your vitamins, built for the Grüns case competition.

Live ↗Deck ↗

Grüns Digital Twins (Grüns Twin)

One-sentence summary: An AI digital twin of your ideal self that evolves as you take your vitamins, built for the Grüns case competition.

The 30-second version. I proposed an identity-based habit loop: an evolving ideal-self twin, small accountability squads and a free-sample referral. The portfolio contains the competition brief and AI-assisted prototype, not a customer rollout.

Timeline: Columbia marketing case competition.

Team and role: Twin concept, brief and AI-assisted build.

Live: https://grunsdigitaltwin.lovable.app

Deck: https://drive.google.com/file/d/11Pcn4aMKJ-0vAOfbHYKqN21kUe8zdwvN/view

1. Product Essence

Grüns turns daily vitamin-taking into a journey of self-transformation. Users create an AI-generated digital twin representing their ideal self, a playful vision of who they become through consistent health habits.

The twin evolves daily based on behavior, unlocking badges, milestones, and shareable moments. It's modern identity-based habit design, wrapped in Gen Z aesthetics and social mechanics.

Unlike generic habit trackers, Grüns makes your health visible, social, and personally meaningful.

2. Who Is the Customer

Primary: Current users of Grüns

Secondary: Friends of current users of Grüns

3. Core Customer Problem

People struggle to stay consistent with health habits because motivation alone isn't enough.

Three Layers to This Problem

Lack of Intrinsic Consistency Systems

Users want to be healthier, but they don't have reliable systems or routines to make vitamin-taking automatic. Motivation fluctuates, so behavior breaks.

Low Engagement / Habit Friction

Taking vitamins is boring, solitary, and easy to forget. There's no built-in reward loop or emotional pull to keep users engaged daily.

Weak Accountability Structures

Users are more consistent when someone is watching or doing it with them, but they lack:

  • Social reinforcement (friends doing it too)
  • External accountability (being seen or tracked)

4. User Stories

Consistency / Self-Optimization

As a Gen Zer taking Grüns vitamins, I want a simple and consistent routine so that I can reliably become the best version of myself.

Social Reinforcement

As a Grüns vitamin taker, I want to build healthy habits with my friends so that staying consistent feels social, fun, and easier to maintain.

Accountability / Visibility

As a person taking Grüns vitamins, I want to share my health journey publicly so that I feel accountable and motivated to stay committed.

5. Product: Grüns

Grüns turns healthy habits into social, AI-powered digital identities that users share to make wellness visible, engaging, and self-expressive.

How It Works (Product Essence)

  • Users create an AI-generated "digital twin" of their ideal self, a playful version of who they become when they consistently take their vitamins, like a modern-day Popeye transformation.
  • The twin evolves based on daily behavior (taking Grüns or skipping it), tracking progress and unlocking shareable badges and milestones.
  • Users can share progress updates, twin evolution, and how they are feeling.
  • Social accountability is built through "twin packs" or squads, where friends track goals together. Staying consistent unlocks rewards like discounts.
  • When users invite friends, those friends receive a free sample to start, join the same journey if they accept, and can see each other's progress. Users can give and receive kudos within their squad, and stronger consistency within a tribe unlocks additional rewards.

6. User Journey

Phase 1: Onboarding

  • User signs up via mobile web (email, phone, or social login)
  • Creates a one-sentence ideal self description: e.g., 'Fit, strong, kind, centered, fearless person ready to run a triathlon on 24 hours notice'
  • Selects traits from guided options (fit, kind, disciplined, focused, fearless, resilient, etc.)
  • AI generates a visual or conceptual twin representation
  • Shares twin on social media (Instagram, TikTok, etc.)
  • Given option to download app or engage via SMS for lightweight experience

Phase 2: Daily Engagement

  • User receives daily check-in prompt: 'Did you take your Grüns today?'
  • System responds dynamically (celebration for yes, light nudge for no)
  • User sees real-time streak counter and progress dashboard
  • Twin evolves visually or narratively based on consistency
  • Badges unlock at milestones (3 days, 7 days, 30 days, etc.)

Phase 3: Social & Sharing

  • User shares progress, twin evolution, or mood updates to social platforms
  • System auto-generates shareable content optimized for each platform
  • Friends and followers can tap through to join the journey

Phase 4: Squad & Accountability

  • User invites friends to join their 'Grüns tribe' (squad)
  • Invited friends receive a free sample offer to start
  • Shared progress visibility across the tribe builds mutual accountability
  • Users give and receive 'kudos' within their squad
  • Stronger tribe consistency unlocks additional rewards and perks

7. User Personas

Persona 1: Maya (The Identity-Seeker)

Age: 21, college student, aspiring health influencer

Motivation: Wants to be visibly healthy and disciplined; craves public recognition

Pain Point: Gets bored with routines; needs external validation to stay consistent

Grüns Value: She loves that her digital twin is proof of her commitment. Sharing it on Instagram makes her feel seen.

Persona 2: Darius (The Accountability Buddy)

Age: 19, college student, values community

Motivation: Thrives when doing things with friends; dislikes going solo

Pain Point: Quits habits when friends aren't invested

Grüns Value: He joined because his roommate invited him. Now they check in daily and celebrate together.

Persona 3: Zara (The Optimizer)

Age: 23, works in tech, self-improvement obsessed

Motivation: Loves data, badges, and visible progress

Pain Point: Vitamin-taking feels like a dead habit with no feedback loop

Grüns Value: The daily check-in, streak counter, and evolving twin give her tangible proof of progress. She tracks it obsessively.

8. MVP Requirements (Ordered by Priority)

Tier 1: Must-Have Core Features

1.1 Account Access & Signup
  • Mobile web sign-up (email, phone, or social login)
  • SMS engagement option for lightweight access
1.2 Digital Twin Creation
  • One-sentence ideal self input with guided examples
  • Trait selection from curated Gen Z options
  • AI-generated twin preview
1.3 Daily Check-In
  • Yes/No vitamin intake prompt
  • Dynamic responses (celebration or nudge)
  • Real-time streak counter
  • Simple progress dashboard
1.4 Twin Evolution & Badges
  • Visual/narrative twin change based on consistency
  • Streak-based badge unlocks (3, 7, 14, 30, 60, 90 days)

Tier 2: Social & Squad

2.1 Social Sharing
  • Share twin creation to Instagram, TikTok, Twitter
  • Share progress updates and badges
  • Auto-generated social copy optimized per platform
2.2 Friend Invitations
  • Shareable squad invite link
  • Track accepted/pending invites
  • Associate free sample offer to invites
2.3 Squad Dashboard
  • View squad members and their streaks
  • Send/receive kudos within squad
  • Track squad-level consistency milestones

Tier 3: Rewards & Retention

3.1 Rewards Dashboard
  • Streak-based reward unlocks (discounts on Grüns, exclusive offers)
  • Referral rewards (friend joins and starts)
  • Squad milestone bonuses
3.2 Engagement Mechanics
  • Restock reminders (when supply is low)
  • Streak break re-engagement nudges
  • Push notifications and SMS for opt-in users

9. Campaign Success Metrics

Primary Metric: 30-Day Retention

Target: 50% of activated users maintain a streak and re-engage by day 30. This signals habit stickiness.

Secondary Metrics

  • Daily Active Users (DAU) / Monthly Active Users (MAU)
  • Average Streak Length (target: 15+ days for active users)
  • Squad Growth Rate (invites sent, friends converted)
  • Social Shares (shares per activated user)
  • Repeat Purchase Rate (tied to app engagement)
  • Referral Conversion Rate (friend invites that lead to purchase)

10. Channels & Acquisition Strategy

Channel 1: Organic

Description: Word-of-mouth, social shares, viral loops

Timeline: Week 1+

How It Works: Users share twins on Instagram and TikTok. Friends click. Friends join.

CAC: $0

Target: 20-30% of acquisition by Month 3

Metrics to Track: Viral coefficient, share rate, organic user signup source

Channel 2: Influencer

Description: Gen Z micro-influencers (50K-500K followers)

Timeline: Week 5-8

How It Works: Partner with 10 micro-influencers. Gifted Grüns + early app access. Organic content.

CAC: $0.50-1.00 per user (gift value / acquired users)

Target: 40-50% of early adopters

Metrics to Track: Influencer reach, engagement rate, conversion to signup, cost per acquisition

Channel 3: Paid Social

Description: TikTok & Instagram Reels (Gen Z targeting)

Timeline: Week 9+

How It Works: Ads showing twin transformations and squad moments. CPC to app or web.

CAC: $2-3

Target: 30-40% of acquisition (scale phase)

Metrics to Track: ROAS (return on ad spend), CPC, conversion rate, cohort retention

Channel 4: Referral

Description: In-app invite + free sample offer

Timeline: Week 1+ (built-in from launch)

How It Works: User invites friend. Friend gets free sample offer. Friend joins squad.

Viral Coefficient: 1.2-1.5 (each user brings 1.2-1.5 friends)

CAC: $0 (internally funded)

Target: Compounding growth engine

Metrics to Track: Invite send rate, acceptance rate, viral coefficient, referral conversion to purchase

Channel Blend Strategy

Phase 1: Soft Launch (Weeks 1-4)

  • Primary channels: Organic + Referral
  • Goal: Validate product, optimize retention
  • Success metric: 50% D30 retention, 30% social share rate

Phase 2: Influencer Launch (Weeks 5-8)

  • Primary channels: Organic + Influencer
  • Goal: Expand reach, prove product-market fit
  • Success metric: 5K+ installs, 40%+ engagement rate
  • Expected CAC: <$1

Phase 3: Full Public Launch & Scale (Weeks 9+)

  • Primary channels: Paid Social + Organic + Referral
  • Influencer: Ongoing partnerships + ambassadors
  • Goal: Scale to 50K+ MAU
  • CAC target: $2-3 with <8 week payback

Key Insights by Channel

  • Organic is strongest: Users want to share because it's identity-based. Viral loop compounds.
  • Influencer accelerates: Authentic Gen Z creators drive early adoption at low CAC.
  • Paid scales efficiently: Once retention is proven, paid social becomes profitable.
  • Referral runs forever: Friend invites create continuous, compounding growth.

11. Go-to-Market Strategy

Phase 1: Soft Launch (Week 1-4)

  • Beta test with 200-500 existing Grüns customers
  • Focus on squad building and referral loops
  • Gather feedback on twin concept and social features

Phase 2: Gen Z Influencer Launch (Week 5-8)

  • Partner with 5-10 Gen Z fitness/wellness micro-influencers
  • Gifted Grüns + early app access in exchange for organic content
  • Focus on dual consumption (vitamins + app)

Phase 3: Full Public Launch (Week 9+)

  • Paid social (TikTok, Instagram Reels) targeting Gen Z
  • Content: User-generated twin reveals, squad milestones, transformation stories
  • Press & earned media around identity-based habit design

Ongoing: Retention & Engagement

  • Squad challenges (monthly themes: '30-day transformation', 'Summer strength', etc.)
  • Private squad challenges and seasonal rewards
  • Content collaborations with users' friends (squad-based UGC)

12. Key Risks & Mitigation

Risk 1: Twin Concept Resonance

Problem: Some users may find the twin concept gimmicky or off-brand.

Mitigation: Extensive beta testing with Gen Z. Iterate on tone and visual language. Option to customize or hide twin if preferred.

Risk 2: Social Pressure Backlash

Problem: Users may feel anxiety over public streak breaks or squad pressure.

Mitigation: Design friendly nudges, not shame-based messaging. Highlight that streaks can restart. Privacy controls for visibility.

Risk 3: Churn After Initial Spike

Problem: Novelty wears off; habit breaks; user disengages.

Mitigation: Continuous reward cycles, seasonal challenges, evolving twin mechanics, squad re-engagement loops.

13. Appendix: Supporting Materials

Sample Twin Descriptions (from onboarding)

  • "Fit, strong, kind, centered, fearless person ready to run a triathlon on 24 hours notice"
  • "Morning person who crushes her goals and takes care of herself"
  • "Glowing, radiant, energized person who feels amazing in my body"
  • "Leader who inspires others through consistency and discipline"

Trait Options for Twin Selection

Fit, Strong, Kind, Disciplined, Focused, Fearless, Resilient, Radiant, Energized, Confident, Centered, Grounded, Inspired, Leader, Visionary, Calm, Playful, Adventurous

Sample Social Copy (Auto-Generated)

  • "Just created my digital twin on Grüns. This is who I'm becoming, one vitamin at a time. Join me? [link]"
  • "7-day streak! My twin is evolving and so am I. Consistency pays off. Wanna build habits together? [link]"
  • "Me vs. my digital twin: who's winning? Find out on Grüns [link]"

Key decisions and trade-offs

  • Identity twin over a plain streak tracker. Rejected alternative: a numerical streak alone. Cost accepted: greater generation complexity and a risk that the twin feels gimmicky.
  • Squads over public leaderboards. Rejected alternative: rank all users publicly. Cost accepted: weaker broad competition in exchange for small-group accountability and less comparison pressure.
  • Free sample referral over a discount code. Rejected alternative: ask a new friend to buy immediately with a coupon. Cost accepted: sample and fulfillment expense before proving repeat purchase.

Non-goals

  • No medical or dosage advice.
  • No twin for non-customers before joining the product trial.
  • No paid acquisition before week 9 of the proposed rollout.

Result

A competition brief, a deck and a working prototype. Campaign metrics are proposed targets.

CBS Collective

Version history: this is V1. Job Spark is V2, a motivation layer designed on top of it.

Built for my Columbia Business School vibe coding class: one board for every non-traditional MBA job, posted by clubs, students and Career Management, with a personal pipeline to track each role.

Live ↗Deck ↗

CBS Collective (CBS Job Aggregator)

Built for my Columbia Business School vibe coding class: one board for every non-traditional MBA job, posted by clubs, students and Career Management, with a personal pipeline to track each role.

The 30-second version. Non-traditional recruiting roles at Columbia Business School live in club WhatsApp groups, Slack, LinkedIn DMs, newsletters, club Airtables and word of mouth. Students told us they spend 3 to 5 hours a week stitching them together. CBS Collective centralizes those roles in a community job board that club AVPs, students and the Career Management Center (CMC) all post to, and lets every student save any role into a personal tracker in one click. Built as a team final project in Columbia's Prompt to Product course and shipped live.

Timeline: Spring 2026, Prompt to Product final project (team). MVP requirements written April 8, 2026.

What I owned: Student interviews, the PRD and acceptance criteria, and the board, tracker and upload feature definition. This shipped as a team course project.

Interview sample: Student and club AVP conversations informed the product.

Live: https://cbscollective.lovable.app

1. Problem statement

The jobs exist; finding them is a full-time job. Three recurring themes came up in student conversations:

  • The intel exists, the system does not. Students already spend 3 to 5 hours a week assembling roles from WhatsApp, LinkedIn, newsletters and word of mouth.
  • You miss a job if you are not watching. Several students learned about roles only after they were filled. Speed and centralization are the same problem.
  • A warm intro changes everything. Students described how referrals meaningfully improve interview rates. The bottleneck is not the posting, it is knowing who in your network is already at the company.

Club AVPs have the opposite problem: each club keeps its own Airtable or channel, with no standard way to share roles.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Centralize club-sourced and student-submitted roles in one searchable, filterable board.
  • Give every student a personal tracker that fills itself from the board.
  • Give club AVPs a standard, low-effort way to post, in bulk or one at a time.

Non-goals for the MVP

  • Airtable auto-sync (replaced by CSV upload).
  • Authentication and private visibility.
  • Messaging, resume parsing, salary data and company ratings.

3. The product

Two layers, one flywheel: more contributors make discovery better, and every save and stage update passively informs the CMC.

LayerWhoWhat it does
Community job boardClub AVPs, networked students, any MBA, CMC staffPost roles with source and "posted by" shown on every role; search and filter by category and industry
Personal trackerEvery studentOne-click save from the board, fields auto-populate; MBA-specific pipeline stages with timestamps; contact log per company; outcome capture
Club uploadClub AVPsCSV bulk upload with column mapping and a 5-row preview, or a manual form

4. User stories

  • As a club AVP, I want to bulk upload our club's roles from a CSV, so that I stop maintaining a separate list nobody outside the club sees.
  • As a student, I want to search every posted role by category and industry, so that I find roles I would never have searched for.
  • As a student, I want to save a role in one click and track its stage, so that my pipeline lives in one place.
  • As a student, I want a contact log per company, so that I can see my warm path.
  • As the CMC, I want anonymized outcome data from accepted offers, so that I understand non-traditional recruiting without extra surveys.

5. Requirements and acceptance criteria

  • CSV upload. Given a club AVP uploads a CSV with Job Title and Company columns, when they click Upload, then all valid rows are saved and appear in search.
  • Flexible mapping. Given a CSV with headers such as "Position" or "Firm", then the system maps them to Job Title and Company.
  • Manual post. Given a user enters Job Title and Company in the form, when they submit, then the role appears in search, with club name saved if they checked "I am a club member of".
  • Filtering. Given a user selects the Tech category and Technology industry, then only roles matching both are shown.
  • Tracker. Given a user opens the tracker, then they see every role they saved.
  • Default visibility. All roles are public in the MVP; private visibility waits for authentication.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

CSV upload instead of Airtable sync. Syncing a dozen club Airtables with different schemas was the riskiest piece. A CSV upload with flexible column mapping delivered 90% of the value in a fraction of the build. Trade-off: data goes stale until the next upload.

Everything public in the MVP. Club-gated visibility matters to some AVPs, but it requires authentication. Shipping public-only first proved the board and tracker loop. Trade-off: some club-exclusive roles will wait for V2.

Provenance on every role. Showing source and "posted by" builds trust in a community board and turns posts into warm-intro leads.

Warm introductions. Referrals were the strongest finding; a contact log per company was the smallest step toward it and a full referral graph was deferred because it needs authentication.

7. Success metrics

All figures below are targets, not observed results.

  • 150 weekly active students by week 4 of a term.
  • 8 clubs contributing roles.
  • 5 or more roles saved per active student.
  • Reported weekly discovery time reduced from the reported 3 to 5 hour baseline to under 1 hour.
  • Interview-stage progression is a follow-up outcome.

What happened

The team shipped and demoed CBS Collective in Prompt to Product.

8. What this project demonstrates

  • User research to product: student interviews turned directly into the problem framing and the two-layer design.
  • Scoping: a clear MVP line (CSV over sync, public over gated) with acceptance criteria.
  • Shipping: a live product built and demoed within the course.
  • Teamwork: built with a Prompt to Product team.

Guardians of the Amazon

Columbia cryptocurrency class project

ConceptColumbia cryptocurrency class
Problem

North star

Built for my Columbia cryptocurrency class: an interactive pitch deck for blockchain-backed tokens that sponsor real rainforest plots and fund conservation.

Live ↗

Guardians of the Amazon

An interactive pitch deck for blockchain-backed tokens that sponsor real rainforest plots and fund conservation.

The 30-second version. Conservation funding rarely shows donors what their money protects. Guardians of the Amazon proposes tokens that each sponsor a real rainforest plot, paired with NFT art and live satellite imagery, so supporters become Guardians who can watch their forest and vote on how funds are used. The concept is presented as an interactive, web-based pitch deck. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

Live: https://amazon-guardians-token.lovable.app

Context: Built as a class project for my Columbia cryptocurrency course.

Role: Deck narrative, token mechanism and PRD.

Problem

Deforestation continues while donors and impact investors lack transparent, ongoing proof that their money reaches the ground.

Who it is for

Climate-minded supporters and impact investors, and the conservation groups and local communities who steward the forest.

How it works

  1. Tokenized plots. Each token sponsors a plot from 0.1 to 81 hectares; proceeds fund conservation, reforestation and sustainable livelihoods.
  2. NFTs with real ground. Unique art is paired with live satellite imagery of the plot.
  3. Low-energy, on-chain governance. A proof-of-stake chain keeps emissions low, and a DAO lets holders direct funds.

Key decisions

  • Tokens over a donation subscription. A visible plot and portable governance rights over a simple recurring donation. Rejected alternative: donation subscription. Cost: custody, regulation and comprehension are harder.
  • DAO over a nonprofit board. Holder voice over centralized allocation. Rejected alternative: board-only governance. Cost: low turnout and unclear accountability need safeguards.
  • Satellite imagery partner over self-reported impact. Independent canopy evidence over partner narrative alone. Rejected alternative: self-reported updates. Cost: imagery has expense, resolution limits and cannot establish every conservation outcome.

Non-goals

  • Not a carbon credit product; tokens fund protection and are not sold as offsets.
  • No speculative trading features or price promises.
  • No impact claims without satellite or partner verification.

Success metrics

Targets, not observed results: concept deck completion 60% and 5 partner conversations; pilot 500 hectares sponsored in year one, 90% canopy retained against the starting baseline, and 30% holder vote participation.

What I would do next

Partner with a verified conservation group and a carbon registry, and pilot with a single protected area.

Unit economics (proposed model, not live)

Benchmark: about $4 per hectare of standing forest per year, the payment level Brazil's Tropical Forest Forever Facility targets. Source: World Resources Institute, Financing Nature Conservation: the Tropical Forest Forever Facility.

  • Proposed token: $10 protects one hectare for one year.
  • Proposed split: 70% to on-the-ground partners (with at least 20% of that reserved for Indigenous peoples and local communities, as the facility requires), 20% to satellite monitoring and verification, 10% to operations.

All figures are proposed, not live. This is a planning model, not a sale, a carbon credit or a promise of permanent protection; partner costs must validate it.

Risks

  • Double counting with carbon registries: plot sponsorship conveys no carbon-offset claim; reconcile parcel ids and rights with the partner and registry before issuance.
  • Crypto regulation: obtain jurisdiction-specific securities and consumer-protection review before any sale; the interactive concept deck does not offer an investment.

What did not work, and what I would change

Calling them tokens invited questions about speculation, when the real product is proof that a plot is protected. I would lead the pitch with the satellite verification and treat the token as the receipt.

What this project demonstrates

  • Built a unit-economics model from a public benchmark, labeled as proposed.
  • Named regulatory and double-counting risks with a mitigation for each.
  • Turned a class concept into an interactive deck.

Product requirements

Street Collectiv

Founder and CEO · Visible Hands VC Fellow · 2020 to 2021

Venture, 2020 to 2021Founder and CEO
Problem
COVID closed storefronts and local shops lost their customers overnight.
North star
Monthly recurring revenue flowing to local shops through subscriptions

A values-based marketplace that moved brick-and-mortar shops online during COVID-19 with a 25% average sales lift for the shops that joined. I ran UX research, built the Figma prototypes, and wrote the PRD.

Live ↗Deck ↗Wireframes ↗

Street Collectiv: Product Requirements Document

AuthorJuliana Cardona Mejia
RoleFounder and CEO, Visible Hands VC Fellow
Version1.0 (as built)
StatusRetrospective, written after the 2020 to 2021 run
Last updatedMay 12, 2026

What happened

During COVID, Street Collectiv moved brick-and-mortar shops online. Shops that joined saw a 25% average sales lift. The company ran from 2020 to 2021. The requirements below are the as-built retrospective and the product plan.

1. Overview

Street Collectiv is a community-driven marketplace that connects local small businesses with values-driven consumers. It combines three product surfaces that today exist in separate apps: a local shopping directory (think Yelp), a subscription platform (think Patreon), and a social network for ethical commerce (think Nextdoor meets Etsy).

The platform's wedge is the subscription model for local shops, which converts unpredictable foot traffic into recurring revenue while giving shoppers exclusive access, deals, and events at the places they already love. Around that wedge, Street Collectiv layers value-based discovery (BIPOC-owned, low-carbon, neighborhood-local), direct shop-to-shopper messaging, and a cross-promotion engine that lets non-competing neighborhood shops co-market.

The strategic bet: in a market dominated by transactional, extractive platforms (Amazon, Instacart, DoorDash), there is room for a relationship-based commerce layer where shop financial health and consumer values are explicitly aligned.

2. Problem statement

For small businesses

Local independent shops operate on thin margins with highly seasonal, unpredictable revenue. They lack the digital infrastructure to compete with big-box retailers on discoverability, loyalty programs, email marketing, and delivery. Existing platforms (Yelp, Google, DoorDash) extract value through ads or commissions without giving shops a meaningful tool to lock in recurring customers.

For conscious consumers

Shoppers who want to align purchases with their values (local, sustainable, equitable) face high friction. Information is scattered, claims are unverifiable, and convenience platforms like Amazon optimize for price and speed at the expense of ethics. There is no single place to discover, filter, and reliably buy from values-aligned local businesses.

For neighborhoods

Local commerce ecosystems are increasingly hollowed out. The social fabric that historically connected shops, owners, and residents has eroded, and there is no digital product that explicitly reinforces it.

3. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  1. Give local shops a recurring revenue stream through consumer subscriptions.
  2. Give consumers a trustworthy, low-friction way to discover and buy from values-aligned local businesses.
  3. Build social infrastructure that makes local commerce feel like a community rather than a transaction.
  4. Enable small shops to cross-promote and act collectively at a scale that approaches what large retailers do solo.

Non-goals (v1)

  • Becoming a fulfillment or logistics provider. Delivery is integrated via third parties, not operated in-house.
  • Competing with Amazon on price or selection for commodity goods.
  • National rollout. v1 focuses on a single pilot city or neighborhood cluster.
  • Replacing point-of-sale systems for shops.

4. Target users

Primary persona 1: The Conscious Consumer ("Maya")

  • 28 to 45, urban, college-educated, household income $75K+.
  • Already shops local when convenient but defaults to Amazon under time pressure.
  • Cares about climate impact, BIPOC and women-owned businesses, and supporting her neighborhood.
  • Pain point: aligning values with shopping requires research she does not have time for.

Primary persona 2: The Small Shop Owner ("Marcus")

  • Owner-operator of a 1 to 10 person retail, food, or service business.
  • Revenue between $100K and $2M annually, with significant seasonal swings.
  • Spends evenings doing marketing, social, inventory, and admin after a full operational day.
  • Pain point: cannot predict next month's revenue and has no infrastructure to build loyalty programs or email marketing the way chains do.

Secondary persona: The Community Organizer ("Renee")

  • Neighborhood association leader, BID member, or local activist.
  • Wants tools to organize local events, drive foot traffic to small businesses, and reinforce neighborhood identity.

5. User stories

Consumer side

  • As a shopper, I want to discover shops in my neighborhood filtered by ownership, sustainability, and category, so I can quickly find places that match my values.
  • As a shopper, I want to subscribe to a favorite shop for a monthly fee and receive perks (a free coffee weekly, 15% off, early access to drops), so I get ongoing value and the shop gets stable revenue.
  • As a shopper, I want to message a shop owner directly with questions about a product, so I get the kind of personal service I would get in person.
  • As a shopper, I want to attend in-person events with other members, so I can meet like-minded people and deepen my connection to the neighborhood.
  • As a shopper, I want carbon impact and equity data on every purchase, so I can track and reduce my footprint.

Shop side

  • As a shop owner, I want to create subscription tiers with custom perks, so I can convert one-time customers into recurring revenue.
  • As a shop owner, I want to see which subscribers are active, lapsing, or churned, so I can intervene.
  • As a shop owner, I want to chat with customers and post updates, so I can build a following without managing five social media platforms.
  • As a shop owner, I want to partner with a neighboring non-competing shop on a joint promotion, so we can both reach new customers.
  • As a shop owner, I want to see how my shop ranks on values-based filters (carbon, equity, local), so I can improve and market that.

6. Features and requirements

6.1 Subscription engine (P0)

The flagship feature. Enables shops to publish subscription tiers and consumers to subscribe.

Functional requirements

  • Shops can create up to 5 subscription tiers with custom price, billing cycle (weekly, monthly, quarterly), perk description, and inventory caps.
  • Consumers can browse, subscribe, pause, or cancel from a single dashboard.
  • Stripe (or equivalent) integration for recurring billing and payouts.
  • Shops can view subscriber list, MRR, churn, and lifetime value in a basic dashboard.
  • Perks can be physical (free item monthly), digital (early access codes), or experiential (member-only events).

6.2 Values-based discovery (P0)

Lets consumers filter and browse by ethical and logistical criteria.

Filters required at launch

  • Location: neighborhood, walk time, or radius.
  • Ownership: BIPOC-owned, women-owned, LGBTQ-owned, immigrant-owned (self-attested at signup, with verification path).
  • Carbon score ("Carbon Calories"): per-shop and per-product where data exists.
  • Wage equity: shops that opt in and disclose wage ratio data.
  • Category: food, retail, services, etc.
  • Delivery available: yes or no, plus delivery window.

Open question: how is ownership verification handled? Self-attestation vs. third-party certification (B Corp, NMSDC, etc.).

6.3 Direct messaging and shop profiles (P1)

Each shop has a profile that functions like a hybrid Instagram and storefront.

Functional requirements

  • Shop profile: photos, story, hours, subscription tiers, products, follower count, carbon and equity badges.
  • Consumers can follow shops without subscribing.
  • 1:1 messaging between consumer and shop, with response time SLAs visible.
  • Shops can post updates (text, photos, short video) to followers.

6.4 Member events (P1)

Subscription tiers can unlock access to in-person events.

Functional requirements

  • Shops or groups of shops can create events with location, capacity, RSVP rules.
  • Subscribers see member-only events in a unified calendar.
  • Consumers can RSVP, see attendee count, and (optionally) see who else is going.

6.5 Cross-promotion engine (P2)

Lets non-competing neighborhood shops co-market.

Functional requirements

  • Shops can opt in to a cross-promotion pool with category and neighborhood tags.
  • System suggests potential partners (e.g., coffee shop + bookstore in the same zip code).
  • Shops can launch joint email campaigns through Street Collectiv's email tool to each other's subscribers (with consumer opt-in respected).
  • Revenue or referral attribution is tracked, so shops can see whether the partnership worked.

6.6 Integrated delivery (P2)

Consumers can choose pickup or delivery at checkout.

Functional requirements

  • Integration with at least one third-party local courier service (e.g., Roadie, local couriers, or platform-specific options).
  • Shops can set delivery zones and fees.
  • Delivery is optional. Shops can opt out entirely if they want pickup-only.

6.7 Carbon and equity scoring (P2)

The data layer that powers values-based filters.

Functional requirements

  • Shops complete a structured onboarding survey covering sourcing, energy, packaging, wages, and ownership.
  • Score is computed by a transparent rubric (published publicly) and displayed as a badge.
  • Shops can improve scores over time and see what levers move them.
  • Consumers can see a per-purchase "Carbon Calorie" estimate on receipts.

Open question: how rigorous does scoring need to be at launch to avoid greenwashing accusations? Likely needs a transparent methodology page and an external advisor before scaling.

7. Non-functional requirements

CategoryRequirement
PerformanceSearch and filter results return in under 2 seconds for the 95th percentile.
MobileMobile-first responsive web at launch. Native iOS and Android in v2.
AccessibilityWCAG 2.1 AA compliance.
PrivacyConsumer data is not sold. Shops only see aggregated analytics, not individual purchase history outside their own shop.
Trust and safetyVerification flow for shop owners. Reporting and moderation for messaging.
InternationalizationEnglish-only at launch. Spanish in v2 (priority given pilot city demographics).

8. Success metrics

North star

Monthly recurring revenue (MRR) flowing to local shops through Street Collectiv subscriptions.

This metric captures the core thesis: are we actually stabilizing small business cash flow?

Supporting metrics

Marketplace health

  • Active subscriptions per consumer (target: 2+ by month 6 of consumer lifecycle).
  • Subscriber churn rate (target: under 8% monthly by month 12).
  • GMV (gross merchandise volume) through the platform.

Shop health

  • Average MRR per shop.
  • Shop retention at 6 and 12 months (target: 80% at 6 months).
  • Net promoter score from shop owners.

Consumer engagement

  • WAU / MAU (target: 0.5+).
  • Average filters used per session (signal for whether values-based discovery is working).
  • Messages sent between consumers and shops.
  • Event attendance among subscribers.

Community and network effects

  • Cross-promotion campaigns launched per month (post-v2).
  • Referrals: consumers acquired through other consumers' shares.

9. Risks, assumptions, and dependencies

Key assumptions to validate

  1. Consumers will pay subscriptions to local shops. Validate with a pilot cohort of 10 shops and 200 consumers before scaling features.
  2. Shops will invest the time to onboard and maintain a profile. This is non-trivial; many small shop owners do not have time for another platform.
  3. Values-based filters drive purchase decisions. Some evidence in adjacent markets (e.g., B Corp, Aspiration), but unproven at the local commerce level.
  4. A pilot city has enough density of both shops and conscious consumers to create marketplace liquidity.

Risks

RiskMitigation
Chicken-and-egg liquidity: consumers won't come without shops, shops won't come without consumers.Seed pilot with 30 to 50 shops in one neighborhood through direct sales, before launching to consumers.
Subscription fatigue: consumers already pay for Netflix, Spotify, etc. and may resist more.Frame as "supporting local" rather than another sub. Bundle multiple shops into a single discounted membership.
Greenwashing accusations: values-based filters could be challenged if scoring is opaque.Publish methodology publicly, partner with credible third-party certifier, hire an advisor.
Shop ops burden: small shop owners drop off because admin is too heavy.Default to extreme simplicity in shop onboarding. Offer "concierge" setup for the first 100 shops.
Disintermediation: once a shop has a consumer's email, they may take the relationship off-platform.Make subscriptions and analytics genuinely better than DIY. Long-term: this is partly OK; we want to grow the local economy, not lock anyone in.

Dependencies

  • Payments infrastructure: Stripe or similar.
  • Delivery infrastructure: at least one third-party courier integration.
  • Mapping and location services: Google Maps or Mapbox.
  • Email infrastructure: SendGrid or equivalent.
  • Initial pilot partnerships: 1 to 2 neighborhoods with high density of indie shops and a values-aligned consumer base (candidates: Brooklyn, Oakland, Philadelphia, Asheville).

10. Out of scope for v1

  • Native mobile apps (iOS, Android).
  • B2B marketplace (shop-to-shop wholesale).
  • International expansion.
  • Branded payment card or financial product for shops.
  • AI-driven personalized recommendations.
  • Influencer or affiliate program.

11. Open questions

  1. Pricing model: does Street Collectiv take a percentage of subscription GMV, charge shops a SaaS fee, charge consumers a membership fee, or some mix? Likely a small percentage of subscription revenue plus optional consumer membership.
  2. Geographic launch strategy: one neighborhood within one city, or one city end-to-end?
  3. Verification standard: self-attestation vs. third-party certification for ownership and sustainability claims?
  4. Shop onboarding model: self-serve, sales-led, or community-led (recruit via local BIDs and chambers of commerce)?
  5. Defensibility, answered: The moat is not the subscription feature. It is a dense, trusted neighborhood network: shop relationships, repeat consumer memberships, permissioned cross-promotions and useful shop revenue history. Shopify or Square can copy subscriptions, but not an already-active local community without acquiring both sides. This remains a strategic hypothesis, not a proven competitive barrier; retained shop revenue and repeat participation are the evidence that would establish it.

12. Appendix: design principles

These should guide every product decision.

  1. Relationship over transaction. Every feature should reinforce the relationship between shopper and shop. If it makes the experience feel more like Amazon, reconsider it.
  2. Shops first. This is a B2B2C product where the shop is the hardest side of the marketplace to acquire. When in doubt, optimize for shop experience.
  3. Trust through transparency. Values-based claims must be backed by published methodology. No black boxes.
  4. Convenience without compromise. Conscious shoppers will not trade convenience for ethics indefinitely. The product must be as easy as Amazon for the ethical choice to win.
  5. Community is a feature, not a vibe. Member events, direct messaging, and cross-promotion are concrete features, not marketing language.

Key decisions and trade-offs

  • Subscription wedge over a commission marketplace. Predictable shop revenue over maximizing transactions. Rejected alternative: charge a commission on every order. Cost: consumers must see enough ongoing value to subscribe.
  • One neighborhood over a city. Dense local supply over a broad, sparse catalogue. Rejected alternative: citywide launch. Cost: slower geographic growth.
  • Self-attested values tags at pilot. Fast onboarding over third-party certification before the first listing. Rejected alternative: require certification up front. Cost: weaker assurance; tags must say self-attested and a verification path is needed before scale.

What this project demonstrates

  • Founder execution: translating a COVID-era shop problem into a commerce product.
  • User research: turning shop needs into prototypes and requirements.
  • Trade-off reasoning: subscription, density and verification choices with explicit costs.
  • Marketplace judgment: treating shop revenue and trust as the core outcome.
  • Retrospective discipline: reporting the result plainly and separating what was built from what was planned.

Product requirements

Talent Hive (V1 and V2)

Founder and CEO · 2017 to 2018

Venture, 2017 to 2018Founder and CEO
Problem
Founders had skills to trade but no cash to pay for help.
North star
70% of signups finish onboarding and claim their first Karma Coins (target)

Digitized the accelerator's skill exchange into a time-bank web app with our CTO. 128 beta tests over 2.5 months across DC, MD and VA, turned into a V2 roadmap.

Live V1 ↗Live V2 ↗Deck ↗

Talent Hive (V1 and V2)

A peer skills marketplace where members trade help priced in reputation, not cash, so expertise circulates inside a community of people who have earned their place.

The 30-second version. Talent Hive lets members post projects and trade skills with each other using Karma Coin, an in-app reputation currency, instead of money. A member posts a project with a Karma bid, contributors apply, counter, or pass, work gets done, and both sides review each other before any Karma is released. The headline asset is reputation: a dual-blind five-axis review system, a reliability score, and a ranking formula deliberately built so that a new high-quality contributor is not buried under sheer volume. The PM work here is the trust machinery of a marketplace, the escrow logic, the anti-collusion rules, and the edge cases for when someone ghosts.

Timeline: 2017 to 2018, built with our CTO; 128 beta tests over 2.5 months across DC, MD and VA, followed by a V2 roadmap and later portfolio prototypes.

Beta evidence

We digitized the accelerator skill exchange with our CTO and ran 128 beta tests over 2.5 months across DC, MD and VA. The tests informed a V2 roadmap. Discovery, matching and reputation legibility are the V2 focus described in this document.

1. Problem statement

Skilled people help each other all the time (a designer reviews a friend's deck, a developer unblocks someone's bug), but that exchange has no structure and no memory. Favors are informal, hard to ask for without feeling like an imposition, and they leave no record of who is actually reliable. The moment money enters, the dynamic changes: cash marketplaces like Upwork and Fiverr turn collaboration into a transactional gig economy where price competition, client-freelancer power imbalance, and recruiter spam crowd out the goodwill that makes peer help feel good in the first place.

The result is a gap. There is no trusted place to trade help among peers where the currency is contribution and the asset that accrues is reputation. Existing platforms optimize for the wrong thing: Upwork and Fiverr optimize for the lowest bidder, LinkedIn optimizes for connections and recruiter reach, and informal favor networks do not scale or remember. Talent Hive replaces cash with Karma and makes a member's track record (completed projects, peer-validated skills, a five-axis review history) the thing they build and carry. Reputation, not price, decides who gets the work.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Let members trade real help without money, so collaboration feels like community, not a gig.
  • Make reputation the headline asset, so reliable, high-quality contributors are visibly rewarded over time.
  • Protect both sides with escrow and dual-blind reviews, so neither party can be left burned.
  • Keep the exchange spam-free and human, so the place feels closer to a creative community than a job board.

Non-goals

  • No cash or Stripe at v1. Premium money features are Phase 2, because the whole thesis is reputation-as-currency.
  • No public Karma leaderboards. Out of scope, because ranking people publicly turns collaboration competitive and gameable.
  • No AI matching at v1. Phase 3, because trust and the manual application flow come first.
  • No DMs outside a project context. Messaging is project-scoped to prevent recruiter spam and cold outreach.
  • No badges, streaks, XP, or levels. Reputation is earned through real reviewed work, not gamified vanity.

3. The product

A member posts a project with a Karma bid; the bid is escrowed immediately. Contributors apply at the posted bid, counter-offer with a justification, or pass. The owner accepts one contributor, work happens, the owner marks complete, and both parties review each other before Karma releases.

StepWhat happensKarma logic
1. PostOwner posts a project with a bidBid escrowed from owner immediately
2. ApplyContributor applies, counters, or passesOne counter per applicant
3. AcceptOwner accepts one applicantStatus moves to in-progress; counter difference refunded if relevant
4. CompleteOwner marks completeTriggers dual review
5. ReviewBoth parties rate on five axes, blindNeither sees the other's rating until both submit
6. ReleaseBoth reviews inKarma releases to contributor; reputation recomputes

Reputation has two formulas. Reliability = completion rate x 0.4 + on-time rate x 0.3 + review average x 0.3. Ranking (used to order applicants) = review average x log(completed count + 1), deliberately logarithmic so a new contributor with a few excellent reviews is not crushed by someone with high volume and mediocre work.

4. User stories

Project owner

  • As an owner, I want to post a project and see applicants ranked by reputation, so that I can pick the person whose track record fits the job.
  • As an owner, I want my Karma escrowed and released only after we both review, so that I never pay for work that was not delivered.
  • As an owner whose contributor ghosts past the timeline, I want a one-tap dispute to reclaim my Karma, so that a no-show does not cost me.

Contributor

  • As a contributor, I want to apply with the posted bid or counter with a justification, so that I can take fair work on fair terms.
  • As a high-quality newcomer, I want the ranking to weight review quality, not just volume, so that I am not buried by people who have simply done more.
  • As a contributor whose owner ghosts after accepting, I want Karma to auto-release to me after a wait, so that I am paid for work I did.

Edge and trust states

  • As a member with too little Karma to post, I want an inline error and a link to browse projects to earn, so that I am guided, not blocked.
  • As the system, I want two all-5-star reviews submitted within five minutes of each other flagged for moderation, so that collusion is caught (no auto-action at MVP, just a flag).
  • As either party, I want a project unreviewed for 14 days to auto-resolve with neutral 3-star reviews, so that Karma never gets stuck in limbo.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Auth and 3-step onboarding granting 100 starter Karma. Given a new user, when they finish onboarding, then they receive 100 Karma and land on Discover.
  • Project posting with immediate escrow. Given an owner with enough Karma, when they post, then the bid is escrowed at once.
  • Application flow (apply / counter / pass) with one counter per applicant.
  • Owner application list ranked by reputation, accept exactly one. Given multiple applicants, when the owner views them, then they are ordered by the reputation rank formula.
  • Dual-blind review gating Karma release. Given a completed project, when both parties submit reviews, then and only then does Karma release and reputation recompute; neither rating is visible until both submit.
  • Project-scoped messaging only.
  • Correct cancellation refunds. Given a cancellation, when it is pre-acceptance, then 100% refunds; when post-acceptance, then 95% refunds with a 5% platform sink.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Reputation profile: Karma earned, review average, completed count, reliability bar, peer-validated vs self-attested skills, portfolio grid, last 5 reviews with per-axis bars.
  • Karma dashboard: balance, escrow, lifetime earned/spent, transaction history, 30-day sparkline.
  • Ghost-handling auto-releases (owner ghosts 7 days, contributor ghosts 14 days, unreviewed 14 days).

Future (P2)

  • Cash and premium features (Phase 2).
  • Multi-contributor team projects (Phase 2).
  • AI matching (Phase 3).

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Decision: Reputation currency (Karma), no cash at v1.

Alternatives considered: real money like Upwork/Fiverr (rejected, that recreates the gig-economy dynamics the product exists to escape: lowest-bidder competition, power imbalance, spam). Why this won: making the currency reputation aligns incentives toward quality and goodwill. The cost, openly, is that Karma has no external value yet, so the early loop depends on intrinsic motivation; cash is a deliberate Phase 2 layer once the trust loop is proven.

Decision: Dual-blind reviews that gate Karma release.

Alternatives considered: one-directional reviews or reviews visible immediately (rejected, both invite retaliation and inflation, "I'll give you 5 if you give me 5").
