Why this won: blind reviews where neither party sees the other's rating until both submit produce honest scores, and gating release on both submissions forces closure. It is the single most important trust mechanism in the product.

Decision: Logarithmic ranking formula (review average x log(completed + 1)).

Alternatives considered: rank by raw completed count (rejected, entrenches incumbents and buries good newcomers) or by review average alone (rejected, a single 5-star review would outrank a proven contributor). Why this won: the log term rewards experience with diminishing returns, so quality newcomers compete while volume still counts. This is the most considered piece of PM math in the build.

Decision: Project-scoped messaging only, no open DMs.

Alternatives considered: open messaging (rejected, instantly becomes a recruiter-spam and cold-pitch channel, the exact thing that makes LinkedIn exhausting). Why this won: tying every conversation to a project keeps the community focused and spam-free. The trade-off is less serendipitous connection, accepted on purpose.

Decision: Flag collusion, do not auto-punish, at MVP.

This is the honest, imperfect call. Two all-5-star reviews within five minutes get flagged for moderation but trigger no automatic penalty. Auto-action was rejected because false positives (two people who genuinely had a great, fast project) would punish exactly the behavior the product wants. The trade-off is that some collusion slips through at MVP; the bet is that a human-in-the-loop flag is safer than an aggressive rule until there is enough volume to tune it.

The four-star floor. A punitive minimum-rating floor is not a rule of this specification. The live landing copy requires correction to match the flag-not-punish rule; soft moderation flags remain the stated product decision, not automatic expulsion.

7. UX

The mood is warm, collaborative, and optimistic, closer to Are.na or Read.cv than Upwork, and deliberately not Fiverr, LinkedIn, gig apps, or crypto exchanges. A serif display face (Fraunces or Lora) carries titles and names so people feel like people, Inter handles the UI, and Karma values render in gold with tabular numerals. Motion is subtle (200 to 300ms fades, a small "+/-" float when Karma transfers, no spring physics) so the interface feels calm rather than playful-to-a-fault. The Discover feed reads like a creative-community digest: recommended projects, trending skills, and "community wins" (recently completed work with the Karma earned and a review snippet). The reputation profile is the most important screen and treats a member's track record as the hero: three stat blocks, a reliability bar, peer-validated vs self-attested skill markers, a portfolio grid, and recent reviews with per-axis mini-bars. Empty states encourage and never blame the user ("No reviews yet, every project is a first").

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Onboarding completion. Target 70% of signups finishing the 3-step onboarding and claiming 100 Karma.
  • First project posted. Target 40% of new members posting or applying within their first session.
  • Application-to-acceptance rate. Target a healthy 50% of projects receiving and accepting an application, the signal that the loop closes.

Lagging indicators

  • Loop completion rate. Target 80% of accepted projects reaching dual review and Karma release (the true North Star, since value only exists when the loop closes).
  • Repeat collaboration. Target 30% of members completing a second project within 30 days.
  • Reputation integrity. Target: collusion flags under 2% of reviews, monitored with ghost auto-resolutions; target, not observed.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. This is a beta-tested prototype; the figures below are targets for the V2 build, not observed results. The honest hard problem is cold-start liquidity: a reputation marketplace is worth nothing until enough members and projects exist to make the feed feel alive, and 100 starter Karma plus a seeded set of demo projects is a thin answer to that. The collusion rule is intentionally soft (flag, no auto-action), which means V1 tolerates some gaming in exchange for not punishing genuine fast collaborations, a trade-off I would revisit with real volume. I would also pressure-test whether reputation alone is motivating enough to sustain participation without any cash on-ramp; that assumption is the load-bearing bet of the entire model, and it is exactly what V2 exists to refine.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • Is reputation-only motivating enough to sustain a marketplace, or is a cash on-ramp needed sooner than Phase 2? (user, data)
  • How do we solve cold-start liquidity beyond starter Karma and seeded projects? (growth, data)
  • When does the soft collusion flag need to become an automated rule, and at what volume? (data, trust and safety)
  • Are the reliability and ranking weights right once real outcomes exist? (data)

v2 scope: refine matching and reputation (the focus of Talent Hive V2), then layer cash premium features, team projects, and eventually AI matching.

Talent HiveUpwork / FiverrLinkedInInformal favors
CurrencyReputation (Karma)CashNoneNone
Optimizes forTrack recordLowest bidderConnectionsGoodwill
Spam controlProject-scoped messagingWeakWeakN/A
Memory of reliabilityYes (reviews, reliability)PartialNoNo

GTM hypothesis: seed tight communities (a cohort, a creative scene) where reputation already has social value, prove the loop, then widen. Why defensible: reputation is portable trust that compounds and cannot be bought, a moat that lowest-bidder cash marketplaces structurally cannot build.

10. What changed in V2

Talent Hive V2 is the second pass at a reputation-currency marketplace where members trade skills using Karma instead of cash. V1 proved the core loop (post, apply, dual-blind review, Karma release) but exposed where it strained: discovery was a busy feed, matching was manual, and reputation was rich but hard to read at a glance. V2 starts from a deliberate planning discipline ("tell me exactly what you plan to build before you build it") and rebuilds the experience as a cleaner wireframe, sharpening how the right contributor surfaces for a project and how reputation is presented so trust is legible in seconds. The PM work here is iteration: deciding what to keep, what to cut, and what to simplify after a first build taught its lessons.

Visible V2 changes: Compare the sidebar navigation, at-a-glance trust scores and contributor spotlight with V1's multi-section feed; those are the intended wireframe comparison points, not new beta outcome claims.

V2 keeps the V1 loop and refines two layers on top of it: how matches surface, and how reputation reads.

LayerV1 behaviorV2 refinement
DiscoveryMulti-section digest (For You, trending, wins, new members)Cleaner, intent-led surface that foregrounds the project or person you want
MatchingOwner manually scans reputation-ranked applicantsSharper surfacing so fit is suggested, not just sortable
Reputation displayDeep profile (five axes, reliability bar, skills, portfolio)Same data, presented so the trust signal lands at a glance
Core loopPost, escrow, apply, dual-blind review, releaseUnchanged, validated in V1

The underlying mechanics are inherited: a project bid is escrowed on posting, contributors apply or counter, both parties review blind, and Karma releases only when both reviews are in. Reputation still rests on the V1 formulas (reliability = completion rate x 0.4 + on-time rate x 0.3 + review average x 0.3; ranking = review average x log(completed count + 1)). What changes is the surface, not the engine.

V2 decisions and trade-offs

Decision: Plan explicitly before building ("tell me exactly what you plan to build before you build it").

Alternatives considered: iterate directly in code as in V1 (rejected for V2, because the lesson from V1 was that an unplanned surface accretes friction). Why this won: forcing a written plan before the build is how you cut scope deliberately instead of adding features reflexively. The entire V2 framing is an act of restraint, and the planning step is what makes that restraint possible.

Decision: Ship a wireframe, not a polished build.

Alternatives considered: a full high-fidelity V2 (rejected, polishing the wrong structure wastes the iteration). Why this won: the V1 lessons were structural (discovery, matching, reputation legibility), so V2 should prove the new structure first and polish later. The unflattering truth is that a wireframe looks less impressive than V1's finished surface, and shipping it anyway is the disciplined choice: validate the flow before investing in fidelity.

Decision: Keep the V1 trust machinery untouched.

Alternatives considered: re-examine escrow and review logic (rejected, those were the parts V1 validated). Why this won: iteration means changing what underperformed and protecting what worked. Re-litigating proven mechanics would risk regressing the product's core asset.

Decision: Refine matching surfacing now, defer learned matching to later.

Alternatives considered: jump straight to AI/ML matching (rejected, same reason as V1: not enough outcome data, and a black box undercuts the legibility V2 is chasing). Why this won: better surfacing and clearer signal solve most of the matching friction without a model, and they generate the very data a future model would need. Sequencing over flash.

11. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: named what cash marketplaces break (goodwill, peer trust) and built the currency around the thing that actually accrues, reputation.
  • Trade-off reasoning: Karma over cash, blind over open reviews, logarithmic over raw-volume ranking, project-scoped over open messaging, flag over auto-punish.
  • Systems thinking: complete escrow and refund logic, ghost-handling timeouts, anti-collusion flags, and two distinct reputation formulas with a clear rationale for each.
  • Validation discipline: loop completion framed as the North Star, every metric a labeled target, cold-start named as the central unvalidated risk.
  • Roadmap sense: a deliberate sequence (prove the reputation loop, then add cash, teams, and AI matching) rather than building everything at once.
  • Communication: a warm, anti-gig product voice and an interface language chosen to feel like a community, not a job board.

Product requirements

Street Investors Exchange

Co-Founder and COO · 2018 to 2019

Venture, 2018 to 2019Co-Founder and COO
Problem
Investors who believed in underrepresented founders had no path to invest.
North star
Qualified investors who are women or people of color, as a share of all investors

A fund-of-funds investment platform behind a 2018 U.S. provisional patent application. It tracked investor performance and gamified investor education so emerging investors could back local startups with micro funds. I led the beta, the PRDs, and the A/B tests.

Prototype ↗Case study deck ↗

Street Investors Exchange

An investor education and matchmaking platform that lets aspiring investors without wealth build a real track record, so early-stage capital is decided by a more diverse group of people.

The 30-second version. Street Investors Exchange (SIEX) was a Delaware public benefit corporation I co-founded in 2018 as a partner to the Street Entrepreneurs accelerator. The thesis: venture capital is homogeneous because you have to be rich to start investing, so diversifying who funds startups means giving unaccredited investors a way to earn a track record. The product combined gamified due diligence and investment education ("Coursera for Shark Tank"), a performance-tracked path to managing real capital through a donor-advised training fund, and a matchmaking layer between founders and investors. I conceived the platform, led the beta, wrote the PRDs, and ran the A/B tests behind a 2018 U.S. provisional patent application.

Timeline: Incorporated July 2018. Beta, PRDs and A/B tests in 2018. I stepped back in 2019.

Live link: 2026 SIEX simulation implements onboarding, fictional tickers, weekly advance, a 10-week index challenge and a $25,000 mock fund. It deliberately leaves out education modules, the diligence library, the donor-advised fund and matching. I chose the simulation loop to make investor judgment and performance visible in one demonstrable rebuild, without presenting mock capital as real investing.

Role: Co-Founder and COO, alongside co-founder and CEO Bethany Quinn.

1. Problem statement

Early-stage investing has a gatekeeping problem that starts before any pitch. Accredited investor rules mean you essentially have to be wealthy already to get into early-stage investment, and most accredited investors are white men. In 2016 there were about 54 women and roughly 15 African Americans in decision-making roles at US venture capital firms. Homophily and homogeneity in who invests limit funding for companies founded by women and people of color.

The problem showed up inside the Street Entrepreneurs accelerator. Alumni wanted to invest in current program participants, which sounds simple but runs straight into the accredited investor wall. Founders, meanwhile, struggle to find investors whose criteria they actually meet, while early-stage investors review around 10,000 companies a year, most of which never fit their thesis.

Existing options fell short. Matchmaking platforms such as Gust, FundingSage and EnrichHer connected founders and investors but did nothing to grow the investor pool. Investor education programs were small, in-person cohorts, and most only admitted accredited investors.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Grow the number of women and people of color working in early-stage investment: double the women and quadruple the African Americans in the first cohort.
  • Give unaccredited, aspiring investors a compliant way to build a real, portable investment track record.
  • Shorten fundraising for founders and screening for investors by matching on investment criteria.
  • Fund the Street Entrepreneurs accelerator over time through the platform's donor-advised funds.

Non-goals

  • Not a crowdfunding portal. Aspiring investors trained and invested through a managed training fund, not by pooling retail money directly into deals.
  • Not a broker-dealer. The donor-advised fund structure kept activity within FINRA rules.
  • Not a generic startup database. Matching existed to serve the education and track-record thesis.

3. The product

LayerWhat the user doesWhat SIEX does
LearnAspiring investor completes gamified lessons on early-stage investingAwards SIEX points for activity and quality
DiligenceWrites due diligence reports on real startupsMakes reports available to investors, outsourcing entry-level VC work
InvestQualifies with enough pointsUnlocks real investments through a donor-advised training fund managed by Impact Assets
Track recordBuilds a performance historyGenerates a profile to present to employers or limited partners
MatchFounder fills a simple form; investor sets alertsTells founders which investors' criteria they meet; alerts investors to fitting companies

The long-term model was a fund of funds run by the platform's best performers. The math from the plan: with 10,000 members and the top 0.5% selected, 50 managers per cohort could each run a $10M fund, a $500M fund of funds large enough for pension funds to invest in.

4. User stories

  • As an aspiring investor without wealth, I want to learn and practice due diligence on real companies, so that I can earn a track record no accreditation rule blocks.
  • As a high-performing aspiring investor, I want my results tracked in a profile, so that employers and limited partners can see what I can do.
  • As a founder, I want to see which investors' criteria I meet, so that I stop pitching people who will never invest.
  • As an investor, I want alerts for companies that fit my thesis and access to diligence reports, so that I screen faster.
  • As a Street Entrepreneurs alum, I want to invest in current program founders, so that the community funds itself.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Gamified education modules with a points system (SIEX points) tied to activity and diligence quality.
  • Due diligence report creation and an investor-facing library of reports.
  • Investor profiles with tracked performance.
  • Founder intake form and investor criteria alerts for matchmaking.
  • Qualification gate from points to the donor-advised training fund.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Employer and LP-facing profile export.
  • Investor Circle oversight and feedback workflow for aspiring investors.

Future (P2)

  • Graduate-run fund of funds sized for institutional investors.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Donor-advised training fund instead of direct retail investing. Letting unaccredited users invest directly would have run into securities regulation. Routing qualified users through a donor-advised fund managed by Impact Assets let them build a real record while staying compliant. Trade-off: a slower, more structured path to real capital.

Start as a consulting business before building the full platform. The plan began with investor research and pitch support for founders and diligence support for investors, with aspiring investors as independent contractors. This tested demand and digitized the knowledge base before heavy engineering. Trade-off: services revenue and manual work early on.

B2B2C acquisition. Investors through angel circles and professional associations, founders through coworking spaces and accelerators, aspiring investors through MBA programs. Trade-off: partnership-driven growth is slower than paid acquisition but builds trust in a credibility-sensitive market.

Gamification as the core engine. Points made learning measurable and created a ranking signal for who earns access to real capital. Risk: gaming the points. Mitigation: points tied to the quality of diligence, not just volume.

7. Success metrics

  • Total users by type, and daily and monthly active users.
  • Diligence reports created; investments recorded on the platform and made through the training fund, in dollars and count.
  • Return on investments; SIEX points as the activity metric.
  • Aspiring investors trained, profiles sent to employers, and job placements.
  • Diversity outcome: share of women and people of color among qualified investors.

Rebuild targets, not observed beta results: 70% onboarding completion and 50% of activated simulation users finishing the 10-week challenge.

8. What happened, and what I would do differently

I led the beta, wrote the PRDs and ran the A/B tests, and that work formed the basis of a 2018 U.S. provisional patent application. I stepped back from the company in 2019. With hindsight, I would narrow the first version to one loop, education plus diligence reports sold to investors, and prove investors would pay for that output before building matchmaking and the fund path in parallel. Three products in one platform stretched a two-founder team.

Beta and patent evidence: I led the beta, the PRDs and the A/B tests, which became the basis of a 2018 U.S. provisional patent application.

9. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem framing: turned a structural market failure (who gets to invest) into a concrete product thesis.
  • Regulatory judgment: designed around accreditation and FINRA rules instead of ignoring them.
  • Sequencing: consulting first, platform second, fund of funds last.
  • Founder execution: incorporation, co-founder partnership, beta, A/B tests and a provisional patent application.

Simulation build

  • What this project demonstrates: turning a dormant venture into a demonstrable product with a bounded 2026 simulation, while keeping fictional funds and omitted venture features explicit.

Product requirements

Quiet Gate

A friction layer you tap instead of Instagram: a counter, then a written prompt, then your to-do list

Live V1 ↗Live V2 ↗
In real useSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Instagram is built to keep you scrolling, and blockers just get switched off.
North star
Median daily Instagram opens down 30% by day 14 (target)

Quiet Gate

A friction layer for intentional Instagram use

The 30-second version. I built a product that reduces compulsive Instagram opens by inserting escalating friction between the user and the app. Built solo in a weekend, built for a 14-day self-use test, designed for commercial expansion via browser extension in v2. The PM work spans problem framing, competitive analysis, design decisions under technical constraints, a validation plan, and a defensible roadmap.

Timeline: Weekend prototype, 14-day self-use test described; quantitative results unreported, extension in design.

1. The opportunity

Smartphone users open Instagram a median of 8 to 14 times per day. Internal research from Meta (leaked in 2021) confirmed that the majority of these opens are not driven by specific intent. They are reflexive, triggered by boredom, anxiety, or habit. This produces measurable harm to attention, sleep, and self-reported wellbeing, and the harm scales with use.

The digital wellness category has responded with two dominant product archetypes, both of which fail in predictable ways:

  • Hard blockers (Opal, Brick, ScreenZen): make Instagram inaccessible for fixed windows. High initial uptake; high churn. Users uninstall within two weeks because the blocker fires during a legitimate use case (responding to a real message, posting an event), produces resentment, and gets removed.
  • Soft nudges (Instagram's own time reminders, weekly screen time summaries): easily dismissed. No behavior change observed in any published study.

The product gap: a tool that scales friction with usage, while keeping the app fully accessible. The bet is that most compulsive opens will not survive even mild friction, while intentional opens will pass through. No tool in market currently implements this approach.

2. The product

Quiet Gate is a web app the user installs on their phone home screen as a Progressive Web App, replacing the Instagram icon. Tapping it routes the user through a friction layer before opening instagram.com.

The friction is a five-stage state machine where each stage is more demanding than the last:

Open #FrictionWhy this level
1 to 3None. Counter visible.Don't punish low-frequency use.
4Counter turns red.Visual warning, no barrier. Cheap signal.
5Required written prompt: "Why are you opening Instagram?"First behavioral cost. Forces explicit intent.
6Required to-do list (3+ items)Surfaces the task being avoided.
7+Today's to-do list shown as alternativesRepeated invitation to redirect, with escape hatch.

The counter resets at midnight in the user's anchor timezone. The to-do list is built once per day and persists through all subsequent opens. Every interaction is logged for personal review.

3. Key product decisions and trade-offs

This section is where PM thinking lives. For each decision below, I'll state the choice, the alternatives I considered, and why I chose what I chose.

Decision 1: Escalating friction, not flat barriers

Choice: Friction scales with opens (none, then visual, then written, then constructive).

Alternatives considered:

  • Flat prompt at every open. Rejected because users adapt within days, typing "k" to bypass. Loses signal value.
  • Fixed daily limit, then hard block. Rejected because the post-limit open is sometimes the most important one. Forces lockouts that produce uninstalls.
  • Time-based gating. Rejected because compulsive and legitimate opens occur in the same hours. Cannot distinguish.

Why this won: Escalation preserves the signal value of friction. The 5th-open prompt feels meaningful because the first four didn't require one. This is the central design hypothesis of the product, and the success metric.

Decision 2: To-do list as the redirect, instead of meditation or exercise

Choice: At open 6, the user builds a to-do list. At opens 7+, they're invited to pick from it.

Alternatives considered:

  • Generic redirects (meditate, exercise, journal). Rejected because they treat the user as someone with abstract wellness goals rather than concrete tasks. Most "I'm bored, opening Instagram" moments are avoidance of a specific thing, not absence of wellness practice.
  • AI-generated suggestions. Rejected because the moment of compulsive opening is the wrong moment to introduce a third party (an LLM) into the loop. The user knows what they're avoiding better than any model does.

Why this won: A self-built to-do list at the moment of urge surfaces what the user is actually avoiding. The list is not enforced (no completion tracking). Visibility is the intervention.

Decision 3: An always-available escape hatch

Choice: "Open Instagram anyway" is reachable from every friction state, in 14px gray underlined text below the primary friction action.

Alternatives considered:

  • No escape hatch. Rejected because it produces uninstalls when users have legitimate emergencies.
  • Time-delayed escape (wait 60 seconds to bypass). Rejected because it adds friction in a direction that conflicts with the user's goal. The escape should be available; the bypass should be logged.

Why this won: The asymmetric visual design (friction is prominent, bypass is small) creates default behavior toward the constructive path while preserving user agency. Bypass rate becomes data: if a user bypasses 80% of the time, the friction is calibrated wrong and the product should adjust.

Decision 4: Anchored timezone for daily reset

Choice: The "daily" reset is locked to the user's anchor timezone (America/New_York for me), not the device's current timezone.

Alternatives considered:

  • Device timezone. Rejected because traveling east effectively grants extra opens, creating an exploitable boundary.
  • UTC reset. Rejected because midnight UTC is the middle of the user's day in most timezones; the reset would feel arbitrary.

Why this won: The anchor timezone keeps the day stable across travel and prevents micro-gaming. This is a small detail with disproportionate behavioral impact.

Decision 5: Single-tenant v1, multi-tenant in v2

Choice: v1 has no auth, no signup, no accounts. Single user, anon Supabase access.

Alternatives considered:

  • Multi-tenant from day one. Rejected because auth, accounts, RLS policies, and password recovery flows would have tripled the build time without adding learning value at the prototype stage.

Why this won: Single-tenant v1 ships in a weekend and validates the core hypothesis. If the hypothesis fails, the multi-tenant work was avoided. If it succeeds, multi-tenant is a v2 unlock, not a v1 dependency. Classic build-measure-learn sequencing.

4. UX

The visual language is deliberately austere. Black on white. One typeface. No icons, illustrations, or animations beyond a 100ms fade on modal screens. This is a contrarian choice in a category dominated by warm, encouraging wellness aesthetics, and the reasoning is in section 3, decision 6 below.

Decision 6: Brutalist aesthetic in a warm-aesthetic category

Choice: Black and white, system sans, no decoration.

Why: Most wellness apps use warm colors and encouraging copy because their marketing positions them as friends. But the moment of compulsive opening is the wrong moment for friendliness; the user needs a stop sign, not a coach. The austere visual language matches the tonal need of the user state.

This is also a competitive differentiator. The product looks different in screenshots, communicates different intent, and signals seriousness in a category that often signals softness.

5. Validation and results

The test plan covers logging opens, bypasses and prompt/to-do interactions, but it contains no export of the 14-day log or verified reduction in daily opens. The existing reflection reports prompt gaming within 3 days and friction reviewing raw logs.

Metric: target: median daily opens down 30 percent by day 14, not observed.

What didn't work, and what I'd change

  • The 8-character minimum on the prompt was gamed within 3 days. Users (me) typed "asdfasdf" to bypass. A v2 version should evaluate semantic content, not just length, or use a delay-and-prompt rather than a character minimum.
  • The to-do list reset at midnight was the wrong choice. Items added at open 6 yesterday were often still relevant today. A 48-hour rolling window or manual archive would have served the user better.
  • The analytics view should have been in v1. Reviewing data weekly via Supabase's raw table editor added friction that almost prevented the weekly review from happening. The product should have shown its own data.
  • Mobile-only is a real limitation. v1 cannot block content inside Instagram, only gate access. For users whose problem is "once I'm in, I scroll for 45 minutes," v1 alone is insufficient. v2 addresses this.

6. Roadmap: v2 commercialization

What is live: V1 is the single-tenant PWA; the V2 link describes the multi-tap web version. The browser extension is designed, not built.

The v1 prototype tests the friction-layer hypothesis.

The v1 prototype validates the friction-layer hypothesis. v2 expands the product into a defensible commercial offering.

v2 scope expansion

Browser extension for desktop. A Chrome and Safari extension that runs on instagram.com and hides the feed, stories carousel, Reels, and Explore tab, while leaving DMs, posting, and profile views fully functional. Where v1 reduces opens, v2 reduces in-session time by removing the highest-dopamine surfaces.

Multi-user platform. Account creation, personal dashboards, configurable thresholds, configurable redirect options, optional weekly summary email.

Cross-app expansion. The friction-layer pattern is platform-agnostic. v2.5 extends the same model to TikTok, X, and Reddit. The underlying behavioral logic is identical.

7. What this project demonstrates

For the hiring manager evaluating fit for a PM role, this project demonstrates:

  • Problem identification. Spotted a market gap (escalating friction without lockouts) that established competitors have not addressed.
  • Trade-off reasoning. Six explicit design decisions with stated alternatives and rationale, including unflattering self-critique.
  • Technical fluency. Built the product, made architectural decisions, identified and worked within platform constraints (iOS Safari sandboxing, popup-blocker rules).
  • Validation discipline. Shipped a single-tenant prototype to test the hypothesis before building the multi-tenant version. Used myself as user zero. Documented qualitative self-reflections; quantitative outcomes remain unreported.
  • Roadmap sense. Articulated a defensible v2 commercialization path with competitive analysis, pricing hypothesis, and a thesis on what creates long-term moat.
  • Communication. Produced a PRD that scales from 30-second skim to 10-minute deep read, structured for the audience.

Product requirements

Luna Health

A cycle and fertility companion that turns logged periods and bloodwork into predictions and an honest readiness score.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Cycle data, bloodwork and fertility decisions live in separate places.
North star
70% of new users log a first cycle in their first session (target)

Luna Health

A cycle and fertility tracking app that turns daily inputs into clear predictions and a readiness picture, so users can understand and plan around their bodies.

The 30-second version. I built Luna Health as a cycle and fertility companion that turns logged periods and bloodwork into predictions and an honest readiness score. It is one V1 product. Lab trends, a reference guide, a testing-timing helper and export are designed as the next release. It is a wellness prototype, not medical advice or a clinically validated fertility assessment.

Timeline: V1 prototype. V2 scope designed, not yet built.

What is live: The live link is https://luna-health-v1.lovable.app. Overview, cycle logging, wellness and lab entry are live, and Try the demo on the sign in page opens it without an account.

1. Problem statement

People who menstruate, and especially those weighing egg freezing or fertility planning, are asked to make consequential, time-sensitive decisions with scattered information. Cycle data lives in one app, bloodwork lives in a clinic portal or a PDF, and the interpretation that connects them ("is my AMH good for my age, and what does that mean for my timeline?") lives only in a rushed appointment. The window for fertility decisions is genuinely time-sensitive, which makes the cost of fragmented, hard-to-interpret data high. Mainstream cycle trackers predict periods well but stop short of the fertility-readiness picture; clinical tools are not built for daily personal use. Luna's hypothesis is that a calm, single place that turns daily inputs and lab values into clear predictions and an honest readiness signal helps users understand their bodies and arrive at clinical conversations prepared, without ever pretending to be a doctor.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Turn logged cycles into clear, glanceable predictions (next period, fertile window, ovulation) (user outcome).
  • Give fertility-minded users an honest, transparent readiness picture from the markers that matter (user outcome).
  • Help users arrive at clinical appointments prepared, with their own data organized and exportable (user outcome).
  • Stay clearly and consistently on the wellness side of the medical line, building trust through honesty about limits (trust outcome).

Non-goals

  • Diagnosis or treatment recommendations. Out of scope and out of bounds; the app interprets and organizes, it does not prescribe.
  • Replacing a reproductive endocrinologist. Out of scope; the app's job is to get users to that conversation better prepared.
  • Selling supplements or fertility services. Out of scope; the checklist educates, it does not monetize through recommendations.
  • Cloud accounts at MVP. Out of scope; sensitive health data stays local until a privacy and security model is properly designed.
  • Social or community features. Out of scope; health data this sensitive stays private to each user.

3. The product

Luna Health V1 has four areas: Overview, Cycle, daily wellness and lab entry.

  • Overview: cycle predictions and a readiness score with a factor breakdown.
  • Cycle: period, flow and symptom logging.
  • Daily wellness: educational checklist, not a prescribed fertility intervention.
  • Lab entry: manually logged AMH, FSH, E2 and AFC with notes and stated limitations.

V2 scope (designed, not built or verified): lab trends, reference guide, testing-timing helper and clinician-ready export. A link alone does not establish a second functioning product.

4. User stories

As someone tracking my cycle, I want next period, fertile window, and ovulation predicted from my logs, so that I can plan around my body.

As someone considering egg freezing, I want an honest readiness picture from the markers that actually predict reserve, so that I know roughly where I stand before a consult.

As a patient preparing for an appointment, I want my bloodwork organized over time and exportable, so that I can have a productive conversation with my reproductive endocrinologist.

As someone whose AMH number means little to me, I want it interpreted relative to my age, so that I understand whether it is normal for me rather than against a generic cutoff.

As a privacy-conscious user, I want my health data stored on my device, so that I control something this sensitive.

Edge/empty state. As a brand-new user with no cycles or labs logged, I want clear empty states ("Log your first period to see predictions," "Log at least 2 results to see your trends") rather than blank charts, so that I know exactly what to do next.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Cycle logging (start, end, flow, symptoms) with persistence. Given a logged period, then it appears in history and feeds predictions.
  • Cycle predictions. Given at least one logged cycle, then next period, fertile window, ovulation, and current phase are shown.
  • Readiness score with transparent factor breakdown. Given available data, then a 0 to 100 score and per-factor status (good/fair/low/missing) are displayed, with missing factors named.
  • Lab logging for AMH, FSH, E2, AFC plus notes, with age-adjusted AMH interpretation.
  • A persistent, prominent not-medical-advice disclaimer.
  • Local persistence of all health data.
  • Daily wellness checklist with educational annotations, not clinical advice.

V2 scope (designed, not built)

  • Lab trends over time with charts and clinical reference lines.
  • A reference guide for each marker and a Day 2 to 3 testing-timing helper.
  • A clinician-ready text export of lab history and prep.

Future (P2)

  • Secure cloud sync with a proper health-data security model.
  • Symptom-to-prediction refinement as data accumulates.
  • Optional integrations (lab-result import, calendar).

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

One focused companion before a fuller studio.

V1 scopes cycle logging, predictions and readiness. Rejected alternative: build trends and exports before testing the basic loop. Cost: no clinician-ready export in V1. V2 is designed, not built.

A transparent rules-based readiness score, not a model.

Alternatives considered: a learned or proprietary fertility score (rejected, no data, and a black box is exactly wrong for a health decision); a single readiness number with no breakdown (rejected, opaque and untrustworthy). Why this won: a transparent points system, with every factor shown and missing data flagged honestly, is explainable and grounded in which markers actually predict reserve (AMH weighted highest). Hard trade-off: a simple points model is a rough approximation of a clinically complex picture; I mitigated it with prominent disclaimers and by routing users toward a reproductive endocrinologist rather than implying the score is diagnostic.

Age-adjusted interpretation instead of universal cutoffs.

Alternatives considered: a single AMH threshold for everyone (rejected, misleading across ages). Why this won: reading a value against the median for the user's age band is more honest and more useful, and it teaches the user something a flat cutoff cannot.

Wellness framing and a hard disclaimer line.

Alternatives considered: more assertive, quasi-clinical guidance for "usefulness" (rejected, it would cross into medical claims). Why this won: staying clearly on the wellness side protects users and the product, and honesty about limits is itself trust-building.

Local-only storage for sensitive data at MVP.

Alternatives considered: cloud sync from day one (rejected, sensitive reproductive-health data demands a security and privacy model I would not rush). Why this won: keep the most sensitive data on-device until sync can be done properly. Trade-off owned: no multi-device continuity yet.

7. UX

The aesthetic is warm and reassuring rather than clinical: a soft cream background, a deep plum-to-mauve header, a Cormorant Garamond serif for headings paired with DM Sans for body, rounded cards, and gentle color-coded status badges. The intent is to make a charged subject feel calm and in the user's control.

Core screens:

  • Cycle-window predictions, the readiness score with its factor breakdown, and dismissible insight cards.
  • A prediction banner with the current phase pill, a period-logging form (flow, symptom chips), and recent history.
  • A grouped egg-quality checklist with a progress bar and a "why it matters" note on each item.
  • Lab entry now; trend, timing, prep and export sub-tabs are designed V2 scope.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Activation: target 70 percent of new users logging a first cycle within the first session. Measured locally.
  • Lab adoption: target 40 percent of fertility-minded users logging at least one bloodwork result.
  • Repeat logging: target 50 percent of users logging two or more cycles (the threshold for regularity insights).

Lagging indicators

  • Preparedness outcome: target 60 percent of users reporting they felt more prepared for a clinical appointment. Measured via an optional prompt.
  • Retention: target 30 percent of users still logging at 60 days (a meaningful interval for cycle data).
  • Trust: zero instances of the product being mistaken for medical advice, tracked through user feedback and the disclaimer's visibility.

Figures are targets, not observed; this is a prototype and no broad validation has occurred.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Three honest reflections. First, the readiness score is a clinically rough approximation rendered as a confident number, and a confident number about fertility carries real emotional weight; I would pressure-test the model and its copy with a reproductive endocrinologist before putting it in front of strangers, and consider showing ranges rather than a single figure. Second, predictions assume a roughly 28-day cycle in the early logic, which is exactly wrong for the irregular cycles that often matter most in a fertility context; I would make the prediction engine learn each user's actual cycle length rather than defaulting to a textbook number. Third, I would keep V1 tight and ship lab trends and export as V2 only after the basic loop proves useful.

9. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: locating the gap as fragmented, hard-to-interpret data at a time-sensitive decision point.
  • Trade-off reasoning: a transparent rules-based score over a black box, and a deliberate V1-first scope with V2 designed.
  • Technical fluency: clinical reference ranges, age-adjusted interpretation, trend charts and export specified as future scope.
  • Domain and ethical judgment: staying firmly on the wellness side of the medical line, with honesty about the score's limits.
  • Validation discipline: explicit target metrics and a stated need for clinician review before exposing the score widely.
  • Communication and iteration: a single PRD that covers one product and is candid about what V2 would add and what still needs work.

Validation and next test

The next test compares predicted and actual cycle dates over three cycles. The readiness score is informational, not medical advice.

Product requirements

Ritual

A 20-day reset that teaches you to meditate on your own.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Meditation apps lose most users because the practice never ends.
North star
25% of paid users finish all 20 days (target)

Ritual: Product Requirements Document v2

The 30-second version. I built Ritual solo with AI-assisted development as a finite meditation reset, not an endless wellness library. The app includes a landing page, Day 1 session and progress surface today. The product bet is one finishable 20-day structure; payment, outcome measurement and a graduate program remain planned.

Live: https://mindful-journey-starter.lovable.app

What is built vs planned

Built: landing page, 20-day structure, session screen and progress.

Planned: paywall, PSS-10 capture and graduate program. Targets below are not observed outcomes.

Date: May 2026

1. Problem Statement

Self-improvement apps have a retention crisis, and the existing playbook for fixing it is not working.

The benchmarks:

  • Health and fitness apps average 6.3% Day 30 retention. Headspace sits at 7.65%, Calm at 8.34%. Insight Timer is the outlier at 16%, owing primarily to its free library and community model (Apptopia, StriveCloud 2026).
  • In randomized controlled trials of mindfulness apps, attrition averages 38.7% in studies with 100+ participants per group; Calm-specific RCT dropout is 28.4% (Linardon et al., 2023).
  • Across app categories, Day 7 retention averages 10 to 15%. Wellness apps with strong motivation design sit at the high end (Enable3, 2026).
  • The habit formation literature finds median time to behavioral automaticity is 66 days, range 18 to 254 (Lally et al., 2010, European Journal of Social Psychology). The "21 days to a habit" and "30 days to a habit" claims that dominate consumer wellness apps have no empirical basis.

The diagnosis: current wellness apps optimize for content consumption (library models, autoplay, daily picks) rather than behavioral execution. Users feel busy without progressing. They open the app, complete a session, and have no scaffold for what comes tomorrow or what they are building toward.

Three structural failure modes:

  1. Library overwhelm. Calm and Headspace each offer thousands of sessions. Beginners do not know which session, in what order, toward what outcome.
  2. No defined endpoint. Subscriptions are designed for indefinite use. There is no "I finished it" moment that produces identity reinforcement or a shareable accomplishment.
  3. Streaks as a brittle proxy. Daily streaks measure activity, not progression. Breaking a streak is demotivating; maintaining one rewards opening the app, not doing the work.

Couch to 5K, Duolingo, and Noom each succeeded by inverting these patterns: finite curriculum, structured progression, daily prescribed action.

2. Strategic Decision: Curated Product, Not Marketplace (Yet)

A creator marketplace and a curated wellness product require different unit economics, defensibility and teams.

Decision: launch as a curated product. Defer marketplace to Phase 3 (post-seed validation).

Rationale:

  • Marketplace dynamics require liquidity on both sides. At launch we have neither creators nor users. Two-sided cold starts are graveyards.
  • Quality and trust are existential in wellness. Open creator submission introduces medical-claim risk, content moderation load, and App Store guideline exposure we cannot afford pre-PMF.
  • The product hypothesis ("curriculum beats library") is testable with a single house-produced system. We do not need creators to falsify it.
  • Once we prove that one 20-day system retains and converts, we can stand up a vetted creator program with quality controls. Until then, creators are a distraction from the core bet.

3. Target User and ICP

Primary ICP: "The Restarter."

Demographics: 28 to 45, urban, knowledge worker, household income $75K+. Female-skewed (60/40), consistent with meditation app DAU composition.

Psychographics:

  • Has downloaded Calm or Headspace, used it inconsistently, and feels guilty about it
  • Has tried 3+ wellness apps in the last two years
  • Reads Atomic Habits, listens to podcasts about routines and rituals
  • Self-identifies as "someone who knows what to do but cannot get themselves to do it"
  • Will pay for measurable results; will not pay for inspiration

The key insight: this user does not need more content. They need a smaller, finishable thing they can actually complete.

Anti-ICP:

  • Experienced meditators (already have a practice; the product is not for them)
  • Users seeking clinical-grade mental health intervention (regulatory minefield, wrong product)
  • Users who already love Calm or Headspace (we are not winning that switch)

4. MVP Wedge: 20-Day Meditation Reset

Launching seven systems across fitness, yoga, meditation, emotional wellness, nutrition, music and vocal training would be a content company, not a focused MVP.

Pick one. Ship it. Prove the thesis.

Recommended wedge: 20-Day Meditation Reset

Why this one:

  1. Lowest content production cost. Audio only, no video, no instructor coordination. One voice talent, one studio week, roughly 6 hours of finished audio.
  2. Best fit for the "ritual" brand. Opening and closing audio reads native for meditation, awkward for triathlon training.
  3. Strongest evidence base. Headspace RCTs show measurable stress reduction at 8 weeks; we can credibly claim measurable change at 20 days (e.g., the Headspace observational study showed a 23.5% reduction in PSS-10 scores among moderate-stress members).
  4. Strongest market signal. Calm did $210M revenue in 2025, Headspace is comparable. TAM is proven. The opportunity is share capture, not category creation.
  5. Cleanest competitive wedge. Calm and Headspace are libraries. We are a course.

5. Why 20 Days (Honest Version)

Twenty days is not an empirically established optimum for behavioral momentum.

Honest framing:

  • 20 days is not enough to form a habit by Lally et al.'s 66-day median. We will not claim it is.
  • 20 days is enough to (a) complete a defined curriculum, (b) experience measurable mood and stress change in line with published 4-week RCT data, and (c) generate a sharable completion moment.
  • 20 days is short enough to feel finishable to a user with prior wellness-app drop-off history.

Positioning: "Your first 20 days. The start of a practice, not the end of one."

Phase 2 follow-on: a 40-day "Practice" system for graduates, framed as the next step. This handles the habit-formation gap honestly, reinforces the curriculum-over-library model, and creates a natural retention and upsell path.

6. Competitive Landscape

ProductModelD30 RetentionPricingWedge vs. Ritual
CalmLibrary + sleep8.3%$69.99/yrLibrary overwhelm; no progression
HeadspaceLibrary + courses7.7%$69.99/yrHas courses but they are buried in the library
Insight TimerLibrary + community16%Free + $59.99/yrCommunity is the moat; not curriculum
Ten Percent HappierLibrary + coursesn/a$99/yrClosest analogue; weaker UX, smaller catalog
Waking Up (Sam Harris)Curriculum-ledn/a$99/yrStrongest curriculum competitor; founder-dependent, no consumer brand
Duolingo (analog)Curriculum + streak~55% D1FreemiumProof structured daily curriculum scales to consumer hit
Couch to 5K (analog)Finite curriculumn/a$2.99 one-timeProof "finishable program" wins beginners

The whitespace: no product combines Calm-quality production, Waking Up-quality curriculum, and Duolingo-quality progression mechanics, marketed to the Restarter persona.

7. Differentiation Thesis

Three things, ranked by importance:

  1. Finite curriculum over infinite library. Every user lands on a single 20-day program. No browsing. No autoplay. One next action, every day.
  2. Practice mechanics over content mechanics. Timers, breath pacing, journal prompts, and reflection beats are integrated into each session, not separate features. The user is doing, not just listening.
  3. Completion as the core unit of value. The product is designed around the "I finished it" moment: completion certificate, pre/post stress score, optional share card, invite to graduate program.

Explicitly not differentiating on at MVP: AI personalization, social feed, gamification badges, video, hardware integrations, live cohorts.

8. Success Metrics (with Targets)

Activation

  • Day 1 session completion: 65% (industry top quartile for wellness onboarding is ~70%)
  • Day 3 session completion: 45%

Retention

  • Day 7 retention: 35% (vs. 10 to 15% industry average)
  • Day 20 retention: 20%

Completion (the headline metric)

  • Full 20-day completion rate among paid users: 25%
  • Adherence (days completed / 20): 60% average

If completion lands above 25%, the thesis is validated and we raise seed on it. If it lands below 10%, the thesis is wrong and we either shorten the program (test a 10-day variant) or reposition.

Monetization

  • Free-to-paid conversion (Day 7 paywall): 8% (Calm reports 3 to 5%, Duolingo Super 5 to 8%)
  • Annual plan share of paid: 60%
  • Month 3 paid retention: 70%

Outcomes (validation, not commitment)

  • Pre/post PSS-10 score reduction among completers: 15% target (Headspace real-world study: 23.5%)

9. Risks and Dependencies

Product risks

  • 20 days is still too long. Mitigation: A/B test a 10-day "Starter" variant in parallel; instrument every session for dropoff analysis.
  • Audio fatigue from one voice. Mitigation: contract two voices; user can switch.
  • Curriculum quality is the entire product. Mitigation: hire a senior teacher (Tara Brach lineage, Insight Meditation Society, or comparable) as Head of Curriculum on staff, not as a contractor.

Business risks

  • CAC. Wellness paid acquisition on Meta runs $40 to $80 per install in 2026. Mitigation: lead with organic (TikTok, Substack, founder-led content) for first 6 months; treat paid as a Q4 lever.
  • Subscription fatigue. The Restarter already holds 4+ wellness subscriptions. Mitigation: position as a 20-day program with an optional graduate plan, not "yet another monthly."
  • Competitive response from Calm or Headspace. Medium risk. Both have launched course features and largely failed to make them sticky. Bet: they cannot reorient a library-first product without cannibalizing existing users.

Regulatory and platform risks

  • App Store wellness claims. Cannot claim treatment of depression, anxiety, or any clinical condition. All marketing copy and in-product language reviewed by counsel.
  • HIPAA. Not triggered unless we integrate with healthcare providers. Out of scope for MVP.
  • Apple in-app purchase 30% fee. Plan for it. Consider web-to-app paid onboarding flow to capture annual subscriptions outside the IAP fee at launch.

Content and brand risks

  • Cultural appropriation. Meditation traditions have lineage and sensitivity. Mitigation: use secular, evidence-based framing for MVP. Do not market a tradition we have not licensed instruction from.
  • Trademark. Ritual Vitamins owns significant mindshare in adjacent wellness. Run a USPTO and common-law search before finalizing the name.

What this project demonstrates

  • I frame the problem as practice execution, not content scarcity.
  • I choose one finite curriculum over a two-sided marketplace.
  • I separate visible prototype scope from paywall and clinical-outcome plans.
  • I use measurable activation and completion targets rather than inventing results.
  • I state the habit-formation limit and the safety boundary plainly.

Product requirements

Cozy Diary

A private, cozy journal with voice entries and AI mood insights.

Live ↗
In real useSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Journaling apps feel either clinical or public, so people stop writing.
North star
40% of week-one writers return in week two (target)

Cozy Diary, Product Requirements Document

The 30-second version. I built Cozy Diary solo with AI-assisted development because journaling apps often feel either clinical or social. My bet is a private journal that feels like paper and works like software: write or speak, then see AI mood insights after enough entries. The live link leads to diary access and sign-in.

Live: https://cozydiary.lovable.app

Tagline: Sign in and we'll take you back to your diary.

Problem

Journaling helps people notice patterns in how they feel, but most journaling apps feel either clinical or social. People stop writing when entries feel exposed, when typing is a chore at the end of a long day, or when nothing comes back from the effort. Cozy Diary answers all three: private by default, voice-friendly, and with gentle mood insights that make writing feel worth it.

1. Vision & Positioning

Cozy Diary is a calm, private, voice-friendly journaling web app. Each diary lives at its own URL, protected by a per-device PIN, with optional AI-powered mood insights. The experience aims to feel like paper but work like software: claim a private URL, write or speak entries, and watch your emotional landscape emerge over time.

Privacy is the default. Entries are owned by the writer, accessed via password and per-device PIN, and recoverable through a memorable phrase. The product is positioned against three categories it deliberately is not: clinical mental-health apps (too medical), mass-market note apps (too utilitarian), and social journaling platforms (not private). Cozy Diary sits in a quiet corner of its own: solitary, warm, reflective, and low-friction.

The product succeeds when a user opens the app, writes for three minutes, and closes it without noticing the interface. The interface is furniture; the writing is the point.

2. Target Users

PersonaNeedPrimary Friction to EliminateSecondary Benefit
Reflective writerDistraction-free daily journalingSetup overhead, blank-page anxietyInsights surface patterns they would otherwise miss
Voice journalerCapture thoughts by speakingPoor transcription, clumsy mobile UXSpeech-to-text invites longer, more honest entries
Self-trackerUnderstand mood patterns over timeClinical, over-medicalized framingPassive mood data without manual logging
Privacy-conscious userStrong access control with simple setupComplex key management, unclear data ownershipPer-device PIN stays local even if password leaks

All four personas share one trait: they want a journal, not a product. The app should feel like a tool they own, not a service they subscribe to.

3. Assumptions & Constraints

Platform. Responsive web only (mobile-first layout). No native mobile app in v1. No desktop installer.

Auth provider. Supabase Auth with email and password. No OAuth, no magic-link-only flows, no anonymous sessions in v1.

Backend runtime. Supabase Edge Functions (Deno, TypeScript). PostgreSQL with RLS. No custom backend services.

AI features. Mood analysis and weekly summary run on demand, not in real time. Transcription runs per-recording, synchronously from the user's point of view.

Account shape. One diary slug per account in v1. No multi-diary, no shared diary, no team accounts.

Browser support. Latest two stable versions of Chrome, Safari, Firefox, and Edge. iOS Safari 16 and newer. Android Chrome 110 and newer. Graceful degradation on older browsers (sign-in works, voice may not).

Internationalization. English copy only in v1. Timezone is the browser's local zone for display; dates stored in UTC.

Connectivity. The app assumes online operation. An offline mode is in scope for a future version.

4. Feature Inventory

4.1 Authentication & Access

Email-plus-password accounts are the only identity path. Passwords require 8 or more characters with at least one number or symbol. A 4-digit PIN is registered per device, hashed with PBKDF2 (200,000 iterations, SHA-256, per-device salt). Device identity is a browser fingerprint scoped to the browser-and-device pair; the fingerprint is stable across visits but resets when site data is cleared.

Recovery uses a 12-word BIP39 phrase (at least 128 bits of entropy), stored as a bcrypt hash (cost 12). The prior 4-word-from-24 scheme is rejected as cryptographically weak (19 bits, brute-forceable). Recovery invalidates all other device PIN rows for the user.

Lockout escalates on the server. After 5 failed PIN attempts on a device, a 30-second cooldown applies. Subsequent failure rounds double the cooldown (30s, 60s, 120s, 240s) up to a cap of 30 minutes. Cooldown state is server-enforced and not bypassable by clearing localStorage.

Unlock state has two timers: a 30-minute TTL in localStorage for navigation within a session, and a 5-minute idle auto-lock measured by user-input events. The idle lock is a stricter constraint and overrides the TTL.

Password reset flows through an emailed link and renders at /reset-password. Tokens expire in 1 hour.

4.2 Diary URLs & Slug System

Each account owns exactly one slug. Slugs are 3 to 40 characters, lowercase alphanumeric with hyphens ([a-z0-9-]), no leading or trailing hyphens, no consecutive hyphens. Slugs are immutable in v1 (rename is listed as future work).

Reserved slugs cannot be claimed: admin, api, signin, signup, reset-password, insights, settings, about, help, privacy, terms, d, static, healthz, www, mail, root.

The slug-status edge function returns { status, hint? } where status is available, claimed, reserved, or invalid. Visiting a claimed slug while signed out renders the sign-in prompt, not the Claim screen. Visiting an unclaimed slug while signed in offers to claim it or switch to the user's existing slug.

4.3 Writing (Editor)

The Editor has an optional title field (plain text, 120-character limit) and a rich-text body (no hard limit; a soft warning appears past 10,000 characters). Autosave fires every 10 seconds and on blur or route change. The Cmd+S or Ctrl+S shortcut triggers a manual save.

Voice recording opens a modal with a single record button, waveform visualization, a 5-minute timer, and a submit action. Audio is sent to transcribe and the returned text is inserted at the cursor position. The audio itself is not persisted server-side.

Spell check runs automatically after 3 seconds of typing inactivity and can be triggered manually. Suggestions with confidence at or above 0.9 are auto-applied when the user has enabled that setting; others are shown as inline underlines with a dropdown.

Draft recovery: if the user navigates away with unsaved content, a "Restore unsaved draft?" banner appears on the next entry open.

4.4 Insights

Insights is gated until at least three entries have been analyzed (not merely written). The mood chart shows one dot per analyzed entry across a rolling 90-day window, with valence on the Y-axis (0 to 1, where 1 is most positive) and date on the X-axis. Dots are colored by dominant emotion and shaped distinctly so color is not the sole differentiator.

The emotion bars show the aggregate share of each Atlas of Emotions category across analyzed entries in the window. The weekly summary card generates a narrative paragraph from weekly-summary, scoped to the last seven days. If fewer than two entries exist in that window, the card shows a "Not enough data yet" state.

A Re-analyze button re-scores all entries (useful after edits). It is throttled to one invocation per 10 minutes per user.

4.5 Settings

Settings include:

  • auto_apply_high_confidence toggle for spell-check
  • Slug display (read-only)
  • Email display (read-only; linked to password reset)
  • Forget this device (removes the current device's row in pin_devices)
  • Delete account (destructive; requires password confirm; hard-deletes all entries, settings, device rows, and mood scores)

5. Information Architecture

The complete route map:

/                              Landing (slug picker + sign-in link)
/signin                        Centralized sign-in
/reset-password                Password reset landing (from email link)
/d/:slug                       Diary shell
  → ClaimDiaryScreen           (slug unclaimed)
  → SignInPromptScreen         (slug claimed, signed out)
  → SetDevicePinScreen         (signed in, no device PIN, skippable)
  → LockScreen                 (signed in, device PIN set, not unlocked)
  → HomeScreen                 (unlocked: default tab)
  → EditorScreen               (unlocked: new or existing entry)
/d/:slug/insights              Insights (mood chart + summary)
/d/:slug/settings              Settings (toggle + device mgmt + danger zone)
*                              NotFound

The diary shell at /d/:slug routes to one of six sub-screens based on auth and device state.

6. Security & Privacy

6.1 Controls

  • RLS on every user-owned table; policies scoped to auth.uid().
  • PINs never leave the device in plaintext; only PBKDF2 hashes are stored.
  • Recovery phrases stored as bcrypt hashes (cost 12). Never logged. Never returned after initial reveal.
  • Device-scoped PINs prevent a leaked PIN from unlocking other devices.
  • Server-enforced lockout with exponential backoff; not bypassable client-side.
  • 5-minute idle auto-lock; 30-minute unlock TTL covers intentional navigation only.
  • Recovery invalidates all other device PIN rows.
  • All edge function responses include Cache-Control: no-store.
  • body_plain (used for mood scoring) is stored separately and never included in entry-list responses.
  • TLS required on all origins. HSTS enabled with a 1-year max-age.
  • Supabase JWTs are short-lived (1 hour) with refresh tokens.

6.2 STRIDE Threat Model

ThreatVectorMitigation
SpoofingAttacker impersonates a user via stolen credentialsPassword + per-device PIN; recovery phrase entropy of 128 bits; device fingerprint binding
SpoofingAttacker replays a captured JWTShort-lived tokens (1h); HTTPS only; tokens bound to user in Supabase Auth
TamperingMalicious client modifies PIN attempt counterLockout state is server-side only; client cannot write to lockout_state
TamperingSQL injection via slug parameterParameterized queries; slug regex validated at edge function and DB
RepudiationUser denies writing an entryentries.created_at and updated_at are server-set; audit log for account changes
Information DisclosureEntry content leaked via error logsError payloads scrub entry bodies; Sentry has content allowlist
Information DisclosureMood data inferred by timing attacks on public endpointsConstant-time PIN comparison; rate limits on slug-status
Information DisclosureEmail enumeration via sign-in errorsGeneric INVALID_CREDENTIALS response for all bad sign-ins
Denial of ServiceBrute-force PIN attempts5-attempt threshold, exponential cooldown, per-device-per-IP limits
Denial of ServiceMass account creationclaim-slug rate-limited to 5 per IP per hour
Denial of ServiceAI endpoint abuseanalyze-mood capped at 100/user/day; transcribe at 20/user/hour
Elevation of PrivilegeUser tries to read another user's entriesRLS policies enforce auth.uid() = user_id on every query
Elevation of PrivilegeUser tries to claim a reserved slugReserved list checked at both edge function and DB CHECK constraint

6.3 Data Privacy & Compliance

Data residency. The Supabase region is documented on the privacy page. Users can see where their data is stored before claiming a diary.

Retention. Entries are retained until the user deletes them individually or deletes their account. Account deletion is a hard delete; there is no soft-delete or grace period in v1.

Right to erasure (GDPR Art. 17, CCPA). Satisfied by the "Delete account" flow in Settings. Deletion cascades through all related tables. Confirmation is via password re-entry; a second email confirmation is under consideration for v2.

Right to access (GDPR Art. 15). Not yet satisfied in v1; export is listed as future work. A manual data-request email workflow is documented in the internal operations handbook.

AI processing disclosure. Mood analysis sends body_plain to the LLM endpoint. This is disclosed in plain language on the privacy page. Entry content is not used for model training; the vendor contract prohibits training use.

Analytics. No third-party analytics in v1. Error monitoring (Sentry) has a strict payload allowlist: no entry content, no email addresses, no PII beyond user ID.

Cookie policy. Session cookies only; no advertising or marketing cookies. Local storage is used for the unlock TTL and nothing else.

7. Design Language

Typography. Serif headings for a paper feel (for example, Fraunces or Lora). Sans-serif body for readability (for example, Inter-substitute or Söhne). No system-font stack.

Surfaces. A paper utility class renders a warm off-white card on a subtle background tint. Borders are 1px solid at low opacity; no drop shadows on cards.

Motion. animate-fade-in for entry transitions. transition-gentle (250ms, ease-out) for hover and state changes. All motion respects prefers-reduced-motion. No spring physics, no bounce, no parallax.

Iconography. Lucide icons at strokeWidth={1.5}, 20px sizing.

Tone. Quiet, encouraging, never clinical. The product speaks in first-person plural ("we'll take you back to your diary") and refers to the user's content as "yours". Error messages are warm and specific. Avoid: diagnostic framing, streak language, nudging.

Density. Low. Generous line height (1.6 for body, 1.2 for headings). No data tables shown to users. Entry list uses calendar-style grouping with labels between groups.

Color. A warm neutral palette: off-white paper, deep slate text, a single muted accent (dusk blue or sage). Emotion-category colors for the chart and bars; these are chosen from a color-blind-safe palette.

8. Non-Goals (v1)

  • Social or sharing features. Diaries are single-user.
  • Native mobile apps.
  • Entry export or import.
  • Multiple diaries per account.
  • Team or family accounts.
  • Reminders, streaks, or gamification.
  • Slug rename.
  • End-to-end encryption of entry bodies.
  • OAuth or social sign-in.
  • Offline mode.
  • Rich media attachments.
  • Search across entries.
  • Tags or custom prompts.

9. Open Questions & Future Work

ItemPriorityNotes
Entry export (Markdown, PDF)HighDirect user value; should ship in v2
Full-text searchHighPrivacy-first: client-side index preferred, encrypted-server-index acceptable
Tags and custom promptsMediumPairs well with mood insights for pattern-finding
Multi-device PIN management UIMediumDevice list view with name, last used, "forget" action
Slug renameLowBreaks bookmarks; requires redirect layer
End-to-end encryption of entry bodiesMediumBlocks server-side AI features; requires UX for key management

10. Success Metrics

SignalTargetHow measured
7-day retention40% of week-1 writers return in week 2Weekly cohort analysis
Writing frequency30% of active users write 3+ entries/weekWeekly rolling metric
Insights unlock rate50% of accounts reach 3 analyzed entries in 14 daysFunnel analysis
PIN lockout rateUnder 1% of unlock attempts hit cooldownlockout_state monitoring
Recovery success rateOver 90% of recovery attempts succeed first tryrecover-pin logs
Transcription satisfactionUnder 5% of voice outputs deleted entirelyEntry diffs after transcription
Autosave reliabilityZero data-loss incidents per quarterIncident tracking
Time to first entryUnder 90 seconds from landing to first saveInstrumented funnel

Key decisions and trade-offs

  • Per-device PIN instead of app-wide lock. Rejected alternative: one shared lock across devices. Cost: extra device setup in exchange for independent revocation.
  • Insights after three analyzed entries. Rejected alternative: chart a single entry. Cost: delayed payoff in exchange for avoiding thin-data interpretation.
  • Voice-first capture. Rejected alternative: typing-only. Cost: transcription latency and sensitive processing disclosures, accepted for easier expression.

What didn't work, and what I'd change

The sign-up, PIN and recovery-phrase chain is heavy for a first visit. This is an interaction risk, not a measured abandonment finding. I would let a visitor understand the private writing experience before making the recovery burden prominent, while preserving access security.

What this project demonstrates

  • Privacy-led product positioning.
  • Device-aware access and recovery reasoning.
  • Thin-data restraint in mood insights.
  • Voice capture with an explicit processing boundary.
  • Scope discipline: solitary writing rather than social engagement.

Product requirements

Daily Affirmations

One steady sentence to start the day, in a few seconds, with no account.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Affirmation apps bury the one sentence you came for under feeds, streaks and upsells.
North star
Return visits on consecutive days

Daily Affirmations

Problem

Affirmation apps tend to bury the one sentence you came for under feeds, streaks and upsells.

Who it is for

Someone who wants one steady sentence to start the day, in a few seconds, with no account.

What it does today

  • Shows one affirmation per day from a set of 48 original lines inspired by Louise Hay.
  • Lets you save the ones you like on your device.
  • Lets you step through all 48 with Previous and Next.
  • Offers a Find support link if you are having a hard time.

Key decisions

  • Original lines. I wrote the 48 lines myself rather than copying published affirmations, to keep the spirit of Louise Hay without reusing her text.
  • No login. Nothing is stored on a server.
  • One card a day. It stays calm and quick.

Out of scope

  • Accounts.
  • Reminders.
  • Social sharing.
  • AI generated affirmations.

How I would measure it

Return visits on consecutive days, and the share of visitors who save at least one card.

What I would do next

A gentle optional morning reminder, and a small Saved view to reread favorites.

Product requirements

Lull

A focused sleep app for a calm, distraction-free wind-down.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
The hour before bed is unstructured and full of a glowing phone.
North star
Wind-down reached on 60% of nights (target)

Lull

A focused sleep app that does one thing: help you wind down and put the phone down at a consistent time.

The 30-second version. Lull is a deliberately small sleep app with exactly three features: an automatic Wind Down that activates 45 minutes before your target sleep time, a Wrap It Up button with 4-7-8 breathing for any moment you need it, and a Morning Payoff that logs how you slept and feels and surfaces one honest insight. No accounts, no streaks, no social, no clutter. I built it solo with AI-assisted development, dark-mode only, optimized for one-handed use, with everything stored locally so a user can start in seconds. It demonstrates the discipline of ruthless scoping: shipping three features that compound rather than ten that distract.

Timeline: Weekend prototype, in self-use testing.

What is live: The live build has Wind Down while open, Wrap It Up and Morning Payoff. A closed browser cannot reliably initiate Wind Down or morning reminders; that depends on notifications and platform support, which remain next scope.

1. Problem statement

A large share of people do not sleep poorly because they lack information; they sleep poorly because the hour before bed is unstructured and full of a glowing phone. The gap between "I should wind down" and actually putting the phone down is where consistent sleep dies. Most sleep apps make this worse: they pile on libraries of sounds, sleep-stage analytics, paywalls, accounts, and streaks that turn rest into another performance to optimize. The cost of the status quo is a nightly drift past the intended bedtime, compounding into chronic short sleep. Lull's hypothesis is that the highest-leverage intervention is not more data, it is a calm, near-zero-friction ritual that nudges a single behavior: be asleep by a consistent time.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Help the user fall asleep at a consistent time by structuring the 45 minutes before it (user outcome).
  • Make winding down feel calm and effortless, not like another task to manage (user outcome).
  • Give the user one honest, motivating insight that connects bedtime to how they feel (user outcome).
  • Prove that a three-feature sleep app can change behavior without accounts, streaks, or upsells (product outcome).

Non-goals

  • Sleep-stage tracking or wearable integration. Out of scope; the bet is behavior change, not measurement.
  • Accounts or login for the MVP. Out of scope to keep time-to-first-use near zero.
  • Streaks, badges, or gamification. Out of scope because pressure undermines rest.
  • Social or sharing features. Out of scope; sleep is private and solitary by design.
  • A large content library. Out of scope; four ambient options are enough, more is clutter.

3. The product

Three features, reachable in no more than three taps, on a single calm home screen showing tonight's target, the current time, one "Wrap it up" button, and a link to history.

  1. Wind Down (automatic). Activates on its own 45 minutes before the saved sleep target. A full-screen breathing gradient, a chosen ambient track (Rain, Binaural Beats, Lo-fi, or Silence), and a live countdown ("Winding down, sleep in 32 min"). The app locks into a calm state with only two choices: "I'm ready to sleep" (logs actual sleep time, transitions to a blank dark screen with a single moon) or "Not yet" (dismisses for 15 minutes, then re-prompts gently).
  2. Wrap It Up (on demand). A button for any moment. A calming transition screen, the user's chosen ambient audio, then a guided 4-7-8 breathing animation (inhale 4, hold 7, exhale 8) for three automatic cycles, ending with "Ready to put the phone down?" and a goodnight log or a few more minutes.
  3. Morning Payoff. At wake time, one question: "How do you feel this morning?" on a five-emoji scale. Then a single insight card (last night's sleep time, mood score, and a one-line comparison to the recent average), plus a 14-night history chart with mood color-coded on each bar and one auto-generated pattern insight ("Your best mornings follow nights you're asleep by 11:15pm").

The data model is intentionally tiny: per night, the target sleep time, the actual sleep time, the morning mood score, and the date. Everything is local.

4. User stories

As someone who scrolls past my bedtime, I want the app to start winding me down automatically, so that I do not have to remember or decide to begin.

As someone wound up at night, I want a quick breathing exercise on demand, so that I can settle without hunting through menus.

As someone curious whether bedtime matters for me, I want one honest insight linking when I slept to how I feel, so that I am motivated by my own data rather than generic advice.

As a first-time user, I want to start with a single question and no sign-up, so that I am using the app within seconds.

Edge/empty state. As a user on my first morning with no history, I want a graceful single-night card instead of an empty chart, so that the app feels alive from day one rather than waiting for two weeks of data.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • First-launch setup capturing sleep target and wake time, no account. Given a first open, when the user picks the two times, then they are saved locally and setup is complete.
  • Wind Down auto-activation 45 minutes before target. Given the saved target, when the clock reaches target minus 45, then the full-screen calm state activates with countdown and chosen audio.
  • "I'm ready to sleep" logs actual sleep time; "Not yet" re-prompts after 15 minutes.
  • Wrap It Up with the 4-7-8 breathing animation, three cycles, and a goodnight log.
  • Ambient audio with four options and saved preference.
  • Morning Payoff mood capture, insight card, and 14-night history chart with one auto-generated insight.
  • Local persistence of target, actual sleep time, mood, and date per night.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Optional account and cloud sync (for multi-device users).
  • True OS notifications at wind-down and wake time (vs in-app reminders).
  • A second ambient pack.

Future (P2)

  • Gentle longitudinal nudges if bedtime is drifting later over weeks.
  • Optional wearable import for users who want it, without making it central.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Exactly three features, no more. Alternatives considered: a fuller sleep suite with analytics and a sound library (rejected, that is the cluttered product the user is escaping). Why this won: each feature targets a distinct moment (the scheduled wind-down, the on-demand reset, the morning reflection) and they compound. Hard trade-off: a thin feature set is easy to dismiss as simplistic and harder to differentiate on a feature-comparison chart; I accepted that because restraint is the entire value proposition.

No accounts, local-only storage. Alternatives considered: require sign-up for sync and retention hooks (rejected, friction at exactly the wrong moment, and it makes the app feel heavy). Why this won: time-to-first-use near zero matters more than cloud features at MVP. Trade-off owned: data is trapped on one device and lost if cleared; sync becomes a clear P1 once the core loop is proven.
