Automatic Wind Down instead of a manual button. Alternatives considered: a "start wind down" button (rejected, it relies on the very willpower the user lacks at night). Why this won: the app initiates the ritual so the user does not have to, which is the behavioral crux. Wrap It Up still exists as the manual on-demand path.

No streaks or gamification. Alternatives considered: a streak counter for stickiness (rejected, it converts rest into performance and punishes the bad nights people already feel bad about). Why this won: the morning insight motivates through self-knowledge, not guilt.

7. UX

Dark-mode only, by design, because the product lives at night. Deep navy and warm amber, soft rounded corners, large readable type for tired eyes, one-handed reach. Every screen is meant to feel like it is already winding you down just by looking at it.

Core screens:

  • A single question, a time picker, no account.
  • Tonight's target large and centered, current time, one "Wrap it up" button, a history link.
  • Full-screen breathing gradient, live countdown, and only two calm choices.
  • The 4-7-8 breathing circle with clearly labeled phases.
  • One mood question, a single insight card, and the 14-night chart.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Wind-down engagement: target 60 percent of nights where the user reaches the wind-down state. Measured locally.
  • Goodnight logging: target 70 percent of nights with a logged actual sleep time. Measured via the nightly record.
  • Morning completion: target 60 percent of mornings with a mood entry.

Lagging indicators

  • Consistency: target a reduction of 20 minutes in the variance between target and actual sleep time over two weeks.
  • Felt outcome: target an upward trend in average morning mood for users who hit their target. Measured from the history data.
  • Retention: target 40 percent of users still logging at 14 nights.

All figures above are targets, not observed; this is a self-use prototype and no broad usage has been validated.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Three honest reflections. First, local-only storage means I cannot actually measure cohort retention or behavior change across users, which is the metric that matters most; without an account or anonymous telemetry, my success metrics are aspirational, and I would add privacy-respecting analytics before claiming the app changes behavior. Second, in-app reminders are weaker than true OS notifications, and a PWA that is not open cannot reliably trigger the automatic Wind Down, which undercuts the single most important feature; I would prioritize real notifications even though it adds platform complexity. Third, the auto-generated "best mornings follow nights you're asleep by 11:15pm" insight is only as good as the pattern logic behind it, and with sparse early data it risks asserting correlations that are noise; I would gate that insight behind a minimum number of nights and label confidence honestly.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • Can a PWA reliably trigger time-based wind-down and wake prompts, or does this need a native shell? (eng)
  • How many nights of data are needed before the pattern insight is trustworthy? (data)
  • Does sync improve retention enough to justify introducing accounts? (data, user)
  • Keep timing open for other users and test adjustable lead time.

v2 scope: optional sync, true notifications, and a confidence-gated insight engine.

Market positioning: against feature-heavy sleep platforms, Lull competes on calm and immediacy. Its defensibility is taste and restraint rather than a content moat, and the bet is that a meaningful segment wants exactly less.

GTM hypothesis: the product is small enough to be its own pitch ("a sleep app with three features and no account"), well suited to word-of-mouth and a single strong landing page rather than paid acquisition.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: isolating the pre-bed hour and a glowing phone as the real failure point, not a lack of data.
  • Trade-off reasoning: defending a three-feature scope and local-only storage, and owning what each costs.
  • Technical fluency: a minimal data model, an automatic time-triggered state machine, and a 14-night insight chart specified cleanly.
  • Validation discipline: acknowledging that local-only storage limits what can be measured, with explicit targets.
  • Roadmap sense: a clear ordering (prove the loop, then add sync and notifications) rather than building everything at once.
  • Communication: a spec that mirrors the product's own calm and clarity.

Validation and next test

The next test compares actual start and goodnight times over two weeks.

Product requirements

Morning Intentions

A morning routine that turns the first hour into a repeatable ritual.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
An overwhelming backlog makes the first move of the day harder than the work.
North star
A first move set on 60% of days (target)

Morning (Morning Intentions)

A morning routine app that turns the hardest part of the day, getting started, into a repeatable momentum ritual.

Live: https://start-the-day.lovable.app

The 30-second version. Morning is a small app built to solve a specific, personal problem: getting out of bed and actually starting the day when you have a million things to do and none of them feel possible yet. Each morning you choose a first move, three big things, five small wins, one fun thing and rest, so you begin with clear direction instead of paralysis. I built it solo with AI-assisted development, scoped tightly around the friction of the first hour rather than a full habit-tracker. It demonstrates starting from a real, observed user pain (my own), translating it into the smallest mechanic that relieves it, and resisting the urge to over-build.

Live today and next

Live today

  • First move, Top 3 for today and 5 small wins.
  • Repeating daily routine rows, one fun thing and daily essentials.
  • Bonus points (organize, workout, meditation) and wellbeing goals (screen time, social media, on time) on a 0 to 3 scale.
  • A 14-day tracker and a weekly rest check across seven kinds of rest.

Next

  • An optional morning reminder.
  • A gentle end-of-day summary.
  • Optional sync for people who use more than one device.

Timeline: Weekend prototype, in self-use testing.

1. Problem statement

An overwhelming backlog makes the first move of the day harder than the work itself. Morning narrows attention to a first move, a few priorities and achievable wins, without making fun or rest something the user has to earn. The hypothesis is momentum from a bounded start, not another all-day task manager.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Give the user a clear first move each morning, so starting is easier than staying in bed (user outcome).
  • Reduce morning overwhelm by capping the day's focus to a few big and a few small things (user outcome).
  • Generate early momentum through quick, achievable wins (user outcome).
  • Prove that a single-purpose morning ritual changes how the day begins, without becoming a full task manager (product outcome).

Non-goals

  • A full task manager or backlog. Out of scope; surfacing everything is the problem this app exists to avoid.
  • Projects, due dates, or tags. Out of scope; complexity defeats the calm-start purpose.
  • Streaks or guilt mechanics. Out of scope; a missed morning should not punish the user into avoidance.
  • Social or sharing features. Out of scope; the morning ritual is personal.
  • Evening or all-day planning. Out of scope at MVP; the bet is specifically on the first hour.

3. The product

One screen, once each morning: choose a first move, three big things, five small wins, one fun thing and rest. Check them off without a backlog or a streak penalty. Fun and rest sit beside work rather than being rewards for productivity.

4. User stories

As someone who struggles to get out of bed, I want to name three big things and five small ones first, so that I start the day with direction instead of paralysis.

As someone overwhelmed by everything I could do, I want a hard cap on how much I plan, so that the morning feels possible rather than crushing.

As someone who needs momentum, I want a few easy wins I can check off quickly, so that early completions pull me into the day.

As a returning user, I want yesterday cleared and a fresh, empty morning, so that the app is about today, not an accumulating backlog.

Edge/empty state. As a user on a low-energy morning, I want it to be fine to fill in only one or two items and still feel I have started, so that the app never shames a small start.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Set up to three big things and five small things for the day. Given a new morning, when the user adds items, then they are saved as today's list within the caps.
  • Check off completion with a clear, satisfying state. Given an item, when the user marks it done, then it is visibly completed.
  • A clean daily reset. Given a new day, then the list starts fresh without yesterday's clutter.
  • Local persistence of the day's items and completion state.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • A pre-bed version of the first-move commitment; the morning first move is already in the flow.
  • An optional one-line "why this matters" per big thing.
  • A gentle end-of-day summary of what got done.
  • An optional morning reminder.

Future (P2)

  • Light reflection on which mornings felt best and why.
  • Optional sync and an account for multi-device users.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Cap the list at three big and five small. Alternatives considered: an unlimited to-do list (rejected, it recreates the overwhelm that is the actual problem); a single MIT-style "most important task" (rejected, too thin to give the day shape or generate quick wins). Why this won: three big items force prioritization while five small ones manufacture early momentum, and the cap keeps the screen from ever feeling like a backlog. Hard trade-off: real life sometimes has more than three priorities, and a hard cap can feel restrictive; I accepted that because the restriction is precisely the therapeutic mechanism.

Iteration: One fun thing and rest were added after the original three-plus-five cap to keep the morning from equating a good day with work alone; this is a scope change, not a measured behavior outcome.

**A morning-only ritual

A morning-only ritual, not an all-day planner. Alternatives considered: a full-day or evening planning surface (rejected, it dilutes the focus on the specific friction point, the first hour). Why this won: solving one moment well beats solving the whole day poorly. Trade-off owned: users wanting an all-day system will need another tool; that is a deliberate boundary.

No streaks or guilt mechanics. Alternatives considered: a streak counter for stickiness (rejected, it punishes the exact bad mornings the user already struggles with and can drive avoidance). Why this won: momentum should come from today's wins, not yesterday's debt.

Local-only and account-free at MVP. Alternatives considered: accounts and sync from the start (rejected, friction at the wrong moment for a fast morning ritual). Why this won: open and start in seconds. Trade-off owned: no cross-device continuity yet, a clear P2.

7. UX

The interaction language is calm and minimal: a short morning screen, generous spacing, large readable type, and a satisfying check-off. The aim is for the act of planning to take under a minute and to feel like a gentle nudge into the day rather than a chore.

Core screens:

  • Three big-thing fields and five small-thing fields, capped and uncluttered.
  • The day's items with clear, tappable completion states and a sense of progress as wins accumulate.
  • A forgiving start that accepts a single item without penalty.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Morning completion of the ritual: target 60% of days where the user sets at least one big and one small thing. Measured locally.
  • Quick-win rate: target 50% of small things checked off by midday.
  • Repeat use: target 40% of users returning the next morning.

Lagging indicators

  • Felt outcome: target an upward trend in a simple "how did the day start" rating on days the ritual is used. Measured via an optional prompt.
  • Retention: target 35% of users still using the ritual at 14 days.
  • Time-to-start: target a self-reported reduction in how long it takes to get going in the morning.

Figures are targets, not observed; this is a self-use prototype built from a personal pain point and has not been validated more broadly.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Three honest reflections. First, the app helps you plan the morning but does almost nothing to get you out of bed, which is the literal first obstacle the founder described; the first move is now included, and the next gap is verifying whether planning actually shortens time-to-start, rather than assuming it does. Second, with local-only storage I cannot measure whether the ritual actually shortens time-to-start across days, so my core claim is currently a hypothesis; I would add privacy-respecting logging of ritual-used versus not and a one-tap morning rating to test it. Third, the three-plus-five cap is an untested intuition, not a validated number; it may be too many on a heavy day or too few on a light one, and I would experiment with a smaller default (one big, three small) to see whether an even lower bar improves the rate of actually starting.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • Is three-plus-five the right cap, or does an even smaller default get more people started? (user, data)
  • What is the highest-leverage way to bridge from "I'm awake" to "I'm up," beyond planning? (design, user)
  • Does a gentle reminder help or does it add pressure that backfires? (user)
  • Is sync worth the friction of accounts for this kind of quick ritual? (data)

v2 scope: the pre-bed "first move" commitment, an optional why-line per priority, a gentle end-of-day glance, and instrumentation to test the core claim.

Market positioning: against full task managers and habit trackers, Morning competes on doing one thing (the first hour) and doing it with the least possible friction. Its defensibility is focus and restraint rather than feature breadth.

GTM hypothesis: the product is its own pitch ("an app for people who can't get started in the morning"), well suited to a single resonant landing page and word-of-mouth among people who recognize the exact problem.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: starting from a real, specifically articulated user pain (the founder's own morning paralysis).
  • Trade-off reasoning: capping scope to the first hour and a fixed list size, and owning what that excludes.
  • Technical fluency: a deliberately minimal data model and a one-screen daily ritual, built to be fast.
  • Validation discipline: naming the core claim as an untested hypothesis and what instrumentation would prove it.
  • Self-critique: recognizing that the app does not yet solve the literal first obstacle (getting up) and reprioritizing accordingly.
  • Communication: a tight spec that matches the product's one-minute, one-screen ambition.

Product requirements

Care Circle

A consent-first button that lets you tell a chosen circle how you are doing. It never guesses your state and never auto-escalates.

Live ↗
LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
People who love someone going through a hard time do not know when they are needed.
North star
50% of new users add three or more circle members (target)

Care Circle

A consent-first button that lets you tell a chosen circle how you are doing. It never guesses your state and never auto-escalates.

The 30-second version. Care Circle lets a person pick a small circle of three to eight trusted people and send check-in signals at four levels, from "I'm okay" to "Not okay," with friends responding through pre-composed, low-pressure messages. The hardest design constraint is the safety architecture: the app never infers mental state, never auto-dials emergency services, and always shows crisis resources before any notification goes out. I built it solo with a strict consent-first model, wrote a non-diagnostic copy library, and specified a clinical-advisor sign-off gate. It demonstrates product judgment in a high-stakes, ethically loaded space where the right answer is often to build less.

Timeline: Weekend prototype, pending clinical-advisor review before any real-world use.

1. Problem statement

The people who love someone going through a hard time often want to help but do not know when they are needed. The person struggling frequently cannot, in the moment, compose a vulnerable "I need you" message to several people at once. The result is a gap: support exists in the network but does not reach the person, because the activation energy is too high exactly when capacity is lowest. Existing approaches fail on both ends. Generic mental-health apps lean on mood inference, behavioral surveillance, or clinical framing that many people find alienating or invasive. Group chats put the burden of explanation back on the person who is already depleted. The cost of the gap is isolation that compounds. Care Circle's hypothesis is that the missing piece is not detection or diagnosis, it is a low-effort, consent-based signal: a button.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Make it nearly effortless to tell a chosen circle how you are doing, at a chosen level of urgency (user outcome).
  • Keep the person in full control of who knows and when, with no surveillance and no automated escalation to services (user outcome, trust).
  • Make it easy for a friend to respond well, lowering the bar to a meaningful reply (user outcome).
  • Build something a clinician would sign off on, so it can be used responsibly in a sensitive space (business and ethical outcome).

Non-goals

  • Inferring mood or risk from social media or behavior. Permanently off-limits because surveillance violates the trust the product depends on.
  • Auto-dialing emergency services. Out of scope because the user must retain agency; the app routes to resources, it does not act for the user.
  • Group chat inside circles. Out of scope to avoid recreating the burden-of-explanation problem.
  • Gamification (streaks, badges). Out of scope because pressure is the opposite of what this user needs.
  • Public profiles, discovery, or friend recommendations. Out of scope; circles are private and self-chosen.

3. The product

The core mechanic is four signals plus a strict notification policy that always puts the user in control.

SignalWhat it doesWho gets notified
I'm okayStatus update, a quiet heartbeatNo one
Quiet dayAggregated into a gentle weekly digestCircle, as a digest
Could use a messageA nudge to the circleAll active members within 15 minutes
Not okaySurfaces resources first, then offers to notifyCircle, but only after the user taps "Notify my circle"

The "Not okay" surface is the heart of the safety design. Before any notification option appears, the screen leads with resources: text a friend now, one-tap call to "my person," a pre-filled crisis line (US 988 first, localized by country), and a two-minute "Sit with me" breathing exercise as the lowest-effort first option. Notifying the circle is a deliberate, separate action the user takes, never automatic. Friends respond through a pre-composed editable message ("Hey {name}, thinking of you. What's going on?"), and a quiet "I can't respond right now" link logs seen-but-not-responded without shame. If a signal goes unanswered, the app may ask the user whether to send a follow-up; it never sends an escalation automatically.

4. User stories

As someone having a hard day, I want to press one button instead of writing a vulnerable message to several people, so that I can ask for support when I have the least energy to.

As that same person on a "not okay" day, I want to see calming resources before I decide whether to tell anyone, so that I am met with help, not just a broadcast.

As a member of someone's circle, I want a pre-written message I can edit, so that I can respond warmly without freezing over what to say.

As a circle member who cannot help right now, I want to mark that quietly, so that the system knows without making me feel guilty.

As a privacy-conscious user, I want a transparency page reachable from sign-up and a one-tap full data deletion, so that I trust the app with something this personal.

Edge/empty state. As someone who has signaled "not okay" and no one has responded yet, I want honest, non-alarming copy ("No one's reached out yet. {N} of your circle have seen this.") rather than silence or a panic banner.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Phone OTP auth, display name, and photo. Given a valid phone number, when a user verifies, then an account is created.
  • Circle setup by phone with consent. Given a user adds three to eight people, when each is invited, then they get an SMS to accept or decline and status is tracked.
  • Four signal buttons with persisted status. Given a user taps a signal, then the correct notification policy fires (okay notifies no one; could-use notifies within 15 minutes; not-okay notifies only after explicit confirmation).
  • The "Not okay" safety rail. Given a user opens the not-okay surface, then resources appear before any notify option, and "Notify my circle" is a separate deliberate tap.
  • Friend response surface with a pre-composed editor and a no-shame "can't respond" link.
  • No automatic escalation: after six hours, offer an optional follow-up for the user to confirm, never notify a second person without that action.
  • Transparency page reachable from sign-up and irreversible account and data deletion.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Self-reflection thresholds: the user sets personal triggers (quiet days in a row, missed routine) that prompt only the user, never the circle.
  • "My person" one-tap crisis call configuration.
  • Circle health view (responsive vs dormant members).

Future (P2)

  • Localized crisis resources beyond the US.
  • Optional check-in scheduling.
  • Caregiver or family-mode patterns, only with the same consent guarantees.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Resources before notification. Rejected alternative: immediately broadcast "not okay." Cost: an extra deliberate step; user agency wins.

Never infer mental state. Rejected alternative: passive surveillance. Cost: the app cannot detect an unreported crisis; it is not an emergency service.

Non-diagnostic copy, clinical review before use. Rejected alternative: imply diagnosis. Cost: review delays broad use.

Invite-and-accept circles. Rejected alternative: auto-add contacts. Cost: slower setup, accepted for both sides' consent.

No automatic escalation. Rejected alternative: a second push after six hours. Cost: an unanswered signal can stay unanswered; the user must confirm any follow-up.

7. UX

The aesthetic is quiet, warm, and deliberately un-clinical: cream background, a Cormorant serif for headlines, generous spacing, and signal colors that are calm rather than emergency-red. No alarm icons, no exclamation marks, no "Are you okay???" copy. The visual restraint is itself a safety and trust decision.

Core screens:

  • A three-screen promise centered on consent and privacy ("You choose who. You choose when.").
  • Add three to eight people by phone, each with pending/active/declined status.
  • A warm greeting and four large, color-banded signal buttons with a recent check-ins list.
  • Resources at the top, "Notify my circle" as a distinct lower action, cancel always available.
  • The signal in context, a pre-composed editable reply, and a no-shame "can't respond" link.
  • A plain-language page on what the app does and does not do, reachable from sign-up.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Circle completion: target 50 percent of new users adding at least three accepted members. Measured in-app.
  • Signal usage: target 60 percent of active users sending a signal in their first week. Measured via the signals table.
  • Response rate: target 70 percent of "could use" and "not okay" signals receiving a friend response within the hour.

Lagging indicators

  • Felt-support outcome: target 4 of 5 on a single "I felt less alone" rating after a responded signal. Measured by an optional one-tap follow-up.
  • Retention of healthy circles: target 40 percent of circles still active at 30 days.
  • Safety: zero incidents of unwanted automated escalation. Measured by design (the path does not exist).

All figures are targets, not observed; this is a prototype and has not been validated, and would not be put in front of real users before clinical sign-off.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Three honest reflections. First, the six-hour follow-up interval is a guess; I would test a user-confirmed reminder rather than an automatic notification, preserving the no-escalation promise. Second, the product asks friends to carry real emotional weight, and I underspecified the friend's experience of burnout or being repeatedly tapped; I would add gentle load-balancing and a way for a member to set their own capacity without abandoning the circle. Third, the entire safety posture depends on a clinical advisor who is named in the spec but not yet engaged; until that review happens, the app is a careful prototype, not a tool I would ship, and I am treating that gate as non-negotiable rather than a nice-to-have.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • What is the humane escalation interval for "not okay," and should it be configurable? (clinical, user)
  • How do we protect circle members from over-reliance without shaming the person reaching out? (clinical, design)
  • What jurisdictions and crisis-line localizations are required before launch in each region? (legal, clinical)
  • Is phone OTP plus contacts the right trust model, or does it create friend-spam risk? (eng, user)

v2 scope: self-reflection thresholds, localized resources, and configurable escalation, all behind the same consent guarantees.

Market positioning: unlike mood-tracking or clinical apps, Care Circle makes no diagnosis and runs no surveillance; its wedge is consent and restraint. The defensibility is trust: a product people are willing to use for something this personal precisely because of what it refuses to do.

GTM hypothesis: grow through the circles themselves. Every invited member experiences the product from the friend side, which is the most credible possible introduction, and seeds the next circle.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: locating the real gap as activation energy and consent, not detection.
  • Trade-off reasoning: choosing user agency over automated "safety," and owning the genuine risk that creates.
  • Ethical product judgment: a clear "what NOT to build" and a clinical sign-off gate treated as a hard requirement.
  • Technical fluency: a complete data model, a precise notification policy, and an escalation rule specified end to end.
  • Validation discipline: explicit target metrics, a refusal to ship before clinical review, and honest interval uncertainty.
  • Communication: a spec that reads as carefully as the subject deserves, with a copy library to match.

Validation and next test

The live screens are onboarding promise, circle setup, signals, resources, friend reply and privacy. The next test is a two-week pilot with one small circle of friends, tracking check-ins sent and replies received.

Product requirements

15 Minutes to Love

A compatibility-first dating app that replaces endless swiping with one real fifteen-minute conversation.

Live ↗
DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Dating apps reward endless browsing instead of real conversations.
North star
10 of the first 100 users land a real date (target)

Related: Vibe Check builds on the same 15-minute call and adds an AI interview, a curated feed and private Keep or Toss.

15 Minutes to Love

A compatibility-first dating app that replaces endless swiping with one real fifteen-minute conversation.

The 30-second version. 15 Minutes to Love is a dating app built on what relationship science actually shows predicts long-term success: attachment style, shared values, communication style, and intent, not photos. Users answer ten conversational questions, see up to five deeply-vetted matches a day, and meet on a hard fifteen-minute video call. Only after both people privately say yes are phone numbers revealed. I built it solo with AI-assisted development as a deliberately capped 100-user closed beta, designed an eight-screen flow end to end, and specified a transparent rules-based compatibility engine. The live link includes a nine-step guided tour of the whole flow, no account needed. It demonstrates constraint-as-strategy thinking, a defensible cold-start plan, and a product where every limitation is the feature.

Timeline: Weekend prototype, in closed-beta design for a 100-user cohort.

1. Problem statement

Dating app fatigue is structural, not personal. The dominant apps optimize for endless swiping and open-ended text threads, which produce thin signal, slow time-to-meeting, and a steady drip of matches that go nowhere. Users burn weeks texting before they learn in five minutes of conversation that there is no spark. The science points the other way: the strongest predictors of relationship longevity are attachment style, shared values, communication and conflict style, and aligned intent, not the things a photo grid surfaces. The cost of the status quo is real time and real morale, paid by people who are genuinely trying to find a partner. Existing solutions fail because their business model rewards engagement (more swipes, more sessions) rather than conversion to a real relationship.

ArchetypeWhat it optimizesWhere it fails the user
Swipe apps (Tinder, Hinge)Volume of matches, session timeThin signal, long text limbo, ghosting
Slow-dating apps (a question a day)PacingStill text-first, still slow to a real conversation
Matchmaking servicesHuman-vetted fitExpensive, low volume, not self-serve
15 Minutes to LoveConversion to a real date(the thesis under test)

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Help a user have one real conversation with someone genuinely compatible, fast (user outcome).
  • Replace text limbo and ghosting with a forced, humane resolution after every call (user outcome).
  • Prove that compatibility-first matching converts to real dates on a small vetted cohort before scaling (business outcome).
  • Build a defensible cold-start story: a closed, invite-only community, not "yet another dating app" (business outcome).

Non-goals

  • In-app chat or messaging. Out of scope because the whole thesis is to skip text and meet on video.
  • Infinite swiping or large daily decks. Out of scope because volume is the problem, not the solution.
  • Monetization, premium tiers, or boosts. Out of scope for a closed MVP focused on validating the core loop.
  • Scaling infrastructure (sharding, queues, horizontal scale). Out of scope because 100 users is a feature, not a limit.
  • Advanced filters beyond chat-captured dealbreakers. Out of scope to keep the compatibility model the differentiator.

3. The product

The core loop is five steps, each designed to raise signal and cut wasted time.

  1. Onboarding chat. An AI interviewer asks ten research-anchored questions covering intent, attachment (two signals), conflict style, top values, kids stance, love languages, pace, dealbreakers, and a one-line "what matters most." The tone is warm and conversational; the underlying capture is structured.
  2. The daily five. A nightly job scores every other active user, applies hard filters, ranks the rest, and surfaces up to five new matches, each with a compatibility percentage and a one-sentence reason.
  3. Mutual like. If both like, a calm celebration screen routes straight to scheduling. A cap of three outstanding mutual matches prevents the swipe-and-never-meet pattern.
  4. The fifteen. A scheduled in-app video call with a visible hard countdown, a three-minute warning, an auto-end at zero, and a single optional five-minute extension if both tap for it. No recording.
  5. The decision. Each person privately answers "Do you want a real date with this person?" Both yes reveals phone numbers and a first note. Either no, or a non-response inside 24 hours, ends the match permanently. No ghosting, no limbo.

The compatibility score is transparent and rules-based, not a black box. Hard filters (age, distance, intent, kids stance, dealbreakers) gate eligibility; soft scoring out of 100 weights attachment compatibility most heavily (30 points), then shared values (25), communication and conflict style (20), pace (10), love-language overlap (10), and life stage (5).

4. User stories

As a dater tired of swiping, I want to answer questions about how I actually relate, so that I am matched on fit rather than photos.

As a busy professional, I want a fixed fifteen-minute video instead of a week of texting, so that I learn fast whether there is a spark.

As someone who has been ghosted, I want a forced private decision after the call, so that I always get resolution and never sit in limbo.

As a privacy-conscious user, I want my phone number revealed only after we both commit, so that I control my exposure.

As the founder/admin, I want invite-code gating and a dashboard of users, matches, and call stats, so that I can run a controlled beta and intervene when needed.

Edge/empty state. As a brand-new user with no matches yet, I want a clear "we are finding your five" empty state rather than a blank feed, so that I trust the system is working. As a user who hits three outstanding mutual matches, I want matching to pause with an explanation, so that I act before accumulating more.

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Invite-code gating and auth. Given a valid single-use 6-character code tied to an email, when a user signs up, then they gain access; codes cannot be reused.
  • AI chat onboarding capturing all ten structured dimensions. Given a user completes the chat, then a complete compatibility profile is stored.
  • Compatibility engine with hard filters plus 100-point soft scoring. Given two eligible users, when matches generate, then a transparent score and a cached one-sentence reason are produced.
  • Nightly daily-five generation with symmetric exposure and the three-outstanding cap.
  • Match card, like/pass, and mutual-match detection.
  • Scheduling against both users' availability windows with confirmation and reminders.
  • Fifteen-minute video with hard timer, three-minute warning, auto-end, optional single extension, no recording.
  • Post-call private decision with the both-yes reveal and the either-no permanent removal, including the 24-hour soft-no rule.
  • Admin dashboard: user list, match stats, pause/remove, report routing.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Push and SMS reminders (vs in-app only).
  • AMH of dating: richer "why you matched" explanations.
  • Reschedule and propose-alternative flow.

Future (P2)

  • Opening beyond the 100-user cohort with pool-health safeguards.
  • Localized crisis and safety resources.
  • Lightweight post-date feedback to tune the algorithm.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Cap the cohort at 100 users. Alternatives considered: open sign-up (rejected, recreates the thin-pool and trust problems of incumbents); waitlist with slow rollout (rejected, still optimizes for growth over learning). Why this won: a small pool lets the algorithm do real work, makes every user a case study, keeps architecture boring, and gives a clean investor narrative. The constraint is the design.

No in-app chat at all. Alternatives considered: optional pre-call messaging (rejected, reintroduces the text limbo we are trying to kill); icebreaker prompts (rejected, same drift). Why this won: forcing the conversation onto a timed video is the entire thesis. Hard trade-off: some users will want to text first and may churn at the friction. I accepted that, because softening here would dissolve the product into another swipe app.

A transparent rules-based score, not ML. Alternatives considered: embeddings or a learned model (rejected, no data at MVP, and a black box undermines trust); pure human matchmaking (rejected, not self-serve). Why this won: a documented points system is explainable to users and investors, debuggable by me, and grounded in published research (attachment weighted highest). It can be replaced with ML later once there is outcome data.

Forced post-call resolution within 24 hours. Alternatives considered: open-ended decision (rejected, recreates ghosting); mutual reveal by default (rejected, removes consent). Why this won: a private, time-boxed yes/no eliminates limbo while protecting the person who says no. Unflattering trade-off: a hard "no, and you disappear" can feel abrupt; I chose clarity over a softer but more ambiguous experience.

Hard timer with a single capped extension. Alternatives considered: no timer (rejected, defeats the fifteen-minute frame); unlimited extensions (rejected, recreates the limbo). Why this won: the timer is the product; one mutual extension acknowledges that real conversations sometimes need a beat more without letting calls sprawl.

7. UX

The visual language is warm and editorial rather than neon-app: cream paper, a Fraunces serif italic for display type, muted gold and rose accents, and calm motion. The intent is for the product to feel like a considered choice, not a slot machine.

Core screens, drawn from the eight-screen wireframe:

  • Logo, a one-line pitch ("Skip the small talk. Find someone actually compatible."), and four numbered principles that set expectations up front.
  • A conversational thread with a progress counter (4/10), tappable options, and warm validations.
  • One photo, a compatibility badge, substance-first fields, plus "What matters to them" and "Why you're a match" callouts.
  • A calm celebration with an immediate "Schedule your fifteen" CTA.
  • Day picker and time slots drawn from the intersection of both users' availability.
  • Live video with a live countdown pill and the three-minute wrap-up toast.
  • A private yes/no with micro-copy explaining the reveal rule.
  • Name, number, and a first note, with the next step explicitly moved off-app.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Onboarding completion: target 80 percent of invited users finishing all ten questions. Measured in-app.
  • Activation: target 40 percent of users reaching their first mutual match within seven days.
  • Call completion: target 70 percent of scheduled calls actually held. Measured against scheduled_calls.

Lagging indicators

  • Conversion to a real date: target the North Star, ten or more of the 100 users landing a real date they would not otherwise have found. Measured via contact reveals plus a short follow-up.
  • Decision resolution rate: target 90 percent of calls resolved inside 24 hours (vs the ghosting baseline of incumbents).
  • Satisfaction: target an NPS or single-question rating of 4 of 5 post-call.

All figures are targets, not observed; this is a prototype in closed-beta design and no usage has been validated yet.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Three honest tensions. First, the hard timer plus no-chat rule is high-friction by design and will likely cost me users who want to warm up by text; I would A/B a single pre-call prompt to test how much friction is too much. Second, the rules-based score is defensible but untested against outcomes; with no data, my weights are research-informed guesses, and I would instrument every match-to-date outcome from day one to learn whether attachment really should carry 30 points here. Third, the 100-user cap that makes the algorithm work also makes the daily-five fragile when the pool is small or skewed; I underspecified what happens when a user has zero eligible matches for several days, and I would design that empty state and a graceful "pool is thin this week" message before launch.

9. Open questions and roadmap

  • How abrupt is too abrupt for the "no" experience? (design, user)
  • What is the right pool size and gender or preference balance to keep the daily five healthy? (data)
  • Do we need identity verification or video safety tooling before opening up? (legal, eng)
  • What is the minimum cohort that still produces enough mutual matches to feel alive? (data, user)

v2 scope: richer match explanations, reschedule flows, push/SMS reminders, and a careful expansion path with pool-health safeguards.

Market positioning: the wedge is conversion to a real relationship, not engagement. The defensibility is a vetted closed community plus a compatibility model grounded in research, paired with a ritual (the fifteen) that incumbents cannot copy without abandoning their ad-driven engagement model.

GTM hypothesis: seed a single trusted community (for example an alumni or professional network) with invite codes, prove the ten-dates thesis, then expand community by community rather than via paid acquisition.

10. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: naming dating fatigue as a structural incentive problem, not a UX one, and designing against the incentive.
  • Trade-off reasoning: choosing friction (no chat, hard timer, forced resolution) on purpose, and owning who it costs.
  • Technical fluency: a complete data model, a transparent scoring algorithm, a nightly match job, and a real video stack specified end to end.
  • Validation discipline: a falsifiable North Star (ten real dates from 100 users) and explicit targets rather than invented metrics.
  • Roadmap sense: a clear path from a deliberately constrained MVP to a community-by-community expansion.
  • Communication: a wireframe-grounded spec that scales from a 30-second skim to a full read.

Naming and evidence

Product name: Fifteen; working title: 15 Minutes to Love. The guided tour is a demo, not an active dating cohort.

Product requirements

DealFlow

A dealflow OS to find and buy small companies.

Live ↗
DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
Problem
Small-business brokers run deals out of spreadsheets and lose time matching buyers.
North star
Median under 30 minutes from deal listed to first intro (target)

DealFlow

A deal OS for solo and small SMB brokers that turns scattered buyer lists into scored intros and a pipeline that moves to close.

The 30-second version. DealFlow helps small business brokers find the right buyer for a deal and move it through diligence to close in one workflow. The core is a rules-based matching engine that scores every buyer against every deal on industry, size, geography, prior closings, speed, and response rate, then surfaces the top 10 with a one-click intro. It is built desktop-first for the broker who is the whole firm: a Kanban pipeline, a CSV buyer importer, intro tracking with open and reply status, and an insights view that benchmarks the broker against the firm average. Subscription priced at $299 per seat per month.

Timeline: Solo build prototype, in testing.

Live demo: dealflowconnect.lovable.app (read-only broker dashboard with sample data). Operational requirements below are specified scope, not proof that a read-only demo sends intros or persists edits.

1. Problem statement

A solo or small SMB broker runs a real business out of a spreadsheet. Deals live in one file, buyers in another, and the matching between them lives in the broker's head. The work that actually closes deals (knowing which of 200 buyers fits this specific manufacturing business in Ohio at this EBITDA band) is invisible, slow, and impossible to scale past the broker's memory. When a broker is the entire firm, every hour spent re-deriving who to call is an hour not spent closing.

Existing tools fail this user in opposite directions. A general CRM (HubSpot, Salesforce) is built for high-volume sales motions, not the low-volume, high-stakes, relationship-heavy world of buying companies, and it does nothing to match buyers to deals. A VDR or data room handles documents but not the pipeline or the intro. Brokers end up stitching together a CRM, a spreadsheet, and an email client, with the most valuable step (the scored intro) done entirely by gut. DealFlow puts the deal, the buyer list, the scoring, the intro, and the pipeline in one product, so the broker's judgment is amplified rather than re-typed.

2. Goals and non-goals

Goals

  • Cut the time from a new deal to a sent intro from hours of manual cross-referencing to minutes.
  • Surface the buyers most likely to close, so the broker spends outreach on the top of a ranked list, not the whole list.
  • Give the broker a single pipeline view that always reflects deal reality, from listing to close or dead.
  • Make broker performance legible (intro-to-LOI conversion, time to first intro) so the broker can improve, not just operate.

Non-goals

  • Not a public marketplace or listing site. DealFlow is a private broker tool, not a place buyers browse, to keep deal confidentiality intact.
  • Not a buyer-side workflow product. Phase 2, because the wedge is the broker.
  • Not a data room. We integrate with VDRs rather than build one, since that is a solved and regulated space.
  • Not deal closing or escrow. Out of scope; DealFlow gets a deal to the table, it does not move money.
  • Not AI-generated outreach at MVP. Deterministic templates only, because a broker's voice and accuracy matter more than novelty.

3. The product

The broker works left to right across six surfaces: Pipeline, Deals, Buyers, Intros, Insights, Settings.

StepWhat the broker doesWhat DealFlow does
1. Add a dealEnters codename, industry, EBITDA, geography, risk tags; uploads a teaserStores the deal, RLS-scoped to the firm
2. Compute matchesClicks "Compute matches"Scores every buyer 0 to 100 and ranks the top 10
3. Send an introPicks a buyer, edits a template, sendsDelivers via Resend/SendGrid, starts tracking
4. TrackWatches status updateRecords opens, replies, and stage automatically
5. Move the pipelineDrags the deal card across stagesPersists stage: Listing to Outreach to Engaged to Diligence to LOI to Closed or Dead
6. LearnOpens InsightsBenchmarks the broker vs the firm average

The match score is a transparent weighted formula: 30% industry fit, 20% size fit, 15% geography fit, 15% prior-closing match, 10% normalized speed score, 10% normalized response rate. Each component is 0 to 100, cached per deal-buyer pair, and recomputed only when an attribute changes.

4. User stories

Solo / small broker

  • As a broker who is the whole firm, I want every buyer scored against a new deal automatically, so that I start outreach with a ranked top 10 instead of re-reading my whole list.
  • As a broker, I want to send an intro from a template and have its opens and replies tracked, so that I know who is engaged without living in my inbox.
  • As a broker, I want a Kanban pipeline that persists when I drag a deal, so that my pipeline always reflects reality at a glance.
  • As a broker importing my book, I want to bulk-upload 100 buyers from a CSV in under a minute, so that I can start matching the day I sign up.

Broker measuring performance

  • As a broker, I want to see my intro-to-LOI conversion against the firm average, so that I can tell whether my matching is actually working.

Edge and multi-tenant states

  • As a broker, I want a clear empty state when I have no deals or buyers, so that I know exactly what to do first.
  • As one of two brokers in the same firm, I want to see only my own deals and buyers, so that confidential pipelines stay separate (RLS-enforced).

5. Requirements

Must-have (P0)

  • Deal CRUD with teaser upload. Given a broker, when they create a deal and upload a teaser, then it persists and is RLS-scoped to their firm.
  • Buyer model with bulk CSV import. Given a 100-row CSV, when imported, then all buyers load in under 60 seconds.
  • Rules-based scoring engine. Given a new deal, when matches are computed, then the top 10 scored buyers appear in under 3 seconds.
  • Intro flow with send and status tracking. Given a chosen deal and buyer, when the broker sends an intro, then the email lands and a buyer reply updates the status automatically.
  • Kanban pipeline with persistent drag-drop. Given a deal card, when dragged to a new stage, then the stage persists on reload.
  • RLS isolation. Given two brokers in one firm, when each logs in, then each sees only their own deals and buyers.

Nice-to-have (P1)

  • Insights view: intro-to-LOI conversion vs firm average, median time to first intro, response-rate cohorts.
  • Buyer intent signals log.
  • Stripe subscription at $299 per seat per month.

Future (P2)

  • A model trained on outcome data (intro to LOI to closed) to replace the deterministic rules.
  • Team members and firm-level roles.
  • VDR integrations and cross-firm buyer sharing with explicit consent.

6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

Decision: Rules-based scoring at v1, an ML model only later.

Alternatives considered: an ML matching model from day one (rejected, there is no outcome data to train on yet, and a black box the broker cannot trust is worse than a transparent formula). Why this won: a broker will only act on a score they understand. A weighted, inspectable formula (with visible score components) earns trust and produces the very outcome data a future model would need. This is the disciplined sequencing, not the flashy one.

Decision: Broker-side only at MVP, no buyer-side tooling.

Alternatives considered: build both sides at once (rejected, doubles scope and dilutes the wedge). Why this won: the broker is the buyer of the software and the daily user. Nail the side that pays first.

Decision: Deterministic templates, no AI-generated outreach at MVP.

This is the unflattering trade-off, because AI copy is the obvious demo. Rejected anyway: an intro that misstates a deal detail or sounds off in the broker's voice can cost a relationship that took years to build. In a low-volume, high-trust market, accuracy and the broker's own voice beat generated polish. AI copy stays a later option, not a launch feature.

Decision: Integrate VDRs, do not build a data room.

Alternatives considered: build a native data room (rejected, it is a mature, security-sensitive, regulated category). Why this won: DealFlow's value is the pipeline and the match, not document hosting. Building a VDR would burn the roadmap on a solved problem.

Decision: Recompute scores only on attribute change, with per-pair caching.

Alternatives considered: recompute on every page load (rejected, wasteful and slow at scale). Why this won: scores only change when a deal or buyer attribute changes, so caching per pair keeps the top-10 fast (under 3 seconds) without redundant work.

7. UX

Desktop-first navigation keeps Pipeline, Deals, Buyers, Intros, Insights and Settings within reach. The Kanban makes deal progression scannable. Scored match cards expose the components of each score instead of asking the broker to trust a black box. The voice is terse and finance-oriented, with numbers and actions ahead of promotional language.

8. Success metrics and validation

Leading indicators

  • Time to first intro. Target median under 30 minutes from deal creation to first sent intro, measured from deal listed_at to first intros.sent_at.
  • Match panel action rate. Target 40% of computed top-10 matches that result in a sent intro.
  • CSV import completion. Target 90% of new brokers importing a buyer list in their first session.

Lagging indicators

  • Intro-to-LOI conversion. Target 15%, benchmarked broker vs firm average in Insights.
  • Pipeline velocity. Target reduction in median days-per-stage versus the broker's pre-DealFlow baseline (target: 20% shorter; not observed).
  • Subscription retention. Target 90% monthly retention at $299 per seat.

What didn't work, and what I'd change. Every metric here is a target, not an observed result; this is a prototype in testing and the matching engine has not yet been validated against real closed-deal outcomes. The honest weakness of a rules-based score is that the six weights (30/20/15/15/10/10) are reasoned, not learned, so they are almost certainly miscalibrated until real outcome data corrects them. That is by design (you cannot train a model with no data), but it means the score's job at v1 is to be transparent and directionally useful, not precise. The single biggest validation risk is reply-status parsing: automatic status updates depend on reliably matching buyer replies to intros, and that is the piece I would harden and instrument first, because a pipeline that silently goes stale is worse than no pipeline.

9. What this project demonstrates

  • Problem identification: a real, underserved user (the broker who is the whole firm) and a specific high-value task (the scored intro) that no incumbent owns.
  • Trade-off reasoning: transparent rules over an untrained black box, broker-side over both sides, deterministic templates over AI copy in a trust-critical market, integrate over build.
  • Technical fluency: a weighted scoring engine with per-pair caching, RLS multi-tenancy, CSV import at scale, and email tracking, scoped with concrete performance acceptance criteria.
  • Validation discipline: weights and metrics framed honestly as reasoned-not-learned and as targets, with the reply-parsing risk called out as the thing to instrument first.
  • Roadmap sense: a clear path from rules to an outcome-trained model, exactly when the data to train it exists.
  • Communication: a terse, professional product voice matched to a finance-adjacent user, no filler, no emoji.

Validation and next test

Next: test the stage list in section 3 with two brokers against the live demo.

a fixed taxonomy over an algorithmic feed for legibility and defensibility.
  • Technical fluency: per-user persistence on Supabase with row-level security, a clean change set, and correct shareability metadata.
  • Validation discipline: honest that the matching thesis is not yet measured, with a concrete instrumentation plan to prove or kill it.
  • Roadmap sense: a credible path from a useful deck to a differentiated matching product.
  • Communication: a spec that knows what not to touch is as valuable as one that says what to build.

    • Product requirements

    Closer (Kindred Spirits)

    Conversation decks for every relationship: eight levels from strangers to family, played in person, across distance, or alone.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Relationships drift into logistics and small talk.
    North star
    2 deck sessions per week per active pair (target)

    Version history: V1 was Kindred Spirits, a deck that read compatibility from how people answer. V2, Closer, widens the same deck to every relationship, from strangers to family. Both live in the same app.

    Closer (Kindred Spirits)

    Conversation decks for every relationship: eight levels, from strangers to family, played in person, across distance, or alone.

    The 30-second version. Most people want deeper conversations but do not know how to start them, and generic question lists feel awkward. Closer is a set of conversation decks organized into eight relationship levels, from strangers to family. People play in person by passing the phone, across distance by sending one question at a time, or alone as a journal. Therapist review of the deeper levels is planned. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://closer-decks.lovable.app

    Problem

    Relationships drift into logistics and small talk. Good questions exist, but they are rarely matched to how close two people actually are, so they feel either too shallow or too intrusive.

    Who it is for

    Friends, couples, families, and people reconnecting at a distance who want a gentle structure for real conversation.

    How it works

    StepWhat happens
    Pick a levelEight levels, from strangers to family; start where you are
    Take turnsOne card at a time: pass the phone, send a link, or sit with it alone
    Skip without judgmentPass on any question and it will not show again
    Keep what mattersSave favorites and keep a private journal

    Requirements (live)

    • Eight relationship levels, from strangers to family, each with its own deck.
    • Three ways to play: in person, across distance one question at a time, or alone as a journal.
    • Card counts shown per level so people can pick a session length.

    Key decisions

    • Match depth to closeness. Levels prevent questions that feel too much, too soon.
    • Three ways to play. In person, async across distance, and solo journaling cover how people actually connect.
    • Expert input on the deep end. A qualified therapist reviewing the deepest prompts is next scope.
    • Skipping is safe. No pressure keeps people coming back.

    Non-goals

    • Not therapy or couples counseling.
    • No scores, streaks or compatibility ratings.
    • No public sharing of answers.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 2 sessions per week per active pair, 8 cards answered per session, 60% of async questions get a reply within 48 hours, and 35% return within two weeks.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    V1 promised to match people on behavior, but the build surfaces compatibility only through which questions resonate, so that claim stayed a design intention. V2 dropped the dating promise and kept what worked: the leveled deck. If I rebuilt, I would capture light signals (save rate by level, which questions a pair both favorite) before claiming any compatibility read.

    What I would do next

    Shared progress for pairs, seasonal decks, and a gift link that starts someone on the right level.

    Content inventory

    Eight levels: Strangers, Acquaintances, Friends, Dating, Partners, Engaged, Married and Family. Proposed authoring rules: Strangers uses low-stakes curiosity; Acquaintances shared context; Friends reciprocal stories; Dating values and boundaries; Partners needs and conflict; Engaged future commitments; Married changing priorities; Family shared history with an opt-out. Deeper prompts require specialist review rather than an unsupported therapist endorsement.

    Validation and next test

    Next: play one level with a partner and track which cards get skipped most.

    Risks

    • Deeper questions can surface painful topics. Mitigation: skipping is always safe, family decks have an opt-out, and specialist review of the deepest prompts is planned.

    Trade-off

    Eight fixed levels instead of open-ended questions. Rejected alternative: one big shuffled deck. Cost: more content to write, accepted so questions match how close two people are.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Designed one content system for three very different contexts of use.
    • Set a safety boundary (therapist review) before going deeper, instead of after.

    Product requirements

    Green Flag

    A private dating counsel that reads a conversation and gives an honest read on intent and safety. Nothing stored.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    Early dating means decoding ambiguous messages alone.
    North star
    70% of uploads lead to a completed read (target)

    Green Flag (The Sit)

    A private dating counsel that reads the conversation, not the person

    The 30-second version. Green Flag, presented in the product as "The Sit," is a private tool that reads screenshots of a dating conversation and returns an honest "read" on emotional intelligence, intent, and safety. The user picks what the conversation is for (vetting a real partner, or exploring pleasure), and the read is tuned to that goal. Designed as an editorial, privacy-first concept with a no-storage requirement, not a verified live app. The PM work spans a sharp problem frame (decision support at the moment of doubt), a two-mode product model, a privacy-by-design stance, and a defensible content-quality roadmap.

    Timeline: Concept, unpublished.

    1. Problem statement

    People in the early stage of dating spend enormous emotional energy interpreting ambiguous messages, and they do it alone or by forwarding screenshots to friends who are biased, busy, or unqualified. The cost is real: wasted months on someone who was never building toward anything, or dismissing a good partner over a misread text. The two dominant alternatives both fail. Friends give warm but inconsistent advice colored by their own history. Generic AI chatbots give confident, sycophantic answers that flatter the user rather than tell the truth. The gap is a tool that reads a real conversation, holds a clear point of view, and stays anchored to what the user actually wants rather than what the other person needs from them.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Give the user a clear, honest read on a specific conversation in under a minute.
    • Tune the read to the user's stated goal, because "is he a real contender" and "is this safe and fun" are different questions.
    • Keep the user anchored to their own wants (the product's "North Star"), not the other person's.
    • Earn trust through a privacy-first stance: nothing is stored.

    Non-goals

    • Not a matchmaking or swiping app. It does not find people; it reads conversations the user already has.
    • Not a therapist or a crisis service. It is decision support, with clear handoffs when safety is at risk.
    • Not a manipulation coach. It will not write lines to extract a result from someone. The product reads, it does not script.
    • Not a long-term relationship tracker in v1. One conversation, one read.

    3. The product

    The user lands on a calm, editorial interface ("The Sit, Vol. I"). The flow is deliberately short:

    StepWhat happensWhy
    North StarA standing reminder of what the user wants from dating (maturity, stability, pleasure, agency).Anchors every read to the user's goals, not the other person's.
    Pick the lens"A real contender" (vetting for partnership) or "For pleasure" (exploration, chemistry).The same message means different things depending on intent.
    Upload the conversationDrop screenshots (PNG or JPG, multiple welcome).Reads the real exchange, not a paraphrase.
    Optional context"Anything I should know?"Lets the user add what a screenshot cannot show.
    The readA careful interpretation tuned to the chosen lens.The product's core value.

    The contender lens reads for emotional intelligence, intent, and whether he is actually building toward something. The pleasure lens reads for safety, agency, and whether the dynamic keeps the user in her body. Nothing is stored, and each session ends with an invitation to start a new read.

    4. User stories

    • As a woman vetting a new match, I want an honest read on whether he is serious so that I do not invest months in someone who is not building toward partnership.
    • As someone exploring casually, I want a read focused on safety and agency so that I can enjoy chemistry without losing myself in it.
    • As a privacy-conscious user, I want assurance that my screenshots are not stored so that I can share intimate conversations without risk.
    • As a user in a confusing exchange, I want to add context the screenshot cannot show so that the read accounts for history.
    • As a first-time user (empty state), I want the North Star framing before I upload anything so that the read is grounded in my goals from the start.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Two explicit lenses (contender, pleasure) that visibly change the read.
      • Given a user selects "For pleasure," when the read is generated, then it foregrounds safety and agency rather than long-term partnership signals.
    • Multi-image upload with on-screen privacy assurance.
      • Given a user uploads screenshots, when the session ends, then no image or text is persisted server-side.
    • A read that states what is and is not inferable from screenshots.
    • Safety handoff: if the read detects coercion, threats or abuse, it stops interpreting and shows a resources card.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Optional free-text context field feeding the read.
    • A short "what to watch for next" follow-up rather than only a verdict.

    Future (P2)

    • Saved, opt-in conversation history for users who want to track a single person over time.
    • Pattern view across multiple reads (recurring red and green flags for this user).

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision 1: Two lenses instead of one general read

    Choice: Force the user to declare intent (contender vs pleasure) before the read.

    Alternatives considered: A single all-purpose read (rejected: it averages two opposite goals into mush). Inferring intent from the conversation (rejected: the same words carry different weight depending on what the user wants, and guessing wrong erodes trust).

    Why this won: Intent is the single biggest input to a correct read. Making it explicit is cheap and dramatically improves relevance.

    Decision 2: Privacy by design, nothing stored

    Choice: No persistence in v1. Screenshots are read and discarded.

    Alternatives considered: Store conversations to enable history and personalization (rejected for v1: the content is among the most sensitive a user owns, and trust is the whole product).

    Why this won: For an intimate-content product, a credible "nothing is stored" promise is a feature, not a limitation. History becomes an opt-in P2, never a default.

    Decision 3: Editorial, non-clinical voice

    Choice: Present as a private counsel ("Yours, Juliana. Vol. I") rather than a scoring app.

    Alternatives considered: A red-flag checklist or a compatibility score (rejected: reduces a human situation to a number and invites gaming).

    Why this won: The tone signals discretion and judgment, which is what a user wants from counsel. It also differentiates sharply from gamified dating tools.

    7. UX

    The visual language is intentionally analog and quiet: paper textures, ochre and plum accents, generous whitespace. This is a deliberate counter to the bright, transactional aesthetic of dating apps. The product should feel like a letter, not a dashboard.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Read completion rate (uploaded a conversation and viewed the read): target 70%.
    • Lens split (share choosing pleasure vs contender): informs which read quality to invest in first.
    • Repeat reads per user in first week: target 2.

    Lagging indicators

    • Self-reported usefulness ("did this read match what happened?") at a 2-week follow-up: target 60%.
    • Word-of-mouth referrals, the natural growth channel for a discreet product.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change

    • A read is only as good as its judgment, and an over-confident wrong read is worse than no read. v1 needs a calibrated humility ("here is what I see, here is what I cannot see from a screenshot").
    • Screenshot quality varies wildly. The prototype assumes clean uploads; a real v1 needs OCR fallback and a graceful path when images are unreadable.
    • "Nothing is stored" must be provably true, not just stated. A real build needs the architecture to back the claim, or the trust collapses on first doubt.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Safety handoff is P0: stop interpreting and show resources for coercion, threats or abuse. Review localization and resource selection before launch.
    • Scope of judgment (user research): how directive should the read be? Users may want a verdict, but the product should not tell people who to date.
    • Monetization (business): one-time reads, a subscription, or a freemium model?

    Roadmap

    • v1: ship the two-lens read with provable no-storage and a safety handoff.
    • v1.5: optional context, OCR robustness, "what to watch for next."
    • v2: opt-in history and a personal pattern view, the first feature that benefits from (consented) memory.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification. Found a real, underserved moment (decision support at the point of doubt) that friends and generic chatbots both serve badly.
    • Trade-off reasoning. Chose explicit intent over inference, and trust over personalization, with stated rationale.
    • Principled design. Treated privacy as a core product promise, not a compliance afterthought.
    • Voice and positioning. Differentiated through an editorial tone in a category defined by transactional design.
    • Responsible scope. Built in safety handoffs and refused the manipulation use case.

    Naming and evidence

    Green Flag is the product name; "The Sit" names the editorial experience and "Yours, Juliana. Vol. I" names its voice.

    Product requirements

    Emotional Compass

    Helps you name and navigate your emotions, an atlas of 88 feelings, with a guided emotion sorter.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Under stress, people reach for vague labels that point to no next step.
    North star
    70% of check-ins end on a specific feeling word (target)

    Version history: V1 was Atlas of the Heart. Emotional Compass is V2.

    Emotional Compass

    A quiet check-in app that helps you name what you are feeling with precision, built on an atlas of 88 feelings.

    The 30-second version. Most people describe their inner life with three words: happy, sad, stressed. Research on emotional granularity, popularized by Brene Brown's Atlas of the Heart, shows that naming a feeling precisely makes it easier to move through. Emotional Compass turns that idea into a three-step check-in: find the region, narrow the word, mark the moment. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://emotionalcompass.lovable.app

    What changed from V1

    V1, Atlas of the Heart, opened with four ways in (I don't know, a guided sorter, start with words, story and body). V2 narrows that to one three-step check-in, adds a sample check-in for first visits, and drops the book title from the name.

    Problem

    People under stress default to vague labels, and a vague label points to no useful next step. Feeling "bad" could be shame, guilt, envy or grief, and each calls for something different. Journaling apps capture text but do not teach the vocabulary.

    Who it is for

    Reflective adults who want more self-knowledge, people in therapy who want language between sessions, and anyone who has read Atlas of the Heart and wants to practice it.

    How it works

    StepWhat the user does
    Find the regionPicks one of 13 broad chapters, such as places we go when we are hurting, when we compare, or when life is good
    Narrow the wordDistinguishes close pairs: guilt from shame, envy from jealousy, empathy from sympathy
    Mark the momentSaves a short check-in to a journal and watches their vocabulary grow

    An Atlas page lets users browse all 88 feelings with paraphrased definitions.

    Requirements (live)

    • A three-step check-in: find the region, narrow the word, mark the moment.
    • Browse the full atlas of feelings without checking in.
    • Weekly reflection is next on the roadmap.

    Key decisions

    • Teach by choosing, not reading. The check-in forces a choice between close words, which builds granularity faster than a glossary.

    • Paraphrase, do not copy. Definitions are paraphrased for educational reflection and credit the book.

    • Quiet by design. No streaks or scores; the product is a pause, not a game. Cost: lower habit pull. A gentle weekly reflection is the proposed replacement, not a live promise.

    Non-goals

    • Not therapy or a mental health diagnosis.
    • No mood scores, streaks or charts.
    • No social sharing of check-ins.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 3 check-ins per user per week, 70% of check-ins reach a specific word, and 35% return at 30 days.

    What I would do next

    Add a weekly reflection that shows which regions come up most, and test whether naming precision changes self-reported mood.

    Validation and next test

    Weekly reflection is next on the roadmap.

    Risks

    • Someone in real distress uses a check-in instead of getting help. Mitigation: a clear not-therapy line, and a link to crisis support next to every check-in (next).
    • Leaning too closely on a published book. Mitigation: definitions are paraphrased and the source is credited.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    V1 opened with four ways in, so the first thing a stressed person had to do was make a choice. V2 cut that to one three-step check-in. Without streaks, the reason to come back is the weekly reflection, which is not built yet; I would ship it before adding anything else.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Translated a research idea (emotional granularity) into a 30-second interaction.
    • Chose precision over volume: one specific word beats a mood score.

    Product requirements

    Recipe Health

    Forty heart-healthy, iron-rich recipes for two, shopping list first, then one recipe at a time, bilingual English and Spanish.

    Live ↗
    In real useSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    People get a health goal for their diet but no help turning it into real meals.
    North star
    55% of users who finish the shopping list start cooking (target)

    Recipe Health

    Turns a nutrition goal into a guided cook-along, so eating well becomes a thing you do, not a thing you read about.

    The 30-second version. Recipe Health is a guided recipe challenge that turns a specific health goal (here, an iron-rich, anti-inflammatory, heart-healthy eating plan for two) into a single flowing experience: choose recipes, check off their shopping list, then cook a deck of 40 recipes you cook one at a time, each under 30 minutes and each tagged with the nutrition reason it earns its place. The current build is a self-contained React prototype with a guided flow (choose, shop, cook, done), with an ES/EN toggle, per-item check state, and a progress bar. It demonstrates PM work in turning a clinical goal into a behavior-change loop, designing for adherence rather than information, and being honest about the safety boundary of nutrition guidance.

    Timeline: Live at https://recipe-health.lovable.app

    1. Problem statement

    People are told what to eat for a health goal (more iron, less inflammation, a kinder diet for the heart) and then left alone to translate that advice into a week of actual meals. The translation is the hard part: deciding what to buy, what to cook, in what order, and whether any given meal is even helping. Most nutrition content stops at the recipe and never closes the loop from goal to grocery list to a meal on the plate, so good intentions stall at the point of execution. The cost is real for anyone managing a condition through diet, where consistency matters more than any single perfect meal. Recipe Health starts from the hypothesis that adherence comes from a guided, low-decision flow, not from another PDF of recipes.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Convert a nutrition goal into action by sequencing the whole journey: shop, then cook, one recipe at a time.
    • Make every recipe legibly purposeful by attaching a short reason it serves the goal (for example, "lentils are an iron powerhouse; lemon adds vitamin C").
    • Lower the activation energy of healthy cooking: every recipe under 30 minutes, scaled for two, with a checkable shopping list so nothing is forgotten.
    • Build a structure that generalizes from this one challenge (Red and Iron) to other goals (low-sodium, anti-inflammatory, postpartum) without redesign.

    Non-goals

    • Calorie or macro tracking. Out of scope because the product is about adherence to a curated plan, not quantified logging.
    • Personalized meal generation by AI. Out of scope to keep the recipes human-curated and trustworthy for a health context.
    • Grocery delivery or commerce. Out of scope for the prototype; the value to prove first is the flow, not the transaction.
    • Medical claims. Explicitly out of scope; the app states it is general nutrition guidance, not medical advice.

    3. The product

    The app is a single guided flow: choose recipes, then shop, then cook, with an ES/EN toggle and a done state. The user moves through them in order, but can jump back to the list at any time.

    StateWhat it does
    ChooseSelect recipes in English or Spanish; frames the challenge (40 heart-healthy, iron-rich recipes, all under 30 minutes, for two), shows the goal chips, and surfaces a medical-advice disclaimer up front
    ShopA categorized shopping list (Produce, Proteins, Beans and Cans, Grains, Nuts and Seeds, Pantry, Dairy) with tap-to-check items, quantities, and a running "X of N gathered" count
    CookA recipe deck, one card at a time: number, meal badge, title, time, the nutrition reason, ingredients, and numbered method steps, with a progress bar and "Mark complete, next recipe"
    DoneA completion screen celebrating all 40 recipes, with options to start over or review the list

    The core mechanic is the cook loop: see one recipe, cook it, mark it complete, advance. The progress bar makes the 40-recipe challenge feel finite and winnable rather than overwhelming.

    4. User stories

    As someone managing my iron and heart health through diet, I want a single plan that tells me what to buy and what to cook next, so that I do not have to assemble a week of meals from scratch.

    As a cook with limited time, I want every recipe to be under 30 minutes and scaled for two, so that healthy eating fits a real weeknight.

    As someone who wants to understand my food, I want each recipe to say why it helps (iron, vitamin C pairing, anti-inflammatory), so that I learn the logic and can keep it up after the challenge ends.

    As a shopper, I want to check items off a categorized list and see my progress, so that I can shop in one trip without forgetting anything.

    As a user who finishes (completion state), I want a clear "done" moment and an easy way to start over, so that the challenge feels like an accomplishment and a habit I can repeat.

    As a user with a heart condition (safety edge case), I want a clear up-front note that this is general guidance and that I should confirm sodium, potassium, and vitamin K targets with my doctor, so that I am not misled into treating the app as medical advice.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Four-state guided flow (choose, shop, cook, done) with forward progression and a back-to-list escape hatch.
      • Given a user starts at intro, when they tap through, then they reach shop, then cook, then done, and can return to the list from any recipe.
    • Categorized, checkable shopping list with a gathered count.
      • Given the shop view, when a user taps an item, then it toggles checked and the "X of N gathered" count updates.
    • One-recipe-at-a-time cook deck with a per-recipe nutrition reason and numbered steps.
      • Given the cook view, when a user marks a recipe complete, then the next recipe loads and the progress bar advances.
