    • Up-front, non-dismissable medical-advice disclaimer.
      • Given the intro screen, when it renders, then the "general nutrition guidance, not medical advice" note is visible before the user begins.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Persistence of check state and recipe progress across sessions (currently in-memory only).
    • Adjustable serving scale (for one, for four).
    • A way to mark favorite recipes to repeat.

    Future (P2)

    • Multiple challenge templates (low-sodium, anti-inflammatory, postpartum recovery) on the same engine.
    • Export the shopping list or sync to a grocery service.
    • Light tracking of how the goal-relevant nutrients add up across the plan.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision: Sequence the whole journey (shop then cook) instead of presenting a recipe library.

    Alternatives considered: a searchable recipe collection (rejected because search puts the decision burden back on the user, which is exactly where adherence breaks); a daily meal-plan calendar (rejected as too rigid for how people actually cook). Why this won: a guided flow with a single next action is the lowest-friction path from goal to plate, which is the behavior the product exists to drive.

    Decision: One recipe at a time with a progress bar, not a grid.

    Alternatives considered: showing all 40 recipes at once (rejected because volume is intimidating and kills momentum). Why this won: a finite, advancing challenge reframes 40 recipes as a winnable journey, and the progress bar borrows the completion psychology that makes habit apps work, without resorting to streaks or guilt.

    Decision: Attach a one-line nutrition reason to every recipe.

    Alternatives considered: a separate "learn" section (rejected as something users skip). Why this won: embedding the reason in the recipe teaches the logic in context, so the user can keep eating this way after the challenge, which is the real outcome.

    Decision (the honest trade-off): Hard-coded content and in-memory state for the prototype.

    The current build embeds the shopping list and all 40 recipes as static data, and check or progress state lives only in component memory, so a refresh resets it. Alternatives considered: building persistence and a content backend first (rejected to validate the flow before investing in infrastructure). Why this won for now: the riskiest assumption is whether the guided flow drives adherence, not whether the data persists, so proving the experience came first. The cost is real, persistence is the most important P1, because a recovery or health plan that forgets your progress is not yet shippable to a real user.

    Decision: Lead with a safety disclaimer.

    Why this won: any product giving nutrition guidance for a health condition has an ethical and trust obligation to state its limits before the user acts. Putting it on the intro, not buried in a footer, is the responsible default.

    7. UX

    The visual language is warm and editorial: a paper-cream background with green, terracotta, and ochre accents, Fraunces serif display type against a Karla sans body, subtle grain, and staggered fade-in animation. It reads like a beautifully designed cookbook rather than a clinical app, which matters because the goal is to make healthy eating feel inviting, not prescriptive. The mobile-first 480px column and large tap targets (checklist rows, full-width primary buttons) keep it usable one-handed in a kitchen.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Shop-to-cook conversion: percentage of users who finish the shopping list and start cooking. Target: 55%. Measured by state-transition events.
    • Recipe completion rate: average recipes marked complete per user. Target: 8 recipes per user in the first two weeks. Measured by the cook progress counter.
    • Reason engagement: whether users who read the nutrition reason complete more recipes (a proxy for whether teaching drives adherence). Measured once instrumented.

    Lagging indicators

    • Challenge completion: percentage of starters who reach the done screen. Target: 20% (not observed).
    • Repeat: percentage who start a second challenge. Target: 20% (not observed).
    • Self-reported habit change: survey on whether users kept cooking this way after the challenge. Target: 20% (not observed).

    What didn't work, and what I'd change.

    The prototype proves the flow but not retention, because state is in-memory and resets on refresh, which makes a multi-day challenge effectively un-trackable for a real user. The first thing I would change is persistence (Supabase or local storage) so that progress survives, since a health plan that forgets where you are is the one thing this category cannot afford. I would also separate content from code so that new challenges (low-sodium, postpartum) can be authored without touching the component, which is the unlock for the product to be more than one challenge. Finally, the nutrition reasons are currently asserted, not sourced; for a health product I would want them reviewed against a credible nutrition reference before any public launch.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    • Should progress live on-device (private, no account) or in an account (cross-device, but adds friction and a privacy surface for health data)? (Owner: design + legal.)
    • What is the right review standard for nutrition claims before public launch? (Owner: legal + a nutrition reviewer.)
    • Does the challenge format generalize, or do some goals need a different structure (for example, a daily rather than sequential plan)? (Owner: user research.)

    v2 scope: persistence, multiple challenge templates on one engine, and adjustable serving scale.

    Market positioning:

    ProductModelGap Recipe Health fills
    Recipe blogs / PinterestLibrary, search-firstNo sequencing, no goal-to-plate flow
    Meal-kit services (HelloFresh)Delivery + recipesCommerce-led, not goal-led, costly
    MyFitnessPalTracking and loggingMeasures intake, does not guide cooking
    Recipe HealthGuided goal-driven cook-alongSequences shop to cook with the nutrition logic built in

    GTM hypothesis: launch one tightly themed challenge (this iron-and-heart plan) as a shareable, finishable artifact, gather adherence signal, then expand to a library of goal-specific challenges. It is defensible because the value is the curation and the sequencing, and because a femtech and health audience rewards trustworthy, purposeful design over generic recipe volume.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: located the real failure point of nutrition advice (the gap between knowing and doing) and built the flow that closes it.
    • Trade-off reasoning: chose a sequenced flow over a library, one-at-a-time over a grid, and validated the experience before investing in persistence, while being explicit about the cost.
    • Technical fluency: a clean four-state React flow with derived progress, checkable list state, and a clear path to persistence and a content backend.
    • Validation discipline: honest that retention is unproven, with persistence named as the gating P1 and nutrition claims flagged for review.
    • Roadmap sense: designed a single challenge as the seed of a generalizable challenge engine.
    • Communication: a spec that puts the safety boundary first and treats a health context with appropriate care.

    Validation and next test

    The next test is to record selected recipes, groceries gathered and meals completed over two weeks.

    Product requirements

    Quill

    Paste messy notes and get a polished follow-up or thank-you message in one of three voices, ready to send.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Thank-you notes and follow-ups are high-effort, so they get skipped.
    North star
    3 useful drafts copied per weekly active user (target)

    Quill

    Paste messy notes and get a polished follow-up or thank-you message in one of three voices, ready to send.

    Live: https://followupquill.lovable.app

    What is live: Paste box, follow-up and thank-you types, Warm, Professional and Playful voices, and copy. Sign-in is required today.

    Problem

    Writing follow-ups and thank-you notes is high-friction emotional labor. People know they should send them after interviews, meetings, intros, and gifts, but staring at a blank message and finding the right tone causes delay and avoidance. The notes either never get sent or come out stiff and generic. There is no fast tool that turns rough thoughts into a sendable, appropriately toned draft.

    Target users

    • Primary: Job seekers and early-career professionals who need to send interview follow-ups and networking thank-yous and want them to land well.
    • Secondary: Founders, salespeople, and consultants who send frequent relationship-building follow-ups.
    • Tertiary: Anyone facing a socially required note (gifts, favors, introductions) who freezes on tone.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Turn rough, unstructured notes into a polished draft in under 30 seconds.
    • Give users meaningful control over tone through clear voice options.
    • Produce drafts that feel personal and specific, not templated.

    Non-goals

    • We are not building a full email client or send-and-track CRM at launch.
    • We are not generating long-form documents or cold outreach sequences.
    • We are not fabricating facts the user did not provide.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • Paste-in box for messy notes, bullets, or context.
    • Output type selector: follow-up or thank-you.
    • Three selectable voices (for example Warm, Professional, Playful) that visibly change tone.
    • One-tap copy of the generated draft.
    • Quick regenerate to get an alternative within the same voice.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Length control (short, standard, detailed).
    • Inline edit and refine with a follow-up instruction (make it warmer, shorten the opener).
    • Saved context fields (name, role, occasion) to personalize output.
    • Recent drafts history.

    P2 (later)

    • Additional output types (apology, intro request, reschedule, congratulations).
    • Email and LinkedIn integrations to send or paste directly.
    • Voice learning from a user's past accepted drafts.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Target: 3 useful drafts copied per weekly active user; not observed.

    Supporting metrics

    • Paste-to-copy conversion: target 60%.
    • Regenerate rate: target under 30%, a proxy for first-draft quality.
    • Voice selection distribution and switch rate.
    • Day-30 retention and repeat-use frequency.

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Output feels generic: drafts that read as AI-written undermine trust. Mitigate by anchoring tightly to the user's pasted specifics, surfacing voices with distinct personalities, and tuning prompts against human-rated quality.
    • Tone mismatch: the wrong register can embarrass the user. Mitigate with clear voice previews, easy regeneration, and conservative defaults.
    • Accuracy and fabrication: the model could invent details. Mitigate with a strict rule to only use provided facts and a visible reminder to review before sending.
    • Thin value perception: users may feel they could write it themselves. Mitigate by making the speed and tone-switching delightfully fast and by nailing the highest-anxiety use case (interview follow-ups).

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Weeks 1 to 3): MVP: paste box, follow-up and thank-you types, three voices, copy and regenerate. Launch to a small job-seeker community and measure paste-to-copy.
    • Phase 2 (Weeks 4 to 7): Refinement: add length control, inline refine, saved context, and history. Tune voice quality against user ratings.
    • Phase 3 (Weeks 8 to 14): Expansion: add more output types and the first send integration, and explore a premium tier for unlimited drafts and voice learning.

    Key decision

    No sending or integrations at launch. Rejected alternative: connect email and LinkedIn to send directly. Cost: a copy-paste step. Reason: privacy and scope; the user stays responsible for review and delivery.

    Validation and next test

    These metrics are targets; the next step is tracking real replies.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    V1 asked people to sign in before they could see a single draft, which hid the value. V2 added a demo with sample notes and no sign in.

    What I would do next

    Remember each user's preferred voice, and add a short why-this-wording note under each draft.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Turned a small, real job (the follow-up after a coffee chat) into a one-step tool.
    • Chose privacy and scope over integrations: no sending, no inbox access.
    • Learned from V1 and removed the sign-in wall.

    Product requirements

    Swim Body

    Made-to-order swimwear sold through a threshold drop that only produces what people actually pre-order.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Swimwear is one of the hardest categories to buy online.
    North star
    65% of customization starts reach confirm (target)

    Swim Body

    Made-to-order swimwear sold through a threshold drop that only produces what people actually pre-order.

    The 30-second version. Swim Body (built as "Swim Drop Collective") is a pre-order swimwear experience built solo with AI-assisted development. It solves two problems at once: swimwear fit anxiety (the category with some of the highest return rates in apparel) and inventory risk. Three styles with bounded customization and a size flow that includes custom measurements replace guesswork with fit; a 100-order threshold with Stripe authorization (not capture) means a drop only goes into production if demand is real, and cards are never charged otherwise. The PM work on display is tiered-pricing and threshold logic, a measurement-driven fit flow designed to cut returns, and disciplined drop-based scope (no always-on inventory).

    Timeline: Weekend prototype, in testing.

    1. Problem statement

    Swimwear is one of the hardest apparel categories to buy online. Fit is intimate and unforgiving, bodies vary far more than standard sizing admits, and the result is some of the highest return rates in fashion plus the waste of producing inventory that comes back. Buyers face guesswork; brands face returns and dead stock. Swim Body's hypothesis is that pairing made-to-measure fit (custom measurements, not just S to XXL) with a threshold pre-order model attacks both failures at once: better fit lowers returns, and producing only against confirmed demand eliminates speculative inventory. The cost of not solving this is the category status quo, returns priced into every sale and landfill swimwear.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Reduce fit-driven returns by fitting suits to each body, including custom measurements (user and business outcome).
    • Produce only what is genuinely wanted via a 100-order threshold (business outcome).
    • Make pre-ordering feel safe by authorizing, never capturing, until the drop succeeds (trust outcome).
    • Let a buyer customize and pre-order in under 4 minutes (user outcome).
    • Create urgency and fairness with transparent tiered pricing and a live progress bar (business outcome).

    Non-goals

    • No always-on shop or inventory. Drops only; standing inventory reintroduces the waste we are avoiding.
    • No wishlist or favoriting. It adds surface without moving the pre-order decision.
    • No user accounts at MVP (guest checkout). A drop is a single decision, not a logged-in relationship yet.
    • No reviews on pre-order items. There is nothing to review until delivery.
    • No influencer or affiliate program. Paid amplification would distort the demand read.

    3. The product

    A single editorial landing page with a live progress bar, feeding a six-step customization flow that ends in an authorized pre-order.

    StepWhat the buyer does
    1. StyleSculpt One-Piece, Modern Bikini Set, or High-Waist Set
    2. Coverage (Sculpt only)Minimal, Medium, Full, with visual diff
    3. FitRegular, Sculpting, Ultra-smoothing
    4. FabricMatte, Glossy, Textured swatches
    5. SizeStandard (XS to XXL), Custom measurements, or a 30-second fit quiz
    6. ConfirmVisual summary, current tier price, remaining slots, pre-order to Stripe

    Pricing rewards early commitment: $95 for the first 30, then $145 standard, up to the threshold of 100. A $25 deposit is authorized, not the full suit price; remaining payment terms must be specified before launch. A live progress bar ("63 / 100 orders. Production starts at 100.") makes the collective mechanic visible. Stripe authorizes a $25 deposit at order time. When the 100th order lands, a cron captures all pending authorizations and emails everyone "we made it." If the drop misses by Day 30, all authorizations void within 24 hours and a "your card was never charged" email fires. Estimated shipping is 5 to 8 weeks from threshold.

    4. User stories

    The fit-anxious buyer

    • As someone who dreads swimwear sizing, I want to enter custom measurements so that the suit is made to my body, not a generic size.
    • As someone unsure of my size, I want a 30-second fit quiz so that I get a recommendation without measuring.

    The values-driven buyer

    • As someone who hates fashion waste, I want to know the suit is only produced if enough people order so that I am not contributing to dead stock.
    • As an early buyer, I want a better price for committing first so that being decisive is rewarded.

    The cautious buyer

    • As a pre-order buyer, I want my card authorized but not charged until the drop succeeds so that I take no risk on a product that may not be made.

    Edge and error states

    • As a buyer when a drop fills to 100, I want to be told production is locked (or offered a waiting list) so that I am not left in limbo.
    • As a buyer in a drop that misses, I want an automatic void and a clear email so that I never have to chase a refund.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Six-step customization flow completable quickly.
      • Given a buyer starts from a style, when they complete all steps, then they reach confirm in under 4 minutes.
    • Size step with standard, custom-measurement, and fit-quiz tabs.
      • Given a buyer chooses custom, when they enter chest, waist, and hips, then measurements are stored with size_type = custom.
    • Stripe authorization at order, not capture.
      • Given a buyer confirms a pre-order, when payment succeeds, then a $25 deposit is authorized (not captured) with status "authorized."
    • Live progress bar toward 100.
      • Given a new order is placed, when it commits, then the progress bar updates in real time.
    • Threshold capture and miss-void automation.
      • Given order count reaches 100, when the cron runs, then all authorizations capture within 10 minutes; given a miss by Day 30, then all authorizations void within 24 hours, each with the matching email.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Tier and capacity tracking with remaining-slot counts per tier.
    • Coverage and fit visual diffs.
    • Waiting list when a drop fills at the final tier.

    Future (P2)

    • User accounts and order history.
    • Post-delivery reviews.
    • Repeat-drop cadence and notify-me for future drops.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision 1: Made-to-order fit, not just standard sizes.

    • Choice: Offer standard sizes, custom measurements, and a fit quiz in one step.
    • Alternatives: Standard sizes only (rejected, leaves the core return driver unsolved); custom-only (rejected, too much friction for buyers who already know their size).
    • Why this won: Three paths meet buyers where they are while making the made-to-measure promise real, which is the whole pitch against returns.

    Decision 2: Drops with a threshold, not always-on inventory.

    • Choice: Produce only when a drop hits 100 orders.
    • Alternatives: Always-on shop (rejected, reintroduces speculative inventory and waste); unlimited pre-order with no threshold (rejected, no production-viability gate).
    • Why this won: A threshold turns demand validation into the production trigger, so the brand never makes something nobody bought. The trade-off is real: a drop that stalls at 90 produces nothing, and good early buyers go home empty-handed. That is the unflattering cost of the model.

    Decision 3: Tiered pricing tied to commitment order.

    • Choice: A $25 authorized deposit, $95 early price for the first 30, then $145 standard up to 100.
    • Alternatives: Flat pricing (rejected, no reward for early commitment, slower momentum); a deep early discount only (rejected, leaves margin on the table once momentum builds).
    • Why this won: Tiers create honest urgency, reward the buyers who take the most risk by going first, and improve margin as confidence builds.

    Decision 4: Guest checkout, no accounts at MVP.

    • Choice: Order by email, no login.
    • Alternatives: Require accounts (rejected, friction on a single-decision flow); full account system (rejected, premature before the model is proven).
    • Why this won: A pre-order is one decision. Accounts are a v2 concern that only matters once drops repeat.

    7. UX

    Quiet-luxury swim direction, Skims meets Jacquemus: warm sand background (#F5EFE7), terracotta accent (#BF7E68), serif display, Inter for UI, editorial photography across three model body types in natural light, explicitly no white-background catalog shots. The hero leads with "Three swim styles. Made only if we bring them to life together." and a live progress bar. Each style scrolls into its own editorial section with a pre-order CTA that opens the flow pre-selected. The size step presents three clearly separated tabs with a size guide and measurement diagrams. The confirm step shows a visual summary, the current tier price, and remaining slots.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Customization completion rate (target: 65% of flow starts reach confirm), via per-step funnel events.
    • Custom-measurement adoption rate (target: 30% of orders use custom sizing), the lever expected to cut returns.
    • Time to confirm (target: under 4 minutes), measured from flow start to confirm.

    Lagging indicators

    • Whether each drop reaches 100 (the binary production trigger).
    • Return rate, segmented by size_type (hypothesis: custom-measurement orders return less). Target return rate below 10%.
    • Repeat pre-order rate across drops (target: 25%).

    All figures are explicit targets, not observed results. No order counts, return rates, or revenue have been validated.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The model's weakness is the stalled drop: a drop that ends at 90 produces nothing and disappoints exactly the early, values-aligned buyers it most wants to keep. I would design a fallback for near-miss drops (extend the window, open the threshold slightly, or roll commitments into the next drop) rather than a hard void at Day 30. I would also pressure-test whether custom measurements actually reduce returns or just shift the error from sizing to mis-measurement; if buyers measure themselves wrong, returns do not drop. A measurement-confidence check and a fit-guarantee policy would need validation before I leaned on "fewer returns" as the core claim.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Is 100 the right threshold for production economics across styles? (finance)
    • Do custom measurements actually lower returns, or does mis-measurement cancel the gain? (data, user)
    • What is the right treatment for a near-miss drop (hard void vs rollover)? (design, finance)
    • How are the three tiers and capacities tuned for momentum without leaving margin behind? (data)

    Roadmap (v2)

    • Near-miss rollover mechanic and notify-me for future drops.
    • Accounts, order history, and post-delivery reviews.
    • Repeat-drop cadence to build a returning audience.

    Positioning

    Swim BodyFast-fashion swimStandard DTC swim
    FitMade-to-order, custom optionFixed sizesFixed sizes
    InventoryProduced on thresholdSpeculativeSpeculative
    Buyer riskAuthorized, not chargedFull, easy returnsFull
    Sustainability storyStrong (no dead stock)WeakMixed

    GTM hypothesis: launch drops to a values-aligned, fit-frustrated audience where "only made if we hit 100" is a feature, not a caveat. Defensible because the combination of made-to-measure fit and threshold production is operationally hard and on-brand in a way always-on competitors cannot easily copy.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: targeted the two linked failures of online swimwear, fit returns and inventory waste, with one model.
    • Trade-off reasoning: embraced threshold production and named its real cost (the stalled drop) rather than hiding it.
    • Technical fluency: scoped tiered-pricing and capacity logic plus Stripe authorize-at-order, capture-at-threshold automation.
    • Validation discipline: defined a binary production trigger and flagged the unproven returns claim as a risk to test.
    • Roadmap sense: identified the near-miss experience as the first thing v2 must fix.
    • Communication: transparent tier and refund copy and a clear "what NOT to build" boundary.

    Production claims and evidence

    Manufacturing origin and a 30-day salt-water test are still to be confirmed with a supplier. The portfolio does not change the external swimwear app or validate its payment behavior.

    Product requirements

    Atelier

    Made-to-measure suiting with a color harmony assistant, so anyone can order a flattering custom suit without a tailor visit.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Custom suits online force a choice between fit and price, and rarely go past size 14.
    North star
    50% of color-analysis starts reach a design page (target)

    Atelier

    Made-to-measure suiting with a color harmony assistant, so anyone can order a flattering custom suit without a tailor visit.

    The 30-second version. Atelier is a custom suiting commerce experience built solo with AI-assisted development. It pairs bounded customization (three signature designs, not infinite options) with two features most made-to-measure brands skip: a guided measurement flow that estimates fit without a tailor, and a Color Harmony Assistant that recommends flattering fabrics from undertone and contrast (three guided questions; no photo in the current flow). The PM work on display is scope discipline (three designs, not a configurator), an ethically careful recommendation system (undertone and contrast, explicitly not skin tone), and a privacy-by-design decision to defer on-device image analysis to P1. Sizes 0 to 28 use a curvy block, women-first positioning, with production proposed in Ho Chi Minh City and finishing in New York; supplier delivery is not validated.

    Live today and next

    Live today

    • Color analysis first: three questions about undertone and contrast, no photo.
    • Three signature designs (The Hourglass, The Statement, The Easy), priced from $860 to $1,240, sized 0 to 28 on a curvy block.
    • Design pages with fit notes, sample fabrics and Begin customization.

    Next

    • Fabric selection cut in the palette the color analysis locks in (sourcing not yet confirmed).
    • Payments and order tracking from Ordered to Delivered.
    • Opt-in selfie color analysis that never leaves the device.
    • A tested supplier for production and finishing.

    Timeline: Weekend prototype, in testing.

    1. Problem statement

    Buying a custom suit online forces a bad trade. Made-to-measure brands either overwhelm the buyer with endless configuration and measurement anxiety, or they flatten everything into generic sizes that defeat the point. Two questions stop most purchases cold: "Will it fit me without a tailor?" and "Will this fabric actually look good on me?" Existing players (Indochino and similar) answer the first with intimidating measurement forms and the second not at all, leaving fabric choice to guesswork that drives returns and regret. Atelier's hypothesis is that bounded choice plus two reassurance tools (fit estimation and color guidance) converts the hesitant first-time custom buyer. The live positioning is women-first, sizes 0 to 28 on a curvy block, made in Ho Chi Minh City and finished in New York.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Let a first-time buyer customize and order a suit in under 8 minutes without a tailor visit (user outcome).
    • Remove fabric-choice anxiety with a recommendation that feels personal but never judgmental (user outcome).
    • Earn measurement trust by being honest about fit confidence rather than promising perfection (trust outcome).
    • Validate that bounded customization (3 designs) converts better than open configuration (business outcome).
    • Keep image analysis private enough that privacy is a selling point, not a liability (trust and business outcome).

    Non-goals

    • No generic AI stylist chat. It invites scope creep and undercuts the curated, quiet brand voice.
    • No skin-tone classification UI. Ethically fraught and not what drives fit; we analyze undertone and contrast only.
    • No endless catalog (no shirts, coats, or accessories in v1). Three designs keeps production and decision load tight.
    • No international shipping beyond US and Canada at v1. Production and returns logistics are not solved yet.
    • No subscription or membership. This is a considered purchase, not a recurring one.

    3. The product

    Three designs and proposed starting prices: The Hourglass $980, The Statement $1,240 and The Easy $860. A mobile-first commerce flow from editorial home to tracked order. The core mechanic is a six-step customization wizard with two optional confidence tools.

    StepWhat the buyer doesWhy it matters
    1. FabricPicks from curated fabric options for the selected designBounded choice, not a fabric encyclopedia
    2. ConstructionLapel, lining, ventReal tailoring decisions, finite options
    3. FinishingButtons, stitchingPersonalization without paralysis
    4. MeasurementsQuiz, manual, or safe fallbackFit without a tailor, with honest confidence
    5. Color Harmony (optional)Three guided questions, no photos today; on-device selfie is P1Fabric reassurance, privacy-first
    6. Review and add to bagVisual summary, price breakdown, "4 to 5 weeks"No surprises at checkout

    The measurement step offers three tabs with stated confidence: a visual quiz (around 85% fit success), manual entry with how-to guides (around 95%), and a size-up or size-down safe fallback. The Color Harmony Assistant maps undertone (warm, cool, neutral), contrast (low, medium, high), and depth (light, medium, deep) to a hand-authored 27-cell recommendation table, returning three fabrics shown on the design silhouette. After checkout, the account view tracks an order through Ordered, In production, QC in NYC, Shipped, Delivered, with photos from the QC stage.

    4. User stories

    The hesitant first-time custom buyer

    • As someone who has never bought a custom suit, I want to customize and order in under 8 minutes so that the process feels approachable, not intimidating.
    • As a buyer unsure about fit, I want an honest confidence estimate for my measurements so that I can decide whether to trust the quiz or measure manually.
    • As a buyer unsure what looks good on me, I want fabric recommendations based on my coloring so that I stop second-guessing and commit.

    The privacy-conscious buyer

    • As someone wary of uploading my face, I want the color analysis to run on my device and never upload my selfie so that I can use it without giving up a photo.
    • As a buyer who would rather not share an image at all, I want a guided question path that reaches the same recommendation so that the selfie is optional.

    Edge and error states

    • As a buyer who cannot measure accurately, I want a safe size-up or size-down fallback so that I am never blocked from ordering.
    • As a buyer who dislikes the recommended fabric, I want a "Show me other options" action so that the assistant advises rather than dictates.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Six-step customization wizard with progress bar and a visual review before add-to-bag.
      • Given a buyer starts on Design A, when they complete all steps, then they can add to bag in under 8 minutes with a correct price breakdown.
    • Three-tab measurement step with a visible confidence indicator.
      • Given a buyer completes the quiz, when measurements compute, then a confidence score is shown and stored with source = quiz.
    • Color Harmony guided fallback returning three fabrics.
      • Given a buyer answers the 3 guided questions, when they submit, then three recommended fabrics render on the silhouette in under 5 seconds.
    • Stripe Checkout with order status initialized to "Ordered."
      • Given a buyer completes checkout, when payment succeeds, then an order is created with status "Ordered" and appears in the account.
    • Order tracking timeline in the account view.
      • Given an order exists, when the buyer opens order detail, then the current stage is highlighted on the Ordered to Delivered timeline.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • On-device selfie path for Color Harmony (client-side analysis, no upload, verifiable in the network tab).
    • QC-stage photos surfaced in order detail.
    • "Request alteration" CTA when an order is marked delivered.

    Future (P2)

    • Internal ops tool for the Vietnam production partner.
    • User reviews on product pages.
    • Expanded catalog and international shipping.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision 1: Three designs, not a configurator.

    • Choice: Ship exactly three signature designs with bounded options per step.
    • Alternatives: A full configurator (rejected, decision paralysis and production complexity); a single hero product (rejected, too little choice to feel "custom").
    • Why this won: Three is enough to feel personal and few enough to keep production, photography, and the buyer's working memory manageable.

    Decision 2: Honest fit confidence over a promise of perfection.

    • Choice: Show explicit confidence (quiz around 85%, manual around 95%) and offer a safe fallback.
    • Alternatives: Claim guaranteed fit (rejected, sets up returns and broken trust); hide the uncertainty (rejected, dishonest and worse for the buyer).
    • Why this won: Naming the uncertainty builds trust and nudges high-stakes buyers toward manual measurement, which lowers return risk. This is the unflattering trade-off: it likely depresses top-of-funnel conversion slightly in exchange for fewer bad fits.

    Decision 3: Undertone and contrast, never skin tone.

    • Choice: A heuristic that classifies undertone, contrast, and depth, with copy that always says "tends to complement," never "you should never wear."
    • Alternatives: ML skin-tone categorization (rejected, ethically fraught and brittle); no color guidance (rejected, leaves the biggest anxiety unaddressed).
    • Why this won: It solves the real anxiety (what flatters me) without classifying people, and the advisory tone keeps the buyer in control.

    Decision 4: Guided questions now; on-device image analysis in P1, no upload.

    • Choice: Three guided questions today. The designed P1 selfie path would sample pixels client-side and never transmit the image; it is not built.
    • Alternatives: Server-side analysis (rejected, creates a sensitive-image liability and a privacy objection); no selfie path (rejected, the guided fallback alone is less magical).
    • Why this won: Guided questions provide advice without asking for a face image. The accepted cost is less automatic analysis; selfie-based recommendations require later validation.

    7. UX

    Quiet-luxury direction: warm bone background (#F4F2EE), serif headlines (Italiana or Cormorant), Inter for UI, editorial imagery of models in environments rather than on white, motion limited to 200ms cross-fades. The home page leads with "Three designs. Made for you." and three product cards. The customization wizard is a single-column, progress-barred flow. The measurement step uses three tabs with a persistent confidence indicator. The Color Harmony Assistant opens with the disclaimer "We analyze undertone and contrast, not skin tone" and renders three fabrics on the silhouette. The account order detail shows a vertical production timeline with QC photos.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Color-analysis completion rate (target: 50% of color-analysis starts reach a design page), measured by funnel events per wizard step.
    • Color Harmony usage rate among buyers (target: 35% opt in), and split of guided vs selfie path.
    • Median time to add-to-bag (target: under 8 minutes), measured from wizard start to add-to-bag event.

    Lagging indicators

    • Order conversion rate from product page (target: 8%).
    • Return or alteration request rate, segmented by measurement source (hypothesis: manual measurers return less). Target: alteration request rate below 15%.
    • Net repeat or referral rate (target: 20%).

    All figures above are explicit targets, not observed results. No sales, conversion, or fit-success figures have been validated; the 85% and 95% confidence figures are design targets in the spec, not measured outcomes.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The hardest open risk is that the heuristic color logic is hand-authored across 27 cells and unvalidated against real outcomes; if recommendations feel off, the assistant erodes trust instead of building it. I would A/B test recommendation acceptance ("Use this fabric" vs "Show me other options") before promoting the assistant in the funnel. I would also instrument the measurement tabs to see whether the honesty about confidence actually steers buyers to manual entry or just adds friction. If it adds friction without lowering returns, the confidence framing needs to be softer and later in the flow.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Does honest fit confidence reduce returns enough to justify any conversion drag? (data, user)
    • Is the 27-cell color table accurate enough to recommend, or does it need a feedback loop? (data, design)
    • Proposed alteration policy: first alteration up to $80, not an unlimited complimentary alteration. What is the real NYC cost, and does the cap hold margin? (legal, finance)
    • Does the selfie path's privacy story actually lift opt-in, or do buyers prefer the guided path regardless? (user)

    Roadmap (v2)

    • Production ops tool and QC photo automation.
    • Recommendation feedback loop to tune the color table from acceptance data.
    • Reviews and an expanded but still bounded catalog.

    Positioning

    AtelierIndochino-style MTMFast-fashion suiting
    ChoiceBounded (3 designs)Open configuratorFixed sizes
    Fit supportQuiz + manual + fallback, honest confidenceHeavy measurement formNone
    Color guidanceYes, privacy-firstNoNo
    VoiceQuiet luxuryPromotionalDisposable

    GTM hypothesis: reach style-conscious first-time custom buyers who have been scared off by configurators, leading with the color assistant as the differentiated hook. Defensible because the curation, the ethically careful recommendation system, and the privacy-by-design analysis are hard to copy without the same restraint.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: named the two purchase-blocking anxieties (fit and fabric) and built directly against them.
    • Trade-off reasoning: chose honesty about fit confidence over a conversion-friendly false promise, and accepted the downside.
    • Technical fluency: scoped client-side LAB color analysis and a hand-authored recommendation table, with a verifiable no-upload privacy claim.
    • Ethical product judgment: undertone and contrast, never skin-tone classification, with advisory (never prescriptive) copy.
    • Validation discipline: separated design targets from measured outcomes and flagged the unvalidated recommendation logic as the top risk.
    • Communication: a bounded scope, an explicit "what NOT to build" list, and clear acceptance criteria.

    Product requirements

    Folio

    A leather carry brand validated the honest way: a refundable deposit that only charges if enough people want the bag.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Most launches validate demand with the weakest signal: an email signup.
    North star
    4% of visitors place a refundable deposit (target)

    Folio

    A leather carry brand validated the honest way: a refundable deposit that only charges if enough people want the bag.

    The 30-second version. Folio (built as "Carryall") is a single-product validation landing page for a leather laptop messenger-backpack hybrid, built solo with AI-assisted development. It does one thing well: it separates curiosity from commitment by offering two CTAs, a low-friction waitlist and a real $25 refundable deposit that authorizes a card without capturing it. Production only tools up at 150 reservations; if the threshold misses, every authorization voids automatically and no one is charged. The PM work on display is demand validation discipline (deposits over vanity signups), Stripe manual-capture authorization logic with auto-expiry handling, and ruthless single-product scope.

    Timeline: Weekend prototype, in testing.

    Status: Stripe in test mode, no live campaign, counter hidden until 10 real deposits.

    Planned test: Send 50 visitors from one LinkedIn post, measure refundable deposit versus waitlist intent, and record abandonment; this is a proposed experiment, not completed evidence.

    1. Problem statement

    Most product launches validate demand with the weakest possible signal: an email signup. People will join a waitlist for almost anything, which tells a founder nothing about willingness to pay and leads to over-producing inventory nobody buys. For a single high-cost leather good, guessing wrong means tooling a production run against demand that does not exist. Folio's hypothesis is that a small, refundable, real-money commitment ($25 authorized, not captured) is a far truer demand signal than a free signup, and that buyers will give it precisely because the refund promise is unconditional. The cost of not solving this is the classic hardware-and-goods failure: a warehouse of unsold bags.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Measure genuine willingness to pay before committing a dollar to production (business outcome).
    • Make committing feel safe enough that real-money validation does not kill conversion (user outcome).
    • Reach a clear go or no-go threshold (150 reservations) with an automatic, trustworthy outcome either way (business and trust outcome).
    • Let a curious visitor express interest in under 15 seconds and a serious one reserve in under 90 seconds (user outcome).

    Non-goals

    • No multi-SKU shop. One product keeps the validation signal clean and the build small.
    • No color or size variants at MVP. Variants dilute the demand signal and multiply production complexity.
    • No accounts or login. The flow is a single decision, not a relationship yet.
    • No influencer or affiliate program. Paid amplification would contaminate the organic demand read.
    • No international shipping at MVP. US-only keeps fulfillment assumptions honest.

    3. The product

    A single anchored landing page where the product photography carries most of the persuasion, ending in one of two commitment flows.

    SectionPurpose
    HeroOn-body messenger image, headline, two CTAs (Reserve $25 / Join waitlist), live supporter count
    Two modesMessenger and backpack shown side by side
    SpecsLabeled diagram, "Fits a 13 to 15 inch laptop. Under 2 lbs."
    Founder storyWhy this bag, why now, "built from one functional prototype"
    The planThe 150-reservation threshold and the refund promise, spelled out
    FAQ and footerRefunds, timeline, shipping, final CTA

    Two conversion flows:

    1. Waitlist: email-only modal, one field, instant confirmation. Low friction, weak signal.
    2. Deposit: name, email, shipping zip, then Stripe Checkout that authorizes $25 with capture_method = manual. Confirmation makes the mechanic explicit: "Your card is authorized. We capture nothing until production starts."

    The threshold logic runs the validation. A cron job reauthorizes pending payment intents before their 7-day expiry so authorizations stay live until the campaign resolves. On hitting 150, all authorizations capture and a "we made it" email fires. On a miss at the deadline, all authorizations void within 24 hours and a "your card was never charged" email fires.

    4. User stories

    The curious visitor

    • As someone mildly interested, I want to join a waitlist in seconds without commitment so that I can stay informed without deciding.

    The serious buyer

    • As someone who genuinely wants this bag, I want to reserve it with a small refundable deposit so that I help make it real and lock founder pricing ($299 vs $399 retail).
    • As a deposit-payer, I want it made unmistakably clear that my card is only authorized, not charged, so that I commit without fear.

    The cautious buyer

    • As someone burned by crowdfunding before, I want an automatic, no-questions refund if the project does not hit its threshold so that I trust the founder's promise.

    Edge and error states

    • As a reserver whose authorization is about to expire mid-campaign, I want it silently re-authorized so that my reservation does not lapse before the go or no-go decision.
    • As any visitor, I want the live supporter count to be real (shown only after the first 10 deposits) so that the social proof is honest, not fabricated.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Single landing page, all sections, fast on mobile.
      • Given a mobile visitor, when the page loads, then it renders in under 2 seconds.
    • Email-only waitlist writing to Supabase.
      • Given a visitor, when they submit the waitlist form, then it completes in under 15 seconds and shows confirmation.
    • Deposit flow via Stripe manual_capture.
      • Given a buyer, when they complete the deposit, then $25 is authorized (not captured) in under 90 seconds and a reservation row is created with status "authorized."
    • Threshold capture automation.
      • Given reservation count reaches 150, when the cron runs, then all pending authorizations capture within 10 minutes and the "we made it" email sends.
    • Threshold-miss void automation.
      • Given count is below 150 at campaign end, when the cron runs, then all authorizations void within 24 hours and the refund email sends.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Live reservation counter on the hero updating in real time.
    • Authorization auto-reauthorize cron to extend past the 7-day expiry.
    • All three lifecycle emails (confirmation, threshold-hit, threshold-miss).

    Future (P2)

    • Color or size variants once a single SKU validates.
    • Accounts and reorder.
    • International shipping.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision 1: A refundable deposit, not just a waitlist.

    • Choice: Offer both, but treat the $25 authorization as the real validation metric.
    • Alternatives: Waitlist only (rejected, measures curiosity not willingness to pay); a full pre-purchase (rejected, too much friction before the product exists).
    • Why this won: An authorized card is the cleanest demand signal short of a sale, and pairing it with a free waitlist captures both intent tiers without forcing everyone to commit.

    Decision 2: Authorize, never capture, until the threshold.

    • Choice: Use Stripe manual_capture so cards are only charged if production actually proceeds.
    • Alternatives: Capture upfront and refund on failure (rejected, refunds are slower, feel worse, and create chargeback and trust risk); collect nothing (rejected, no real signal).
    • Why this won: The buyer takes zero financial risk, which makes the refund promise credible and lifts deposit conversion. The cost is engineering complexity: authorizations expire in 7 days and must be re-authorized on a cron until the campaign resolves. This is the unflattering trade-off, real operational fragility in exchange for an honest, low-risk commitment.

    Decision 3: One product, no variants.

    • Choice: Validate exactly one bag with no color or size pickers.
    • Alternatives: Launch a small range (rejected, splits demand across SKUs and muddies the read); offer variants (rejected, multiplies production complexity before demand is proven).
    • Why this won: A single SKU gives one clean number to decide on. Variants are a v2 problem that only matters if the core demand exists.

    Decision 4: Honest social proof, gated.

    • Choice: Show the supporter count only after the first 10 real deposits.
    • Alternatives: Fake or seed the count (rejected, dishonest and a trust risk if discovered); always show it (rejected, "1 supporter" is worse than no number).
    • Why this won: Social proof that only appears once it is real protects credibility, which is the whole foundation of a deposit-based campaign.

    7. UX

    Quiet leather-goods direction closer to Cuyana than Bellroy: warm cream background (#F5F0E8), leather-brown accent (#6B4E2E), serif headline, Inter for UI, editorial on-body photography in both carry modes. The image carries roughly 80% of the conversion, so the hero is photography-first with two clearly ranked CTAs. The deposit modal foregrounds the authorize-not-capture promise in plain language. The plan section lays out the 150-threshold and the automatic refund as a numbered sequence so the go or no-go logic is unambiguous.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Deposit conversion rate among visitors (target: 4%), the headline validation number.
    • Ratio of deposits to waitlist signups (hypothesis: a healthy ratio signals real willingness to pay), target ratio 1:5.
    • Time to reserve (target: under 90 seconds), measured from deposit-modal open to authorization success.

    Lagging indicators

    • Whether the campaign hits 150 reservations by the deadline (the binary go or no-go).
    • Deposit-to-capture retention (target: under 10% void requests before the threshold), measuring how many reservers stay committed.
    • Post-capture fulfillment satisfaction (target: 80%).

    All figures are explicit targets, not observed results. No reservation counts, conversion rates, or revenue have been validated.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The authorization-expiry cron is the riskiest part of the build: if a re-authorization silently fails, a committed reserver drops out of the count through no fault of their own, which corrupts the exact metric the project exists to measure. I would add expiry-monitoring with alerting and a buyer-facing "we re-confirmed your reservation" email before shipping this for real. I would also question whether 150 is the right threshold; it was set in the spec without unit-economics backing, and the go or no-go decision is only as good as that number. Before launch I would back the threshold into the actual production minimum and margin.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Is 150 the true break-even production minimum, or a placeholder? (finance)
    • What deposit-to-waitlist ratio counts as "validated enough" to tool production? (data)
    • How do we handle re-authorization failures without losing committed reservers? (eng)
    • Does the refund promise create any chargeback or regulatory exposure? (legal)

    Roadmap (v2)

    • Variants and a small range once one SKU validates.
    • Reservation accounts and reorder.
    • Repeat-drop mechanics if the model works.

    Positioning

    FolioKickstarterStandard pre-order
    CommitmentRefundable authorizationPledge, capturedCaptured upfront
    Buyer riskNear zeroFunds heldFull
    Brand fitCurated, on-brand pagePlatform-genericVaries
    Signal qualityHigh (real money, low risk)High but platform-boundHigh but high-friction

    GTM hypothesis: launch to a warm audience (founder network, niche carry communities) where the founder story lands, and let the refund promise do the de-risking. Defensible because the honest-validation mechanic and the on-brand experience are hard to replicate on a generic crowdfunding platform.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: recognized that waitlists measure curiosity, not willingness to pay, and designed for the latter.
    • Trade-off reasoning: chose authorize-not-capture and accepted real operational fragility for a low-risk, honest commitment.
    • Technical fluency: scoped Stripe manual_capture with authorization auto-expiry handling on a cron, the genuinely hard part.
    • Validation discipline: defined a binary go or no-go threshold and separated it from explicit targets, not observed results.
    • Scope sense: a strict single-product, no-variant MVP with an explicit "what NOT to build" list.
    • Communication: plain-language commitment copy that makes the financial mechanic impossible to misread.

    Product requirements

    Splitcheck

    Snap the receipt, share a link, and everyone taps what they had. Tax and tip split automatically. Built for my own dinners and used by CBS classmates.

    Live ↗
    In real useSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Splitting a shared bill is awkward and error-prone.
    North star
    80% of splits fully settled within 48 hours (target)

    In use: I built Splitcheck for my own dinners and trips, and CBS classmates now use it to settle real group checks, almost always from their phones.

    Results so far

    About 100 splits created with it so far, by me and Columbia Business School classmates, usually from phones. Settlement within 48 hours is still a target, not an observed result.

    A real use case

    A group finishes dinner. The receipt shows $242.00 in food, $21.48 in tax, and a $40.00 tip, for $303.48 in total. One person snaps a photo of the check and shares a link in the group chat. Each friend opens it on their phone and taps the dishes they had. Splitcheck spreads tax and tip in proportion to what each person ordered, so everyone sees their exact share and the shares add up to $303.48. Nobody does math at the table, and the person who paid is not left chasing anyone.

    Splitcheck

    One-line summary: Snap the receipt, share a link, and everyone taps what they had. Tax and tip split automatically. Built for my own dinners and used by CBS classmates.

    Problem

    Splitting a shared bill is socially awkward and error-prone. The person who paid is left chasing friends, "just split it evenly" feels unfair when people ordered very differently, and the mental math plus the payment requests create friction at exactly the moment everyone wants to relax. Existing tools are either clunky ledgers or even-split calculators that ignore who actually ordered what.

    Target users

    • Primary: Friend groups in their 20s and 30s who dine out, travel, and share expenses and want fairness without confrontation.
    • Secondary: Roommates splitting recurring shared costs.
    • Tertiary: The "designated organizer" in any group who always ends up fronting and reconciling the bill.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Make fair, itemized splitting fast enough to do at the table.
    • Remove the awkwardness of asking friends for money.
    • Get every participant to settle up with minimal follow-up.

    Non-goals

    • We are not building our own payment rails or holding funds at launch.
    • We are not a long-term group accounting or budgeting ledger.
    • We are not handling currency conversion or international tax in v1.

    Key features

    P0 (live core)

    • Receipt photo scan to auto-populate line items.
    • Share link, no accounts: friends open the link and claim their items from their phones.
    • Create a split, add participants, and enter the bill total with tax and tip.
    • Itemized splitting: assign line items to specific people, including shared items split among a subset.
    • Fairness logic that proportionally allocates tax and tip based on each person's subtotal.
    • Per-person owed amount summary with a clear breakdown.
    • External payment handoff: Venmo and Cash App deep links are planned, not yet built.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Saved friends and groups for repeat splits.
    • Settlement tracking (who has paid, gentle nudges to those who have not).
    • Even-split and percentage-split modes for simple cases.

    P2 (later)

    • Recurring shared expenses for roommates.
    • Group balance netting across multiple outings.
    • In-app settle-up via an integrated payment partner.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Target: 80% of splits fully settled within 48 hours, confirmed by the organizer rather than inferred from an item claim.

    Leading indicator: Target: median time from photo to shared link under 60 seconds.

    Supporting metrics

    • Split creation to settlement conversion rate.
    • Median time from bill entry to first payment handoff.
    • Participants per split who actually pay through the handoff.
    • Repeat-organizer retention (Day-30 and Day-90).

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Payment friction: handoff to external apps can drop people. Mitigate with clear amounts and organizer-confirmed settlement; deep links and native settle-up require separate verification and integration.
    • Itemization effort: manual entry is tedious. Mitigate with receipt scanning and saved groups to cut repeat input.
    • Trust and accuracy: a wrong split breaks trust instantly. Mitigate with a transparent, auditable breakdown every participant can see and verify.
    • Cold-start network effects: value rises when friends use it. Mitigate by making the payer-side experience valuable even when others are not users, since participants just receive a request link.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Weeks 1 to 5): MVP: receipt scanning, share links without accounts, itemized claims, fairness logic for tax and tip, and per-person summaries. Payment deep links remain unconfirmed. Launch to friend-group beta testers.
    • Phase 2 (Weeks 6 to 10): Reduce friction: add saved friends and groups, and settlement tracking with nudges.
    • Phase 3 (Weeks 11 to 18): Stickiness and depth: add recurring expenses, cross-outing balance netting, and an integrated settle-up partner, optimizing toward fully settled bills.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • Share link with no accounts. Speed over saved history. Rejected alternative: require every friend to register before claiming items. Accepted cost: no account-based cross-outing history.
    • Proportional tax and tip by default. Fairness over the simplest even split. Rejected alternative: divide every check evenly regardless of what people ordered. Accepted cost: item claims and transparent rounding need more logic.

    What did not work and what I would change

    Receipt OCR can misread crumpled receipts, and a friend who never opens the link can leave a split unfinished. These are the two failure points I watch most. I would make every scanned line editable, show unclaimed items clearly, and give the organizer a manual assignment fallback before adding more payment integrations.

    What I would do next

    Venmo and Cash App links in each person's share, and an organizer view that shows who has paid.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Built from my own pain point and adopted by classmates for real checks.
    • Chose speed (no accounts) and fairness (proportional tax and tip) with clear costs.
    • Named the two failure points and the fixes.

    Product requirements

    Deal Scout

    A $9 a month membership that surfaces last-minute and standby flight deals on popular short-haul routes.

    Live V1 ↗Live V2 ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Last-minute and standby flight deals exist but are scattered and gone fast.
    North star
    70% of paying members still subscribed at month 3 (target)

    Deal Scout

    A $9 a month membership that surfaces last-minute and standby flight deals on popular short-haul routes.

    Problem

    Airlines fly with empty seats and release discounted last-minute and standby inventory, but that inventory is scattered, time-sensitive, and hard for travelers to catch. Flexible travelers who could grab a cheap NYC-to-Miami flight this weekend have no single place that watches for these deals and alerts them in time. The deals expire in hours, and casual fare trackers are tuned for advance planning, not the last-minute window.

    Target users

    • Primary: Flexible urban travelers 22 to 45 on high-frequency leisure corridors (for example NYC, Miami, LA) who can move on short notice.
    • Secondary: Remote workers and digital nomads who travel opportunistically.
    • Tertiary: Budget-conscious frequent visitors (visiting family, second-home trips) on a fixed route pair.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Reliably surface genuinely good last-minute and standby deals on supported routes.
    • Alert members fast enough to act before inventory disappears.
    • Make the $9 per month subscription pay for itself within a member's first deal.

    Non-goals

    • We are not a full OTA or booking engine at launch; we surface and hand off.
    • We are not covering every global route initially; we focus on a few dense corridors.
    • We are not guaranteeing seats on standby; we surface opportunities, not promises.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • Route subscription: members pick home corridors (origin and destination pairs).
    • Deal feed of current last-minute and standby fares with price, departure window, and time remaining.
    • Real-time push and email alerts when a qualifying deal appears on a watched route.
    • Deal detail with a clear booking handoff to the airline or partner.
    • $9 per month membership with simple subscribe and cancel.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Price thresholds and flexibility filters (max price, acceptable departure windows, weekend only).
    • Deal quality score versus typical fares on that route.
    • Saved trips and one-tap re-alert for a specific date range.
    • Member savings dashboard (total saved versus subscription cost).

    P2 (later)

    • Additional corridors and expansion to medium-haul routes.
    • In-app or assisted booking to reduce handoff drop-off.
    • Group or annual plans and referral incentives.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Net member months retained, reflecting that members keep paying because deals deliver value. Target: 70% of paying members still subscribed at month 3.

    Supporting metrics

    • Trial-to-paid conversion: target 25%; monthly churn: target under 8%.
    • Alert-to-click: target 30%; track click-to-booking handoff separately.
    • Average member savings: target at least 3x the $9 monthly fee ($27), not observed.
    • Deals surfaced per active route per week (supply health).

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Deal supply variability: thin inventory weeks erode value. Mitigate by concentrating on dense corridors with frequent releases and by setting expectations on cadence.
    • Standby unpredictability: standby seats may not clear. Mitigate with clear labeling, confidence indicators, and emphasis on confirmed last-minute fares.
    • Churn after a slow month: members cancel without a recent win. Mitigate with the savings dashboard, threshold alerts so users only hear about relevant deals, and proactive re-engagement.
    • Booking handoff leakage: value is hard to attribute through external booking. Mitigate with handoff tracking and a later assisted-booking flow.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Weeks 1 to 6): Single corridor pilot: launch one high-density route (for example NYC to Miami) with the deal feed, alerts, and membership. Validate supply and willingness to pay.
    • Phase 2 (Weeks 7 to 12): Personalization: add filters, deal quality scores, and the savings dashboard. Expand to two or three more corridors.
    • Phase 3 (Weeks 13 to 24): Scale and retention: broaden corridors, test assisted booking, and add annual and referral plans, optimizing for net member months retained.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • Surface-and-hand-off over in-app booking. Rejected alternative: become a booking engine. Cost: attribution and conversion leakage; hand-off first avoids payment, ticketing and support scope.
    • One corridor over many. Rejected alternative: a thin global feed. Cost: smaller audience; one dense corridor tests useful supply before expansion.

    Feasibility

    Candidate supply sources are airline last-minute fare pages, standby programs and fare APIs such as Skyscanner or Kiwi. Access agreements and licensing are not established. The biggest unknown is whether true standby inventory is exposed at all; confirmed last-minute fares and standby opportunities must be labeled separately.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Standby inventory is not exposed publicly, so the core promise could not be proven with live data; the demo runs on sample fares. V1, Standby+, covered one route (New York to Miami). I would validate demand on that one route with a paid test before building more data access.

    What this demonstrates

    • I define the inventory-access risk before promising a subscription.
    • I choose a single corridor to test supply density.
    • I keep booking with the supplier until alerts prove value.

    Validation and next test

    Next: 10 interviews on last-minute travel habits and a $9 price test.

    Product requirements

    Job Habit

    A habit tracker that turns an overwhelming job search into three timed daily sessions, so seekers stay consistent through a long search.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    A long job search offers almost no positive feedback.
    North star
    All three sessions completed on 60% of days (target)

    Job Habit

    A habit tracker that turns an overwhelming job search into three timed daily sessions, so seekers stay consistent through a long search.

    The 30-second version. Job Habit is a daily job-search session tracker built solo with AI-assisted development, with a companion application-pipeline dashboard. It reframes the job hunt from an anxious, formless slog into a repeatable 3.5-hour ritual: three timed sessions (Warm Outreach 90 min, Applying 60 min, Follow-Up 60 min), each with its own task list, circular timer, and a streak counter that rewards showing up. The PM work on display is behavior design (consistency over intensity, structure over willpower), a tight focus on the one variable a seeker controls (daily effort, not outcomes), and an interaction model built around momentum and small wins.

    Timeline: Built in a focused session, in testing.

    Live: https://jobhabit.lovable.app

    1. Problem statement

    A job search is a long, demoralizing process with no fixed end date and almost no positive feedback along the way. Rejection is the norm, progress is invisible, and the work itself is formless: there is always more outreach to do, more applications to send, more follow-ups pending, and no signal that today was "enough." That shapelessness is what burns people out. They binge for a weekend, crash, then avoid the search for days. Job Habit's hypothesis, drawn from habit and behavior design, is that the seeker controls exactly one thing (daily effort, not the outcome) and that giving that effort a bounded, repeatable structure (a fixed 3.5-hour ritual with visible streaks) sustains the consistency that a long search actually requires. The cost of not solving this is the avoidance spiral: missed days, lost momentum, and a search that drags longer than it should.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Make daily job-search effort consistent by giving it a bounded, repeatable structure (user outcome).
    • Shift the seeker's focus from uncontrollable outcomes to controllable daily action (user outcome).
    • Reward showing up with streaks and visible progress so motivation survives a long search (user outcome).
    • Reduce decision fatigue by pre-defining what each session contains (user outcome).

    Non-goals

    • Not a job board or aggregator. Finding roles is a different problem; this is about doing the work.
    • Not an applicant tracking system in the main app (the companion dashboard covers pipeline). The tracker stays about today's effort.
    • No gamified vanity metrics beyond streak and session completion. Vanity points would distract from real effort.
    • No account or cloud sync required to start. Friction at the door defeats a daily-habit tool.

    3. The product

    A single-screen daily ritual built around three timed sessions and a streak.

    SessionDurationFocusSample tasks
    Warm Outreach90 minRelationshipsLinkedIn messages, school check-ins, speaker follow-ups, recruiter notes
    Applying60 minSubmissionsTier A roles first, customize materials, track listings, note gaps
    Follow-Up60 minMomentumReply to inbound, nudge after 5 to 7 days, update statuses, schedule chats

    The screen shows today's date, the title "Your 3 1/2 Hours," a streak pill and a sessions-done pill, and a full-width progress bar that fills across all 210 minutes. Each session is a card with a circular SVG progress ring, an MM:SS countdown, a task list that appears when the card is active, and an optional notes field. A controls bar (Start, Pause, Reset, Done) drives the active session; completing one triggers a pop-in animation, and finishing all three reveals a celebration banner. A rotating motivational quote anchors the footer. The companion HTML dashboard tracks the actual application pipeline (metrics row, status columns for active, their move, interviews, and rejected or closed) as a separate, outcomes-facing view.

    4. User stories

    The overwhelmed seeker

    • As someone who does not know where to start each day, I want three pre-defined sessions so that I can just begin instead of deciding.
    • As someone who burns out, I want a bounded 3.5-hour daily target so that I know when I have done enough and can stop guilt-free.

    The motivation-starved seeker

    • As someone facing constant rejection, I want a streak that rewards showing up so that my effort is acknowledged even when outcomes are not.
    • As someone who needs momentum, I want a celebration when I complete all three sessions so that finishing feels like a win.

    The organized seeker

    • As someone juggling many applications, I want a separate pipeline view so that tracking outcomes does not clutter my daily-effort ritual.

    Edge and error states

    • As a user who gets interrupted, I want to pause and resume a session without losing my time so that real life does not break my streak.
    • As a user who only finishes some sessions, I want partial progress reflected honestly so that the tool stays trustworthy rather than punishing.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Three timed sessions with correct durations (90, 60, 60).
      • Given a user selects a session and presses Start, when time elapses, then the countdown and ring decrement accurately and mark the session done at zero.
    • Start, Pause, Resume, Reset, and Done controls.
      • Given a running session, when the user pauses, then the timer holds and Start becomes Resume; when reset, the session returns to full duration.
    • Streak and sessions-done counters that update dynamically.
      • Given a user completes sessions, when state changes, then the streak and "N/3 Sessions" pills update.
    • Overall progress bar across all 210 minutes.
      • Given any session progresses, when time elapses, then the full-width bar fills proportionally.
    • Completion and celebration states.
      • Given all three sessions are done, when the last completes, then the celebration banner appears.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Per-session task lists and optional notes.
    • Rotating motivational quotes.
    • Companion pipeline dashboard for outcomes tracking.

    Future (P2)

    • Persistence and cross-device sync of streaks and notes.
    • Customizable session durations and tasks.
    • Light analytics on consistency over time.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision 1: Consistency over intensity.

    • Choice: A bounded daily ritual (3.5 hours, three sessions) with a streak, rather than open-ended productivity tracking.
    • Alternatives: An unbounded to-do list (rejected, reproduces the formlessness that causes burnout); pure outcome tracking (rejected, rewards things the user cannot control).
    • Why this won: Habit research says consistency beats sporadic intensity, and a bounded target gives the seeker permission to stop, which is what makes the habit sustainable.

    Decision 2: Three fixed sessions with pre-defined tasks.

    • Choice: Outreach, Applying, Follow-Up, each with a starter task list.
    • Alternatives: Let users define their own structure (rejected, decision fatigue is the enemy); a single undifferentiated work block (rejected, hides that the three kinds of job-search work need different energy).
    • Why this won: Pre-defined structure removes the daily "where do I start" friction and reflects that outreach, applying, and following up are genuinely different modes.

    Decision 3: Effort tracking and outcome tracking, kept separate.

    • Choice: The daily app tracks effort and streaks; a companion dashboard tracks the application pipeline and outcomes.
    • Alternatives: Merge them into one screen (rejected, outcome data, full of rejections, would poison the daily-momentum tool); drop outcome tracking entirely (rejected, seekers still need a pipeline view).
    • Why this won: Keeping rejection-heavy outcome data out of the daily ritual protects the motivational core of the habit, while the separate dashboard still serves the organizing need. This is the deliberate, slightly unflattering trade-off: two surfaces instead of one clean integrated product.

    Decision 4: Works immediately, no account wall.

    • Choice: Start the ritual with no login required.
    • Alternatives: Require sign-up for streak persistence (rejected, friction at the door kills daily-habit adoption).
    • Why this won: A daily-habit tool has to be frictionless on day one. The cost is that streaks do not persist across devices without later sync, which is a v2 problem.

    7. UX

    A calm, single-screen daily dashboard. Plus Jakarta Sans, a soft off-white background (#FAFAFA), and three accent colors that code the sessions (coral outreach, teal applying, amber follow-up). The header states the date and "Your 3 1/2 Hours"; stat pills show streak and sessions done. Each session card carries a stroke-animated circular progress ring with the countdown inside, a status label, and, when active, its task list and notes toggle. Motion is purposeful and rewarding: staggered fade-ups on load, a pop-in on completion, a gentle pulse on the running timer, and a celebration banner when all three finish. The companion pipeline dashboard uses a darker, editorial treatment (Fraunces display, IBM Plex) to visually separate outcome tracking from the daily ritual.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    Leading indicators

    • Daily completion rate (target: user completes all three sessions on 60 percent of days), measured by completion events.
    • Streak length distribution (target: median streak of 5 days), measured from streak data.
    • Session start rate (target: 80 percent of opens lead to at least one session started), measuring whether the structure lowers activation energy.

    Lagging indicators

    • Retention over a multi-week search (target: 40 percent of users active in week 4), the real test of a habit tool.
    • Self-reported consistency and reduced burnout (target: 60 percent report the ritual helped them stay consistent).

    All figures are explicit targets, not observed results. No usage, streak, or retention data has been validated.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The biggest honest weakness is the missing persistence: a streak that resets when you close the tab undercuts the exact mechanic the whole product depends on, so streak storage is the first thing I would build before any real test. The fixed 3.5-hour target is also an assumption, not a validated dose; for some searches it is too much (part-time seekers, those still employed) and the rigidity could itself cause avoidance. I would test adjustable durations and a "minimum viable day" option. Finally, splitting effort and outcome into two surfaces is a real cost: I would validate whether users actually want a unified view despite the motivational risk, or whether the separation holds.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Is 3.5 hours the right daily dose, or should it flex by situation? (user, data)
    • Does the streak mechanic sustain motivation or eventually create guilt that drives avoidance? (user)
    • Should effort and outcome tracking eventually merge, and at what cost to morale? (design, user)
    • What is the minimum persistence needed for streaks to feel real? (eng)

    Roadmap (v2)

    • Streak and notes persistence with optional sync.
    • Adjustable session durations and a minimum-viable-day mode.
    • Light consistency analytics over time.

    Positioning

    Job HabitGeneric habit trackerSpreadsheet job tracker
    FocusJob-search effort, bounded ritualAny habit, genericOutcomes only
    Daily structurePre-defined sessions and tasksUser-definedNone
    MotivationStreaks, celebration, quotesStreaksNone
    Outcome viewSeparate companion dashboardNoneYes, but no effort side

    GTM hypothesis: reach active job seekers (including MBA and early-career searchers) through job-search communities, positioning the bounded daily ritual as the antidote to search burnout. Defensible because the behavior-design framing and the deliberate effort-versus-outcome separation are opinionated product choices, not generic features.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: named search burnout and formlessness as the real enemy, not a lack of job listings.
    • Behavior design: applied habit principles (consistency over intensity, controllable effort, streaks, bounded scope) to a real emotional problem.
    • Trade-off reasoning: deliberately separated effort from rejection-heavy outcome data to protect motivation, and named the cost.
    • Technical fluency: precise client-side timer logic (intervals, refs, ring animation via strokeDashoffset) with clean state handling.
