    • Validation discipline: flagged missing persistence and the unproven 3.5-hour dose as the top risks to test.
    • Communication: a single-screen ritual that makes "what does enough look like today" instantly legible.

    Validation and next test

    Next: save progress across sessions, the main gap I found in my own use.

    Product requirements

    Job Spark (concept)

    Unpublished.

    Concept, V2 of CBS CollectiveSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    A long job search makes real effort feel invisible.
    North star
    60% of testers say the weekly reflection made the search feel manageable (target)

    Version history: V1 is CBS Collective (live). Job Spark is V2, a motivation layer designed on top of it, not yet built.

    Job Spark (concept)

    V2 of the CBS job tools: a motivation layer on top of CBS Collective (concept, not yet built)

    Problem

    A long job search makes real effort feel invisible. The concept is encouragement grounded in a student's own tracked actions, not a second job board.

    How it works

    Use the CBS Job Aggregator for discovery, posting and pipeline tracking. Job Spark would reflect a small real win from that activity.

    What I would build first

    One optional weekly reflection on saved roles and completed outreach, tested before adding streaks or generic encouragement.

    Decision 4: The motivation layer rides on the tracker, it is not a separate app.

    • Choice: Build encouragement and small-wins surfacing on top of real tracked activity rather than as standalone affirmations.
    • Alternatives: A pure motivation app with generic encouragement (rejected, hollow without grounding in the user's actual progress); ignore motivation (rejected, it is the product's positioning).
    • Why this won: Encouragement tied to the user's real actions is credible; encouragement floating free of them is noise.

    Non-goals

    • Not a second job board; discovery stays in CBS Collective.
    • No generic motivational quotes; every win comes from the student's own actions.
    • No public leaderboards comparing classmates.

    One metric

    Target: 60% of concept testers report that the weekly reflection made the search feel more manageable; not observed.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The honest tension here is positioning: the recovered build is an aggregator, while the product is described as a motivation app, and those are not the same thing. Before building further I would resolve which is the wedge. The aggregator has a concrete, testable value (less cross-checking) and a clear cold-start risk (an empty feed if clubs do not link); the motivation layer is more differentiated but harder to make non-hollow. I would validate the aggregator's cold-start first (can I get a critical mass of clubs linked?), because the motivation layer is only credible once there is real tracked activity to motivate around. I would also pressure-test whether private-by-default suppresses sharing so much that the public feed stays thin.

    Product requirements

    Skin Recipe Genie

    Turns how your skin feels into personalized DIY skincare recipes from pantry ingredients.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    People want simple, natural skin care, not a 10-step routine.
    North star
    60% of visitors generate at least one recipe (target)

    Skin Recipe Genie

    Live as Glow DIY.

    Describe how your skin looks today and get personalized DIY skincare recipes from simple pantry ingredients.

    The 30-second version. Skincare advice is generic and products are expensive, while skin changes day to day. Skin Recipe Genie lets a user describe their skin in their own words, or pick a common state such as "oily T-zone with breakouts" or "red and sensitive after sun", and returns tailored recipes made from kitchen ingredients. Built solo with AI-assisted development, with AI-generated recipes and clear safety guidance.

    Live: https://skin-recipe-whisperer.lovable.app

    Problem

    People want simple, natural care for how their skin looks right now, not a 10-step routine. DIY recipes online are not matched to the person and rarely include safety guidance.

    How it works

    • A free-text box (up to 500 characters) or one-tap examples: dry and flaky cheeks, oily T-zone with breakouts, dull and tired, red and sensitive after sun.

    • AI returns personalized recipes using pantry ingredients, matched to the described condition.

    Who it is for

    Adults who want simple, low-cost skin care made from ingredients they already have, adjusted to how their skin feels today.

    Requirements (live)

    • Describe your skin in your own words or pick a common state.
    • Get tailored recipes from kitchen ingredients, with steps and timing.
    • Safety guidance on every recipe, including a patch-test reminder.

    Key decisions

    • Describe, do not diagnose. Users describe how skin looks today, which keeps the product cosmetic.

    • Safety in the flow. Every result carries patch-test guidance and a pointer to a dermatologist for persistent concerns.

    • Pantry only. Ingredients people already own lower cost and friction.

    Non-goals

    • No treatment for skin conditions; cosmetic use only.
    • No product sales or affiliate links.
    • No photo-based skin diagnosis.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 60% of visitors generate at least one recipe, 25% generate a second, and 70% rate suggestions useful.

    What I would do next

    Saved recipes and an ingredient filter for allergies.

    Risks and guardrails

    • Restrict prompts to cosmetic suggestions, require a patch test, and redirect medical-sounding symptoms or persistent concerns to a qualified clinician. Patch-test guidance shows in the app today; enforcing it and the medical redirect in every generated recipe is next. Pantry ingredients can still irritate skin; no treatment or cure is promised.

    Trade-off

    AI-generated recipes over a curated library: broad input coverage wins for a prototype. Rejected alternative: a fixed small library. Cost: less consistent and less-reviewed recipes; curation and safety review are needed before broad use.

    Validation and next test

    The next step is a short survey with friends who try it.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    AI recipes can suggest ingredients that commonly irritate skin, such as citrus or cinnamon, and the same request gives a different recipe each time, so a favorite is hard to repeat. I would add an ingredient block list and a way to save a recipe.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Put safety guardrails into the core flow of an AI product, not a footer.
    • Made a personal, changing need (skin today) the input instead of a fixed skin type.

    Product requirements

    Nutrition Fix

    Pick a common symptom, like bloating or brain fog, and see which foods to lean on and which to avoid, in English and Spanish.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Food advice for everyday symptoms is long, contradictory and mostly in English.
    North star
    60% of visitors open at least one condition (target)

    Nutrition Fix

    A bilingual, symptom-first food guide: choose what you are feeling and get a short list of foods to eat and foods to avoid.

    The 30-second version. When people feel bloated, foggy or run down, they search the internet and get long, conflicting articles. Nutrition Fix starts from the symptom instead of the nutrient. A user picks one of 15 common conditions, from acid reflux and fatigue to cold sores and joint stiffness, and sees about seven foods to lean on and six to avoid, each with a one-line reason. It works in English and Spanish and makes no medical claims. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://nutricionalamano.lovable.app

    Problem

    Everyday symptoms like bloating, headaches or poor sleep often have a food angle, but the advice online is long, contradictory and written in English. People who cook for their families, including Spanish-speaking households, want a quick, trustworthy answer to one question: what should I eat for this?

    Who it is for

    Adults managing common, non-urgent symptoms who want practical food choices, and Spanish-speaking families who are underserved by English-only wellness tools.

    How it works

    1. Choose a condition from a grid of 15, each with a plain-language description.
    2. See two short lists: "Eat this" and "Avoid this," each item with a one-line reason.
    3. Switch between Spanish and English at any time with one tap.
    4. Every screen carries a reminder that this is general wellness information, not medical advice.

    Requirements (live)

    • 15 common conditions, each with a plain description.
    • "Eat this" and "Avoid this" lists, each food with a one-line reason.
    • Full English and Spanish with a one-tap switch.
    • "Not medical advice" on every page.

    Key decisions

    • Symptom first, not nutrient first. People think "I feel bloated," not "I need magnesium," so the entry point matches how users describe the problem.
    • Short lists with one reason each. About seven foods to eat and six to avoid, so the answer fits on one screen and can be used at the grocery store.
    • Bilingual from the start. Spanish is a full first-class language, not a later translation, because the audience includes Spanish-speaking households.
    • Curated content, not live AI. Every list is written and reviewed in advance, trading open-ended coverage for consistency and safety.

    Safety trade-off: the app covers common, non-urgent symptoms only and never suggests doses or supplements. Anything that sounds urgent belongs with a doctor, and the footer says so on every page.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 60% of visitors open at least one condition, 30% open two or more, and 25% of sessions use Spanish.

    What I would do next

    A saved shopping list built from the "eat this" foods, simple recipes for each condition, and a short source note for every food so users can see where each claim comes from.

    Validation and next test

    Next: a test with five Spanish-speaking and five English-speaking users, asking which condition they would open first and whether the lists change what they buy that week.

    Non-goals

    • No diagnosis, meal plans or calorie targets.
    • No supplement recommendations.
    • No accounts; one symptom, one answer.

    Risks

    • Someone with a real condition treats a food list as treatment. Mitigation: a clear not-medical-advice line and a nudge to talk to a clinician when symptoms persist.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    The lists say what to eat and avoid, but not always why for that person, and there is no source note per food yet. I would add a one-line source for every food before adding more symptoms.

    Trade-off

    Curated lists instead of live AI answers. Rejected alternative: generate advice on the fly. Cost: fewer symptoms covered, accepted so every answer is consistent and reviewed.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Started from the user's symptom, not the nutrient.
    • Chose curated content over live AI to keep a health product consistent and safe.

    Product requirements

    Mood Food Match

    Foods, adaptogens, and nutrients matched to how you feel or an illness you are navigating, in English and Spanish.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Nutrition advice is organized by nutrient, but people live their day by mood.
    North star
    70% of visitors submit a mood (target)

    Mood Food Match

    Live as Mood Nourish.

    Tell it how you feel and get foods, adaptogens and nutrients that support that state, with simple meal ideas, in English and Spanish.

    The 30-second version. When people feel tired, anxious or foggy they reach for whatever is closest. Mood Food Match turns a feeling into specific foods rich in the adaptogens, vitamins and nutrients associated with supporting that state, plus easy meal ideas. Ten one-tap moods or free text, bilingual. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://moodfoodmatch.lovable.app

    Problem

    Nutrition advice is organized by nutrient, but people experience their day by mood. There is no quick bridge from "I feel wired but tired" to what to eat next.

    How it works

    • Pick a mood (tired, anxious, foggy, low energy, stressed, bloated, sad, restless, wired but tired, sore) or describe it in your own words.

    • Get foods, the nutrients behind them, and simple meal ideas.

    • Toggle English or Spanish.

    Who it is for

    People who want practical food ideas for how they feel today, including Spanish-speaking families.

    Requirements (live)

    • Ten one-tap moods or free text.
    • Specific foods and easy meal ideas for that mood, with the nutrients behind them.
    • Full English and Spanish.

    Key decisions

    • Start from the feeling. The input is a mood, not a nutrient, which matches how people think.

    • Wellness inspiration, not treatment. Clear "not medical advice" framing.

    • Bilingual. Built for Spanish-speaking family members as well as English speakers.

    Non-goals

    • No calorie counting, diets or weight goals.
    • Not nutrition treatment or medical advice.
    • No accounts needed to get a suggestion.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 70% submit a mood, 30% use free text, and 15% return within 7 days.

    What I would do next

    Save favorite foods and connect to Recipe Health so a mood can lead to a full recipe.

    Language and trade-off

    Spanish output is prompted in Spanish rather than translated afterward. Ten fixed moods lower starting effort, while free text catches feelings outside the list. Rejected alternative: free text only. Cost: maintaining both routes and avoiding a falsely exhaustive taxonomy.

    Risks

    Adaptogen and nutrition suggestions are inspiration, not treatment. Copy must avoid promises to treat anxiety, illness or treatment side effects and direct medication or dietary questions to the care team. A disclaimer shows today; reviewing generated outputs against it is next.

    Validation and next test

    Built for Spanish-speaking family as well as English speakers. Next: a test with three Spanish-speaking users.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    The first Spanish results had missing accents and stray English words. I fixed it in November 2026 by prompting directly in Spanish instead of translating. Ten fixed moods also miss mixed feelings, so free text matters more than I expected; I would surface it next to the mood buttons instead of below them.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Organized nutrition around how people experience their day, not around nutrients.
    • Designed bilingual from the start, generating Spanish directly rather than translating.

    Peace and Pose

    A yoga pose and an affirmation matched to your mood, with a Tai Chi mode.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    The gap between "I should move" and moving is decision fatigue.
    North star
    80% of visitors request a pose (target)

    Peace and Pose

    Share how you feel and get one yoga pose to meet you there, plus an affirmation to carry with you.

    The 30-second version. Yoga apps offer long classes when someone has two minutes and a specific feeling. Peace and Pose asks how you feel right now (anxious, tired, stressed, sad, restless, unmotivated, grateful, overwhelmed) and suggests one pose and one affirmation. A Tai Chi mode offers an alternative practice. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://pose-and-peace.lovable.app

    Problem

    The gap between "I should move" and actually moving is decision fatigue. Choosing a class is harder than doing one pose.

    How it works

    • One-tap feelings or free text.

    • One yoga pose matched to the feeling, with an affirmation.

    • A "Prefer Tai Chi?" path for a gentler practice.

    Who it is for

    People with two minutes and a specific feeling who want to move, not take a full class.

    Requirements (live)

    • Pick how you feel from eight options.
    • Get one pose with clear instructions and one affirmation.
    • A Tai Chi mode as an alternative practice.

    Key decisions

    • One pose, not a class. The smallest possible action is the one people take.

    • Body safety. "Move gently. Skip anything that doesn't feel right in your body."

    • Pair movement with words. The affirmation makes the pause emotional as well as physical.

    Non-goals

    • Not a full yoga course or class replacement.
    • No video or pose correction.
    • No accounts or progress tracking in V1.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 80% of visitors request a pose, 30% request a second, and 20% return within a week.

    What I would do next

    Short pose illustrations and a three-pose sequence option.

    Known limitation and relationship

    Poses are described in text without illustrations yet, so a beginner may not know the alignment. The /tai-chi mode uses the same engine as the separate Spanish-first Tai Chi app, which offers three postures rather than one.

    Trade-off

    AI-generated pose choice over a fixed eight-feeling/eight-pose mapping. Rejected alternative: one fixed pose per mood. Coverage for free text wins for the prototype; inconsistent suitability is the cost, requiring conservative suggestions and clear opt-outs.

    Validation and next test

    Next: a beginner alignment test.

    Risks

    • A beginner strains a muscle following a pose described only in text. Mitigation: gentle beginner poses and a stop-if-it-hurts note on every pose.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Text-only poses are hard to follow, and one pose per mood felt thin as a practice. That is why V2, Tai Chi, moved to a short sequence of three postures. Next I would add a simple drawing for each pose.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Cut a category (yoga classes) down to the smallest useful unit: one pose.
    • Paired movement with words to make a two-minute practice feel complete.

    Product requirements

    Tai Chi

    A few minutes of calm movement at home, in Spanish, matched to how you feel right now.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Spanish speakers who want a short, gentle practice at home have few simple options.
    North star
    Share of visitors who complete all three postures

    Version history: V1 was Peace and Pose (English, one pose). Tai Chi is V2: Spanish first, three postures, same engine.

    Tai Chi

    Problem

    Most guided movement content is in English and assumes a gym or a class. Spanish speakers who want a short, gentle practice at home have few simple options.

    Who it is for

    Spanish speakers who want a few minutes of calm movement at home, matched to how they feel right now.

    What it does today

    You tap one of eight feelings (for example ansioso, cansado, triste) or describe it in your own words. The app suggests three Tai Chi postures with step by step instructions and hold times, then closes with an affirmation. Everything is in Spanish.

    Safety

    Each sequence reminds you to move gently, skip anything that does not feel right, and stop if you feel pain, dizziness or shortness of breath. It is gentle movement, not medical treatment.

    Evidence

    A 2010 systematic review and meta-analysis in BMC Complementary and Alternative Medicine (Wang and colleagues) found Tai Chi was associated with improvements in stress, anxiety, depression and mood, while calling for more rigorous trials. I use it as support for gentle practice, not as a medical claim. Read the review.

    Out of scope

    • Medical advice.
    • Video instruction.
    • Accounts.

    How I would measure it

    Share of visitors who complete all three postures, and repeat use within a week.

    What I would do next

    A follow along timer that walks through each posture, and a short note on adapting postures for sitting.

    Yoga Finder

    A tracker of free and intro-offer yoga classes on the Upper West Side, refreshed on demand; scheduled daily scans are planned.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Free and low-cost yoga classes are scattered across small sites and PDFs.
    North star
    10 new classes found per week (target)

    Yoga Finder

    Live as UWS Free Yoga Tracker.

    An on-demand scan of free and intro-offer yoga classes on the Upper West Side, in one list with notifications.

    The 30-second version. Free and low-cost yoga in New York exists, but it is scattered across church bulletins, library calendars, park programs, studio intro offers and university listings. Yoga Finder scans those sources when Refresh is pressed; scheduled daily scans are the next step. It sorts results into Free and Intro or discounted, and can notify the user when something new appears. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live today and next

    Live today

    • One list of free and intro-offer Upper West Side classes.
    • Refresh button that runs a new scan on demand.
    • Free and Intro or discounted categories.
    • Notifications for newly found classes.

    Next

    • Scheduled daily scans, so the list is fresh without pressing Refresh.
    • Class times checked against each studio before they show.
    • More neighborhoods once the UWS list holds up.

    Live: https://uws-yoga-finder.lovable.app

    Problem

    People who want to practice on a budget have to check many small sources by hand, and most listings are posted late or buried in PDFs. The result is that free classes go half empty while people who want them never hear about them.

    Who it is for

    Upper West Side residents, students and newcomers who want regular yoga without a studio membership.

    How it works

    StepWhat happens
    ScanA scraper runs when a user presses Refresh; scheduled daily scans are the next step
    SortResults are grouped into All, Free, and Intro or discounted
    NotifyUsers can turn on notifications for new classes
    RefreshAnyone can trigger a fresh scan

    Requirements (live)

    • Scan free and intro yoga sources when Refresh is pressed.
    • Sort results into Free and Intro or discounted, with a link to each source.
    • Notify when something new appears.

    Key decisions

    • Hyperlocal first. One neighborhood done well beats a city done badly, and it keeps the source list manageable.
    • Price is the filter. Free and intro offers are the two categories this user cares about, so they are the main tabs.
    • Pull many small sources. The value is in the long tail that general class apps do not list.

    Non-goals

    • Not a booking or payment tool; each listing links to its source.
    • Not citywide at launch; Upper West Side only.
    • No paid studio memberships listed.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 10 new classes found per week, 90% of listings still accurate on class day, 30% notification opt-in and 25% weekly return rate.

    What I would do next

    Scheduled background scans so the list is never empty on arrival, a map view, and expansion to a second neighborhood.

    Known limitation

    Listings can go stale between scans. The app should show the last scan time prominently.

    Validation and next test

    The next test logs scan counts and checks selected listings against the venue before attendance.

    Risks

    • Scraping some studio sites may break their terms. Mitigation: labeled sample listings and a link back to every source.
    • Stale listings send someone to a class that moved. Mitigation: show the last scan time.

    Trade-off

    Scan on demand instead of a scheduled cache. Rejected alternative: a nightly scan. Cost: the first visit can show older results, accepted to keep the build simple until demand is proven.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Class times first showed in the wrong hours and had to be fixed to 7 AM to 8 PM Eastern. A manual Refresh also means the list is only as fresh as the last person who pressed it. Scheduled scans are the next fix.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Turned a scattered, manual search into one list with clear categories.
    • Was honest about the data boundary: sample listings until scans are scheduled.

    Product requirements

    Wanderline

    Name any neighborhood and an AI guide plans a walking tour, writes each stop, and narrates it automatically as you arrive.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    People walk past interesting places without knowing their story.
    North star
    60% of started tours reach the last stop (target)

    Wanderline

    Name a place and an AI guide plans a walking tour, writes each stop, and reads it aloud the moment you arrive.

    The 30-second version. Audio tours exist for famous museums, not for the neighborhood you are standing in. Wanderline lets a user type any place or use their location, choose what to lean into (history, architecture, food, street art, hidden gems) and a length of 3, 5 or 8 stops. AI plans the route and writes each stop, and narration starts automatically, by GPS, as the walker approaches. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://wanderline-app.lovable.app

    Problem

    Travelers and locals walk past interesting places without knowing their story. Guidebooks require reading while walking, and recorded tours cover only a few landmarks.

    Who it is for

    Travelers, new residents and curious locals who want a self-guided walk anywhere, with headphones in and phone in pocket.

    How it works

    1. Type a neighborhood or use your current location.
    2. Choose a focus (history, architecture, food, street art, hidden gems) and a length (3, 5 or 8 stops).
    3. AI plans the route on a live map and writes a script for each stop.
    4. Narration plays automatically when you come within range of each stop.

    Requirements (live)

    • Plan a 3, 5 or 8 stop route from any place name or current location.
    • Write a short script for each stop, shaped by the chosen focus.
    • Narrate automatically by GPS as the walker approaches, with an adjustable trigger distance.
    • Preview a sample tour at a desk, without GPS.

    Key decisions

    • Location-triggered audio. The walker never has to look at the screen; the tour talks when you arrive.
    • Adjustable trigger distance. Phone GPS drifts 10 to 30 meters between tall buildings, so the trigger radius can be tuned on the tour screen.
    • Interest-led routes. The same street becomes a different tour depending on what the user wants to notice.

    Script accuracy trade-off: AI writes each stop rather than using a curated library, giving any-place coverage at the cost of possible wrong facts about real places. Interest-led phrasing and avoiding unsupported dates are the proposed current guardrails. Planned mitigation: source-backed scripts and review for well-known sites.

    Non-goals

    • Not a turn-by-turn navigation app.
    • No historical dates or facts asserted without a source.
    • No paid tours or guide marketplace.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 60% of started tours reach the last stop, 80% of planned stops are narrated per completed tour, and 40% of users build a second tour within 30 days.

    What I would do next

    Saved and shareable tours, offline audio for weak signal, and fact-checking of generated scripts for well-known sites.

    Validation and next test

    Adjustable trigger distance is described in the PRD; a tall-building walk is the next validation. A desk-preview or sample tour must exist before "Preview any stop from a desk" is added to the live flow.

    Risks

    • The AI states a wrong historical fact. Mitigation: scripts avoid asserting dates, and each stop names its source (next).

    Trade-off

    AI-written stops instead of a curated script library. Rejected alternative: hand-written tours. Cost: uneven accuracy, accepted to cover any street on demand.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Tours needed GPS to start, which made them hard to try from a desk, so I added a sample tour preview. AI scripts also sound alike from stop to stop; I would give each stop one specific detail to look at.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Turned a vague "I want to explore" into a concrete, configurable product with three clear inputs.
    • Designed for real-world constraints: GPS drift, walkers who do not look at screens, and recruiters at a desk.

    Product requirements

    Question Architect

    Build a precise AI prompt by walking through ten structural elements, then copy it into any AI tool. Built from the precision-questioning method I learned in Columbia's AI leadership course.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Vague requests to AI tools get generic answers.
    North star
    70% of sessions end with a copied prompt (target)

    Question Architect

    A thinking tool that walks you through the ten elements of a strong prompt and assembles a precise question to use in any AI tool.

    The 30-second version. Most weak AI answers come from weak questions. Question Architect breaks a prompt into ten structural elements (role, context, task, constraints, tone, dos and don'ts, examples, output format, open-ended framing and an iteration hook) and builds the prompt live as the user fills them in. It is a thinking tool, not a chat interface: the user copies the finished question into whichever AI they trust. Built on the QI framework for executive question design from my Columbia coursework. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://think-a-prompt.lovable.app

    Problem

    People type vague requests into AI tools and get generic answers. Prompt advice is scattered across threads and rarely structured as a repeatable method.

    Who it is for

    Professionals, students and job seekers who use AI for strategy, writing and outreach and want consistently better answers.

    How it works

    • Ten guided steps, each with a short tip (for example, define a specific methodology instead of a generic "expert" label).
    • Skip any element that does not serve the task.
    • A live preview assembles the prompt and counts completed elements; one click copies it.
    • Starter modes for Product and Strategy, Job Search and Outreach, and Personal Writing.
    • Expand: develops a short request into a more complete structured prompt.
    • Audit: reviews the prompt for missing context, constraints and output requirements.
    • Split: separates a compound request into smaller prompts.

    Requirements (live)

    • Ten structural elements, each with a short explanation and an example.
    • Live prompt preview that updates as each element is filled in.
    • Load an example to see a finished prompt in one click.
    • Copy the finished prompt to use in any AI tool.

    Key decisions

    • Build the question, do not answer it. Keeping the product model-agnostic makes it useful with any AI tool.
    • Structure over volume. Ten named elements teach the method, so users improve even without the tool.
    • Skippable steps. Not every task needs all ten; the tool rewards precision, not length.

    Non-goals

    • Not a chatbot; it builds the prompt, it does not answer it.
    • No prompt marketplace or sharing feed.
    • No model-specific tuning at launch.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 70% of sessions copy a prompt, median 6 of 10 elements completed, and 30% of users returning within 14 days.

    Trade-off cost: Ten steps add friction for simple tasks; the skip control and starter modes let a short task stay short.

    Worked example

    Basic: "Help me prepare for a product interview."

    Architected: "Act as a product hiring manager. I am a founder interviewing for a senior consumer PM role. Build a 30-minute practice interview about prioritization. Use one marketplace scenario, ask for assumptions and success metrics, and return five questions plus a scoring rubric. Keep feedback direct and do not invent my work history."

    The architected prompt requests a bounded scenario and rubric instead of generic preparation advice. This is an illustrative comparison of the requested outputs, not a claim that answers were run or scored in a user test.

    What I would do next

    Saved prompt templates and a before-and-after view comparing answers to a basic prompt and an architected one.

    Risks

    • People paste private or company information into a prompt. Mitigation: nothing is stored, and a reminder to remove names and sensitive data.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Ten steps feel long for a quick question. Starter modes and skip help, but I would add a three-step fast path and keep the full ten for important prompts.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Turned a course method into a repeatable tool, not a one-off cheat sheet.
    • Chose a thinking tool over a chat interface, keeping the product focused on the user's question quality.

    Product requirements

    LabLens

    Reads a photo or PDF of your lab work, flags out-of-range values in plain language, and suggests foods and questions for your next appointment.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Lab results arrive days before the doctor can explain them.
    North star
    60% of users copy at least one question for their appointment (target)

    LabLens

    Upload a lab report and get plain-language explanations of flagged values, foods to discuss with your care team, and questions for your next appointment.

    The 30-second version. Patients and caregivers managing chronic illness, chemotherapy or immunotherapy get lab results full of numbers and abbreviations, often days before they can talk to a doctor. LabLens reads a phone photo, screenshot or PDF of the results, highlights every value outside the printed reference range, explains each one in plain language, suggests food sources to discuss with the care team, and gives tap-to-copy questions for the next appointment. It works in English and Spanish, does not save uploads, and never diagnoses. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://lab-smart-guide.lovable.app (use "Try a sample report" to see the full flow without your own labs)

    1. Problem statement

    Patient portals now release lab results the moment they are ready, often days before the appointment where a doctor explains them. For people in cancer treatment or managing a chronic condition, that gap is filled with anxiety. Caregivers search each abbreviation one by one, land on frightening or contradictory answers, and walk into the visit without the questions that would make it useful. Spanish-speaking families face the same gap with even fewer resources in their language.

    The real job is not "tell me what is wrong with me." It is "help me understand what I am looking at, so the 15 minutes with my doctor are spent on the right questions."

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Turn a raw lab report into a short, plain-language summary of what is outside the reference range.
    • Prepare the user for the appointment with specific, copyable questions.
    • Offer food sources linked to flagged values, framed as topics to discuss with the care team.
    • Work equally well in English and Spanish, from messy real-world inputs.

    Non-goals

    • No diagnosis, treatment advice, supplement dosing or risk scores.
    • No account, history or long-term storage of medical data in v1.
    • No interpretation of imaging, pathology or genetic reports.

    3. The product

    UploadPhone photo, screenshot or PDF, in English or Spanish. A built-in sample report lets anyone try the full flow without personal data.
    UnderstandEach value outside the printed reference range is flagged and explained in one or two plain sentences.
    Eat with purposeFood and nutrient sources related to the flagged values, labeled as topics to raise with the care team.
    Ask betterTap-to-copy questions for the next appointment, written so a caregiver can read them aloud.

    4. User stories

    • As a caregiver who just got a portal notification, I want to see which values are out of range and what they mean, so that I can stop searching abbreviations one by one.
    • As a patient in treatment, I want questions I can copy into my notes, so that I use my appointment time well.
    • As a Spanish-speaking family member, I want the whole experience in Spanish, so that I can help without translating medical terms myself.
    • As a cautious user, I want to know what happens to my upload, so that I can decide whether to share it.

    5. Requirements

    Must have (P0)

    • Accept photos, screenshots and PDFs; read values and their printed reference ranges.
    • Flag only values outside the range printed on the report, so the app never invents its own thresholds.
    • Plain-language explanation for every flagged value, plus copyable questions.
    • Full EN and ES toggle across every screen and output.
    • "Informational only, not medical advice" on every screen, and a plain statement of how uploads are handled.
    • A sample report so the flow can be seen without personal data.

    Should have (P1)

    • Signal low-confidence reads (for example, a blurry photo) and ask the user to check the value.
    • Export or email the questions list.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    • Prepare, do not diagnose. Every output ends in questions for the care team. Alternative rejected: a severity score, because it would invite users to act on the number instead of talking to their doctor.
    • Use the report's own reference ranges. Labs differ, so the app flags against the range printed on that report. Trade-off: if the range is unreadable, the value cannot be flagged, and the user is told so.
    • Accept messy inputs. Phone photos and screenshots are what caregivers actually have. Trade-off: more read errors than clean PDFs, which is why low-confidence signaling is a priority.
    • Bilingual from day one. Caregiving often happens in a second language. Trade-off: every explanation and question must work in both languages.
    • No storage. Uploads are analyzed and not saved to an app database or the browser. Trade-off: no history view in v1, in exchange for a simpler privacy story.
    • Food as conversation, not prescription. Food sources are framed as topics to discuss, because some foods interact with treatment.

    7. Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results.

    • North star: share of users who copy at least one question (target: 60%), the clearest sign the app prepared them for the appointment.
    • Read quality: 90% of uploads produce a complete read with every flagged value explained.
    • Preparedness: 70% of users rate "I feel more prepared for my appointment" 4 of 5 or higher in a two-question exit survey.
    • Reach: at least 25% of sessions in Spanish.

    8. Risks and mitigations

    • Misread values. A wrong number could cause unnecessary worry. Mitigation: show the value as read next to its explanation, and flag low-confidence reads.
    • Food advice that conflicts with treatment. Mitigation: frame all food content as questions for the care team, and plan a clinical review before broader use.
    • Privacy. Uploads go to an AI service for analysis. Mitigation: no app storage, a plain disclosure on the upload screen, and the sample report for anyone who prefers not to share.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    • Next: low-confidence signaling and a questions export.
    • Then: clinical review of explanations and food guidance by an oncology nurse or dietitian.
    • Later: an opt-in history view to follow one value across several reports, only with an explicit privacy design.
    • Open question: should caregivers be able to share a summary with the patient's care team directly, and what consent does that require?

    Validation and next test

    Next: five caregiver sessions using their own recent reports, measuring whether every flagged value was read correctly and which questions they actually brought to the appointment.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Photos of printed reports can misread a value, and a wrong number explained confidently is worse than no explanation. I would show the values the app read and ask the user to confirm them before explaining anything.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • User insight: reframed the job from "diagnose me" to "prepare me for my appointment."
    • Judgment in a sensitive domain: clear non-goals, conservative thresholds and safety framing on every screen.
    • Inclusive design: bilingual from day one and built for the messy inputs real caregivers have.
    • Technical fluency: an AI pipeline that reads documents and photos, with a no-storage privacy model.

    Product requirements

    Tandem

    A shared Pomodoro room for two cofounders: one timer, one rhythm, and a focus block only starts when you both press start.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Remote cofounders struggle most with starting deep work.
    North star
    The second person joins in 60% of rooms within 5 minutes (target)

    Tandem

    A shared focus room for two cofounders: one timer, one rhythm, and a block only starts when both people press start.

    The 30-second version. Cofounders who work remotely lose the quiet accountability of sitting at the same desk. Tandem recreates it: one person opens a room and shares a five-character code, the other walks in, and both run the same 50-minute focus and 10-minute break timer. A block only starts when both press start, so neither person drifts. A Solo mode keeps the timer for days alone. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://cofounder-focus-flow.lovable.app

    What is live today: Room code, Solo mode and the 50/10 timer work today. The shared task line and weekly summary are planned, not built.

    Problem

    Early-stage founders often work apart, and the hardest part of deep work is starting. Solo Pomodoro apps track time but add no social commitment, and video "body doubling" calls are heavy and distracting.

    Who it is for

    Two-person founding teams and close collaborators who want light accountability without a meeting.

    How it works

    StepWhat happens
    Open a roomEnter your name and an optional room name; get a five-character code
    Walk inYour cofounder enters the code; rooms hold exactly two people
    Start togetherThe 50/10 block begins only when both press start
    Solo modeThe same timer when you are working alone

    Requirements (live)

    • Open a room and share a five-character code.
    • Shared 50-minute focus and 10-minute break timer; a block starts only when both press start.
    • Solo mode for days alone.

    Key decisions

    • Two people, not a group. The product is a desk for a pair; if a room is full, someone is already holding your chair.
    • Mutual start. Requiring both people to press start turns the timer into a small commitment device. Cost: a block cannot begin while one person is away. The fallback is Solo mode; a suggested switch after a 2-minute wait is a proposed rule, not a confirmed automatic transition.
    • No video. Presence without a camera keeps attention on the work.

    Non-goals

    • No video or chat; presence without a camera.
    • No teams larger than two.
    • No task manager or project tracking.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 1 room opened per active pair per week, second person joins in 60% of rooms within 5 minutes, pairs complete 3 or more blocks per week, and 40% weekly pair return rate.

    What I would do next

    A shared task line for each block and a weekly summary of focus time per pair.

    Validation and next test

    The next test is paired starts and Solo fallback during a real remote work session, with interruptions recorded.

    Risks

    • The product needs two people at the same time, so it can feel empty. Mitigation: Solo mode keeps it useful alone.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Mutual start blocks the timer when one person is late, and sharing a room code outside the app adds a step. I would add a copy-link button and suggest Solo after a short wait.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Built accountability into the mechanic itself rather than relying on reminders.
    • Kept scope to one tight loop, with Solo mode as the fallback that keeps the product useful.

    Product requirements

    Vibe Check

    A dating protocol with no small talk: an AI interview writes your profile, and every match is one 15-minute video call.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Most app matches stall in chat.
    North star
    50% of matches book a call within 48 hours (target)

    Related: 15 Minutes to Love tested the core idea first: one person, one 15-minute call.

    Vibe Check

    A dating protocol with no small talk: an AI interview writes your profile, and every match is one 15-minute video call.

    The 30-second version. Dating apps reward endless messaging that rarely turns into a real meeting. Vibe Check replaces messaging with a single rule: meet on video for fifteen minutes. An AI confidant interviews each user and writes their profile, the feed only shows people vetted against them, and every match becomes a 15-minute call with a hard timer. Afterward both people privately choose Keep or Toss. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://vibe-check-dating-club.lovable.app

    Problem

    Most app matches stall in chat. Users spend weeks texting people they would have ruled out in a few minutes face to face, and writing a good profile is hard.

    Who it is for

    Busy daters who want to know quickly whether there is chemistry, and who are tired of small talk.

    How it works

    StepWhat happens
    InterviewAn AI confidant asks about your last relationship, your humor and what you cannot tolerate, then writes your profile
    Curated feedYou only see people the system has vetted against you; a like means "I will give them fifteen minutes"
    The callOn a match, three time windows appear; pick one and a video room opens with a 15:00 countdown that ends on time
    Decide in privateBoth tap Keep or Toss; a mutual Keep reveals phone numbers, anything else closes the match quietly

    Requirements (live)

    • AI interview that writes each profile.
    • Feed limited to people vetted against the user.
    • Every match becomes a 15-minute video call with a hard timer.
    • Private Keep or Toss after each call; Report and Block save.

    Key decisions

    • One rule, no messaging. Removing chat removes the main place matches go to die.
    • A hard timer. Fifteen minutes is long enough to know and short enough to say yes to.
    • Private, mutual decisions. No one is left guessing, and no one sees a rejection.
    • AI writes the profile. The interview lowers the effort of joining and makes profiles more honest.

    Non-goals

    • No in-app messaging.
    • No swiping or endless browsing.
    • No public profiles or ratings of other users.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 50% of matches book a call within 48 hours, 80% of booked calls complete, 25% mutual Keep rate, and 30% booking a second call within a week.

    Safety decision: The app enforces the hard timer, private decisions and no phone-number reveal until mutual Keep. Reporting, identity checks and an in-call end button are launch blockers, not optional polish.

    No-messaging cost: Users cannot ask a logistics question or negotiate by chat. Three time windows limit scheduling friction, but a rescheduling fallback still needs validation.

    What is live today: The AI interview, curated feed, call booking and private Keep or Toss decision, plus a public demo at /demo. The next test is a full two-person call from match to decision.

    What I would do next

    Calendar sync for call windows, photo upload instead of photo links, and safety features such as reporting and identity checks before launch.

    Validation and next test

    Next: a call-versus-texting survey and a two-person safety check.

    Risks

    • Safety on video calls with strangers. Mitigation: hard timer, private decisions and Report and Block; identity checks are a launch blocker.
    • Not enough people in one place for good matches. Mitigation: launch one neighborhood at a time.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    A full call cannot be tested without two real accounts, so the demo shows sample profiles and the call screen rather than a live match. I would run a two-person test before adding features.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Replaced the core behavior of a category (messaging) instead of adding features on top of it.
    • Treated safety as a launch requirement: reporting, blocking and private decisions.

    Product requirements

    Wisdom Circle

    Expands Wisdom Lens to 16 traditions, each in its own voice, with a 2,000-character input.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Most reflection tools draw on a narrow set of Western and Asian traditions.
    North star
    50% of visitors open a circle (target)

    Version history: V1 was Wisdom Lens (five traditions). Wisdom Circle is V2.

    Wisdom Circle

    Wisdom Lens left religious, communal and Indigenous perspectives out; Wisdom Circle expands to 16 traditions, each in its own voice, with a 2,000-character input.

    The 30-second version. Wisdom Circle widens the idea behind Wisdom Lens. A user shares something they are living through and receives a circle of perspectives, from Stoic, Christian and Buddhist to Ubuntu, Existentialist and Indigenous traditions, each written in its own voice. A Wisdom Map shows the traditions together. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://wisdomcircle.lovable.app

    Problem

    Wisdom Lens drew only on five mostly Western and Asian philosophical schools. Many people find meaning in religious, communal and Indigenous traditions that were missing.

    Who it is for

    People from many cultural and faith backgrounds facing family conflict, career doubt, aging or betrayal who want to feel less alone with it.

    How it works

    • Describe what you are living through (up to 2,000 characters), or start from a prompt such as "A friend betrayed my trust and asked for forgiveness."
    • Open the circle to receive perspectives from 16 traditions, each with a guiding question.
    • Explore the Wisdom Map inside the live app to see how the traditions relate.

    Requirements (live)

    • Share a situation in up to 2,000 characters.
    • Receive perspectives from 16 traditions, each in its own voice.
    • A Wisdom Map that shows the traditions together.
    • A sample circle for first-time visitors.

    Key decisions

    • Breadth over depth. V2 trades five deep lenses for a wider, more inclusive circle.
    • Each tradition in its own voice. Tone matters as much as content when the topic is personal.
    • Honest about AI. A note on every result says reflections are AI-generated starting points, not authoritative interpretations.

    Non-goals

    • No ranking of traditions or claims of authority.
    • Not religious instruction or therapy.
    • No user comments or debate between traditions.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 50% of visitors open a circle, average 3 traditions expanded per session, and 20% return in two weeks.

    What I would do next

    Review by scholars or practitioners of each tradition, and let users follow the traditions that resonate most.

    Risks

    Misrepresenting living religious traditions can cause harm. The AI disclosure is live on every result; practitioner review of tradition-specific voice and interpretation is planned, not completed.

    Validation and next test

    Expanding the set of traditions was a coverage decision. Next: test which traditions people return to.

    Trade-off

    Breadth over depth. Rejected alternative: keep V1's five deep lenses. Cost: each tradition gets less space, accepted because who was missing mattered more.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Sixteen voices is a lot to read on one screen. I would let people pick two or three traditions to go deeper on after the first circle.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Shipped a V2 that answered a real gap in V1: whose wisdom was missing.
    • Handled culturally sensitive content with clear framing: perspectives, not advice.

    NYC Event Finder

    NYC events from Eventbrite, Time Out, Meetup and Partiful in one place, filtered by date, borough, category and price.

    Live ↗
    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    NYC event listings are split across many platforms.
    North star
    20 relevant events per search (target)

    NYC Event Finder

    Live as NYC Events.

    NYC events from Eventbrite, Time Out, Meetup and Partiful in one place, filtered by date, borough, category and price.

    The 30-second version. Finding something to do in New York means checking four or five apps that each show a slice of the city. NYC Event Finder is designed to pull events from Eventbrite, Time Out, Meetup and Partiful into one feed, with quick filters for today, this weekend or the next seven days, borough, category and free only. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live today and next

    Live today

    • One feed with filters for date (today, this weekend, next 7 days), borough, category and free only.
    • Sample events that show the experience end to end.

    Next

    • Connect live sources (Eventbrite, Time Out, Meetup, Partiful), starting with those whose terms allow it.
    • Refresh that pulls current events from connected sources.
    • Saved searches for the weekend plan.

    Live: https://nyc-events-spy.lovable.app

    Problem

    Event discovery is fragmented by platform. Each source has its own audience and format, so people miss good events or give up and stay home.

    Who it is for

    New Yorkers and newcomers who want a quick answer to "what is happening this weekend," especially on a budget.

    How it works

    • Pick a window: Today, This weekend, Next 7 days, or custom dates.
    • Filter by borough (Manhattan, Brooklyn, Queens, Bronx, Staten Island) and category (Music, Food and Drink, Art, Tech, Nightlife, Wellness, Community, Other).
    • Toggle Free only.
    • Browse sample events today; live sources are next.

    Requirements (live)

    • One feed of events, built for four platforms.
    • Filters for today, this weekend or the next 7 days, borough, category and free only.
    • Sample events until live sources are connected.

    Key decisions

    • Aggregate, do not host. Each event links back to its source, so the product stays light and current.
    • Time first. Most people start with "when," so the date window is the first control.
    • Free as a first-class filter. Cost is often the deciding factor in a city this expensive.

    Non-goals

    • Not a ticketing platform; events link back to their source.
    • No user-posted events at launch.
    • No social feed or friend activity.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 20 relevant events per search, 30% source click-through, 40% of searches use Free only, and 25% weekly return rate.

    What I would do next

    Remove duplicates across sources, save favorite categories, and send a weekly digest.

    Known limitation and trade-off

    Source scraping can break when Eventbrite, Time Out, Meetup or Partiful changes access or markup; source-by-source success has not been checked. Cross-source de-duplication remains next scope. On-demand scraping was chosen for a fresh prototype over a scheduled cache; cost: slower, fragile refreshes. A scheduled cache would load faster but can be stale.

    Validation and next test

    Next: run weekly scans for a month and track how many events I actually attend.

    Risks

    • Scraping some event sites may break their terms. Mitigation: sample events are labeled, and live sources are added only where terms allow.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Four sources was too much for a first version, and the scraper was never connected, so today the app shows sample events only. I would start with one source whose terms allow it and prove people use the weekend filter before adding more.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Designed aggregation around a real decision: "what can I do this weekend?".
    • Flagged the legal and terms-of-service limits of scraping before building on them.

    Product requirements

    Wisdom Lens

    Share what is on your mind and get five contrasting perspectives from Stoicism, Buddhism, Existentialism, Taoism and Aristotelian ethics. Not advice, a way to think.

    Live ↗
    Live, V1 of Wisdom CircleSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    When stuck, people hold on to one interpretation of their situation.
    North star
    60% of visitors complete a reflection (target)

    Version history: this is V1. It evolved into Wisdom Circle (V2).

    Wisdom Lens

    Live as Philosopher in Your Pocket.

    Share what is on your mind and see it through five philosophical traditions. Not advice, a way to think.

    The 30-second version. Most apps answer "What should I do?" Wisdom Lens answers a different question: "How else could I think about this?" A user writes what is on their mind, and the app reframes it through Stoicism, Buddhism, Existentialism, Taoism and Aristotelian ethics, each with a small practice to try. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://philosopher-in-your-pocket.lovable.app

    Problem

    When people are stuck, they tend to hold one interpretation of their situation. Advice apps and chatbots add more instructions, when what helps is a wider view.

    Who it is for

    Reflective adults working through a decision, a regret or a hard feeling who want perspective, not prescriptions.

    How it works

    1. Write what is on your mind (up to 800 characters), or tap a starter such as "I feel behind in life" or "Should I quit my job?"
    2. Receive five contrasting perspectives, one per tradition.
    3. Each perspective ends with a small practice that tradition would recommend.

    Requirements (live)

    • Write what is on your mind.
    • Reframe it through five philosophical traditions, each with a small practice to try.
    • Optional sign in; core use needs no account.

    Key decisions

    • Reframe, do not advise. The product never tells the user what to do; the goal is to loosen the grip of a single story.
    • Five traditions, side by side. Contrast is the feature; seeing views disagree is what opens thinking.
    • Clear boundaries. The app states that it is not advice and not therapy.

    Non-goals

    • No advice or instructions; perspectives only.
    • Not therapy or crisis support.
    • No ranking of which tradition is right.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: 60% complete a reflection, 30% try a suggested practice, and 20% return within two weeks.

    What I would do next

    Save reflections to a private journal and let users choose which traditions to include.

    Risks and V2

    The model can invent or misattribute quotations. Mitigation: a visible "AI-generated, not an authoritative reading" line on every result, the same disclosure Wisdom Circle shows. Wisdom Circle is V2, expanding beyond the original five traditions.

    Validation and next test

    Next: a test with five people on a real dilemma, asking which lens helped most.

    Trade-off

    Five traditions side by side instead of one best answer. Rejected alternative: a single recommended view. Cost: more reading, accepted because contrast is what opens thinking.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Five mostly Western and Asian schools left out religious, communal and Indigenous perspectives. That gap is why I built Wisdom Circle as V2.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Reframed the product question from "what should I do?" to "how else could I see this?".
    • Learned from V1 and expanded it into Wisdom Circle.

    Product requirements

    DevaCup Applicator

    A painless menstrual-cup applicator that lowers the biggest barrier to switching to a reusable cup.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    Many would-be menstrual cup users quit because insertion is intimidating.
    North star
    75% comfortable first-try insertion (target)

    DevaCup Applicator

    One-line summary: A painless menstrual-cup applicator with a pre-folded chamber, soft iris tip, anatomical angle guide, and depth-stop ring that makes cup insertion confident, comfortable, and repeatable.

    Problem

    Menstrual cups are sustainable and cost-effective, but a large share of would-be users abandon them because insertion is intimidating, messy, and uncomfortable. Folding the cup by hand is fiddly, the angle and depth are guesswork, and the learning curve drives people back to disposables. There is no purpose-built applicator that removes the fear and mess from the first and every insertion.

    Target users

    • Primary: People who menstruate and want to switch to or stay with a cup but are deterred by insertion difficulty or discomfort.
    • Secondary: First-time cup users (often younger) who need a confidence-building tool to adopt at all.
    • Tertiary: People with dexterity limitations or sensitivity who find manual folding impractical.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Make cup insertion painless, clean, and repeatable on the first try.
    • Remove the angle and depth guesswork that causes leaks and discomfort.
    • Reduce cup abandonment by lowering the learning curve.

    Non-goals

    • We are not redesigning the cup itself; we make existing-style cups easier to insert.
    • We are not making clinical or contraceptive claims.
    • We are not targeting removal in v1; the focus is insertion.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • Pre-folded chamber that holds the cup in an insertion-ready fold so the user does not fold by hand.
    • Soft iris tip that opens gently to ease entry and minimize discomfort.
    • Anatomical angle guide molded into the body to direct correct insertion angle.
    • Depth-stop ring that prevents over-insertion and signals correct placement.
    • Body-safe, medical-grade materials with a reusable, easy-to-clean design.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Size and firmness variants to fit common cup brands and anatomies.
    • Travel and storage case with hygienic drying.
    • Clear illustrated and video onboarding for first use.
    • Single-use sanitary applicator variant for travel or shared facilities.

    P2 (later)

    • Companion app guidance and reminders (cleaning cadence, sizing help).
    • Removal-assist accessory.
    • Retail and subscription bundles with compatible cups.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Target: 75% comfortable first-try insertion; not observed. Successful first-try insertion rate among new cup users (self-reported comfortable, leak-free placement on the first attempt).

    Supporting metrics

    • Cup adoption and 3-month continuation rate among applicator users.
    • Reported pain or discomfort scores during insertion.
    • Net Promoter Score and repeat purchase or reorder of consumable parts.
    • Return and defect rates.

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Safety and regulatory: an insertion device for an intimate area carries compliance obligations. Mitigate with medical-grade materials, biocompatibility testing, and appropriate regulatory pathway review before launch.
    • Anatomical variability: one geometry will not fit everyone. Mitigate with size and firmness variants and an adjustable depth-stop.
    • Cup compatibility: cups vary in size and shape. Mitigate by designing to common dimensions first and publishing a clear compatibility list.
    • User trust and education: people fear getting it wrong. Mitigate with strong onboarding, illustrated guidance, and a reassuring depth and angle feedback design.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Months 1 to 4): Prototype and validation: build functional prototypes, run usability and comfort testing with a recruited panel, and confirm material safety and the regulatory pathway.
    • Phase 2 (Months 5 to 8): Pilot production: manufacture a limited run in one or two sizes, launch a small direct-to-consumer cohort, and measure first-try success and continuation.
    • Phase 3 (Months 9 to 14): Scale: add size and firmness variants, travel case, and onboarding content, then expand distribution and explore cup bundles, optimizing for first-try insertion success.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • Reusable applicator over disposable. Rejected alternative: single-use plastic. Cost: cleaning and durability tests, accepted for lower recurring waste.
    • Applicator for existing cups over redesigning the cup. Rejected alternative: a proprietary cup system. Cost: compatibility constraints; existing cups keep the first scope narrow.

    Regulatory path and evidence

    The likely US pathway needs device-class confirmation with a regulatory reviewer; this applicator is not assumed exempt or cleared. Material biocompatibility, mucosal-contact, cleaning, mechanical integrity and usability testing shape Phase 1 before any human-use launch. A 2019 systematic review reports that 73% of 1,144 participants across 13 studies wished to continue cup use, with familiarization often needed; this is not an applicator success rate or an unaided 40% baseline.

    Concept bill of materials

    Proposed components, not a manufactured artifact: a medical-grade silicone soft iris tip, reusable polymer pre-folded cup chamber, molded angle guide, adjustable depth-stop ring, removable pusher and ventilated storage case. All materials, dimensions and compatible cups need testing before use.

    Product requirements

    Vela

    Discreet safety jewelry with location on trigger for sharing location and sending silent alerts.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    People who feel unsafe want a way to signal for help that is instant and invisible.
    North star
    99% of triggered alerts reach a contact with an accurate location (target)

    Vela

    One-line summary: Discreet personal-safety jewelry with location on trigger and a one-touch panic signal, so wearers can call for help without anyone seeing a device.

    Problem

    People who feel unsafe walking home, on dates, commuting late, or traveling alone want a way to signal for help that is instant and invisible. Phones are slow to reach in a crisis, obvious safety devices can escalate a threat, and existing wearables look clinical or unappealing. There is no everyday-wearable that looks like jewelry, blends in, and silently sends location and an alert when it matters.

    Target users

    • Primary: Women and other safety-conscious adults who navigate solo situations (commuting, dating, nightlife, travel) and want discreet protection.
    • Secondary: Caregivers and family members buying for loved ones (students, older relatives).
    • Tertiary: Lone or field workers who want a low-profile duress signal.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Let a wearer trigger a help signal instantly and discreetly.
    • Deliver accurate location to chosen contacts or a monitoring service.
    • Make the device attractive enough to wear every day.

    Non-goals

    • We are not a replacement for emergency services or a security guarantee.
    • We are not building continuous always-on tracking that compromises wearer privacy.
    • We are not launching a full medical-alert clinical product in v1.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • One-touch (or press-and-hold) panic trigger built discreetly into the jewelry.
    • Phone-derived location captured and transmitted only on trigger, through a Bluetooth-linked companion app.
    • Alert dispatch to pre-set trusted contacts with location and a help message.
    • Companion app for setup, contact management, and alert history.
    • Rechargeable battery with multi-day life and a low-battery reminder.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Live location sharing for a session (walking home) with auto-stop.
    • Discreet check-in and "I'm safe" cancel to reduce false alarms.
    • Multiple form factors (necklace, bracelet, ring) on shared internals.
    • Audible or haptic confirmation that the alert was sent.

    P2 (later)

    • Optional 24/7 professional monitoring and emergency dispatch subscription.
    • Fall or inactivity detection for at-risk wearers.
    • Two-way communication and an in-app safety network of nearby trusted contacts.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Successful alert deliveries (a triggered alert that reaches at least one contact with accurate location), reflecting the product doing its core job when needed. Target: 99% of triggered alerts delivered with an accurate location.

    Supporting metrics

    • Daily wear rate (percentage of owners wearing it on a given day).
    • Trigger-to-delivery latency and location accuracy.
    • False-alarm rate and cancel-success rate.
    • Battery satisfaction and charge adherence.

    Key risks and mitigations

    • False alarms: accidental triggers erode trust and annoy contacts. Mitigate with a deliberate trigger gesture, a brief cancel window, and clear confirmation feedback.
    • Reliability when it matters most: a failed alert is catastrophic. Mitigate with redundant connectivity, delivery confirmations, and rigorous field testing.
    • Privacy: location on trigger raises legitimate concerns. Mitigate by making location capture trigger-based by default, encrypting data, and giving the wearer full control over sharing.
    • Wearability versus tech: beautiful jewelry constrains electronics. Mitigate with a modular internals platform across multiple designs and careful industrial design tradeoffs.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Months 1 to 5): Prototype and trust testing: validate the trigger, GPS accuracy, and alert delivery in hardware prototypes; test the cancel flow to drive down false alarms.
    • Phase 2 (Months 6 to 10): Limited launch: ship one or two flagship designs to an early cohort, prove daily wear and successful alert delivery, and refine battery life.
    • Phase 3 (Months 11 to 18): Expand and monetize: add live session sharing, more form factors, and an optional professional monitoring subscription, optimizing for successful alert deliveries and daily wear.

    Architecture decision

    V1 is proposed as phone-tethered Bluetooth jewelry, not standalone cellular GPS. Rejected alternative: cellular jewelry that works without a phone. Bluetooth is cheaper and smaller; cost: an available charged phone, permissions and connectivity are required. Cellular would improve independence but needs a larger battery and a service plan. Neither architecture is built.

    Targets

    Proposed targets, not observed: 7-day battery life, trigger-to-contact delivery under 10 seconds with connected phone and network, false alarms under 1% of triggers, and $99 device price. These targets are not safety guarantees.

    Competitive context

    ProductApproachVela hypothesis
    invisaWearPhone-linked safety jewelryA consent-first location-on-trigger flow with explicit phone dependency
    ResQ ShaktiSafety necklace with alerts and optional monitoringStart with trusted contacts, not a monitoring subscription

    ResQ vendor description. These are comparison hypotheses, not independent reliability results or proven unique features.

    Validation and next test

    Next: field tests of alert reliability before any launch.

    Product requirements

    Social Dating

    A social-listening dating concept that infers compatibility from how people engage and express themselves.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    Swipe apps reward volume over compatibility.
    North star
    25% of concept-page visitors join early access (target)

    Social Dating

    Dating rebuilt on taste, not on swiping.

    The 30-second version. Social Dating is a consent-first dating concept that matches people on revealed cultural behavior (the music they return to, the films and writers they share, the causes they show up for, the people they already have in common) instead of on a self-reported profile. Built as a concept landing page and interaction model, not a live app, to pressure-test one bet: that taste is a more honest and durable compatibility signal than another personality quiz. The PM work on display here is problem framing in a crowded, profitable, but deeply unhappy market, a regulatory and AI timing thesis, and an honest enumeration of the riskiest assumptions before a line of production code gets written. This concept is distinct from the archived Kindred Spirits document and the Closer conversation-deck build.

    Timeline: Weekend concept artifact, in early validation.

    1. Problem statement

    The swipe era is collapsing under its own weight. A decade of swiping trained people to treat each other as interchangeable, and the result is mass burnout and a slow exodus even as the apps keep printing money. They are removed as research findings. People are not done with dating. They are done with how it feels.

    The market gap is structural, not cosmetic. Every incumbent asks you to self-report who you are, so you write a profile that is aspirational, edited, and a little dishonest, and then you are matched on fiction. The archetypes:

    ApproachWhat it matches onWhy it falls short
    Swipe apps (Tinder, Bumble, Hinge)Photos and a short self-written bioOptimizes for volume and time-on-app, trains interchangeability, burns people out
    Quiz/algorithm apps (eHarmony, OkCupid)Self-reported answers to questionnairesPeople are poor at describing what they actually want; answers are aspirational
    Matchmakers / conciergeHuman judgmentHigh quality, but expensive and unscalable

    Social Dating's wager is that there is room for a fourth approach: match on revealed behavior, the cultural fingerprint people already leave online, read with permission rather than scraped.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • (User) Replace performance with recognition: make the first thing you learn about someone what moves them, not how they angled a camera.
    • (User) Reduce dating burnout by surfacing a small, curated set of high-resonance introductions rather than an infinite stack.
    • (User) Make privacy the product: every user sees, approves, and controls their own taste fingerprint before anyone else sees a thing.
    • (Business) Validate that resonance-led introductions convert to real dates at a higher rate than swipe-led ones.
    • (Business) Prove that consent-based "bring your data" onboarding is acceptable enough to a real cohort to be a durable acquisition pipeline.

    Non-goals

    • Not a faster swipe. We are not optimizing the existing swipe loop, because the loop itself is the problem.
    • Not a personality quiz. We will not add another self-report questionnaire, because self-report is the signal we are explicitly trying to replace.
    • Not a global launch. We will not chase broad geographic coverage at the start, because match quality depends on local liquidity and a real shared graph.
    • Not a data broker. We will never sell or repurpose user data, because where data trust is a hypothesis to test, consent is the differentiator, not a feature to monetize.
    • Not a claim that taste predicts chemistry. We are not asserting a compatibility "score"; we treat taste as a high-quality filter and conversation starter, not a magic match guarantee.

    3. The product

    Social Dating reads the cultural fingerprint a person already leaves online, with explicit permission, distills it into a private taste profile the user controls, and uses it to make a few high-resonance introductions each week. The core mechanic is consent-first by design: the user owns the data, Social Dating reads the signal.

    StepWhat the user doesWhat Social Dating does
    01 Connect what you chooseLinks the accounts that hold real taste (music, photo library, the platforms they live on) through official, permission-based connectionsIngests only what the user grants, through legitimate connections. Nothing is scraped.
    02 Build the taste fingerprintReviews and edits the generated profile before anyone sees itDistills signals into a private fingerprint across music, film, books, values, humor, and the social graph
    03 Meet a few who resonateReceives a small set of introductions each week, each with a stated reason and shared mutualsSurfaces high-resonance matches with a real reason to click and the mutual connections in common

    The trust layer is the social graph. "You have 3 people in common" is social proof a cold-stranger app cannot offer, and it makes a first conversation warmer and safer.

    4. User stories

    Burned-out dater (primary persona)

    • As a Gen Z dater exhausted by swiping, I want to receive a few introductions with a real reason behind each, so that I stop treating people as interchangeable and actually go on dates.
    • As someone who is bad at writing a dating bio, I want to be matched on what I genuinely return to (my music, my films, my causes), so that I am represented honestly rather than aspirationally.

    Privacy-conscious user

    • As a user who distrusts how dating apps handle data, I want to see, edit, and approve my taste fingerprint before anyone else sees it, so that I stay in control of what is shared.
    • As a user, I want a clear guarantee that my data is never sold or used without consent, so that connecting an account feels safe rather than risky.

    Edge and empty states

    • As a new user in a city with too few members, I want Social Dating to tell me honestly that the network is still thin here and offer to notify me when it is ready, so that I am not handed low-quality matches that erode my trust.
    • As a user who connects only one sparse account, I want a graceful low-data state that still produces a usable (if smaller) fingerprint and tells me what connecting more would unlock, so that onboarding does not dead-end.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Consent-first account connection through official, permission-based integrations (no scraping).
      • Given a user wants to connect a source, when they authorize it, then Social Dating ingests only the granted data through the official connection and records the consent scope.
    • A user-reviewable, user-editable taste fingerprint that is private until the user approves it.
