      • Given a fingerprint has been generated, when the user opens it, then they can see every inferred dimension, edit or remove any of it, and nothing is visible to others until they approve.
    • A curated, limited weekly introduction set (not an infinite stack), each with a stated reason and shared mutuals.
      • Given a user is active, when the weekly batch runs, then they receive a small set of introductions, each annotated with the specific shared signal and mutual connections.
    • An explicit, enforced data-use guarantee (never sold, never used without consent).

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Deeper fingerprint controls (tune weighting across music, values, humor, graph).
    • "See who resonates with you" reciprocity view.
    • Priority introductions and richer match context for paid users.

    Future (P2)

    • Human-assisted concierge matchmaking tier (curated, vetted introductions).
    • Expansion across cities once per-city liquidity thresholds are met.
    • Group and community-seeded introductions that lean on the shared graph.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision: Match on revealed behavior, not self-report. Alternatives considered: another personality quiz (rejected, it reproduces the exact aspirational-fiction problem we are trying to kill); photo-first swiping (rejected, it is the burnout engine). Why this won: revealed taste is a richer, more honest signal and doubles as a built-in conversation, not a checklist.

    Decision: Consent-first ingestion instead of scraping. Alternatives considered: scraping public profiles (rejected, legally fragile and a trust violation where trust is important but the claimed prevalence is unsourced); manual taste entry (rejected, high friction and low signal). Why this won: the EU Digital Markets Act, Article 6(9), requires designated gatekeepers to support end-user data portability, including continuous and real-time access for authorized third parties; it is not a universal right to every platform API, which for the first time makes consent-based "bring your data" a durable pipeline rather than a hack. The honest cost: this adds real onboarding friction, and willingness to connect accounts is one of our top open risks (see section 8).

    Decision: A few curated introductions per week, not an infinite stack. Alternatives considered: the standard endless feed (rejected, it optimizes time-on-app at the expense of the user's actual goal). Why this won: the product should win when a user leaves to go on a date, not when they stay scrolling. The unflattering trade-off: this caps engagement metrics and session counts that investors are used to seeing, so we are deliberately choosing a harder growth story in exchange for a healthier one.

    Decision: Subscription, not ad-supported or pay-per-match. Alternatives considered: ad-supported (rejected, it re-introduces the attention-maximizing incentive we are trying to escape); pay-per-introduction (rejected, it nickel-and-dimes the user and distorts behavior). Why this won: a subscription aligned with actually meeting people keeps incentives pointed at outcomes, and premium matchmaking is the fastest-growing slice of the market.

    Decision: Narrow, single-city, single-community launch. Alternatives considered: broad launch (rejected, match quality collapses without local density). Why this won: mutual connections have to be real from day one, which is only possible where the graph is dense. The trade-off is a slower, smaller start.

    7. UX

    The concept landing page sets the interaction language: warm, editorial, and calm, the opposite of a slot-machine feed. Fraunces serif headlines, a clay-and-bone palette, and a subtle grain texture signal "considered" rather than "addictive."

    • Two people rendered as nodes joined by an overlapping "resonance" field, with floating taste tags (indie folk, climate work, slow travel, A24 films, salsa) and a "3 mutuals" badge. The visual argues the thesis before any copy does: you meet through what you share.
    • A side-by-side "Old way vs Social Dating" table that turns vague self-report ("I love travel and good vibes") into revealed behavior ("Saves trips to Oaxaca, Lisbon, Kyoto. Follows three slow-travel writers."). This is the clearest single artifact of the product's premise.
    • A three-step flow (Connect what you choose, Build your fingerprint, Meet a few who resonate) that foregrounds consent and review at every stage.
    • An honest "open questions" section, on the page itself, naming the riskiest assumptions. Putting the risks in the marketing is itself a design decision: it sets the trust tone the product depends on.

    The interaction principle throughout: depth over volume. Every screen is built to feel like recognition, not a quota.

    8. How I would validate this

    Because Social Dating is a concept, the section below is a validation plan, not a record of results. Each hypothesis is paired with the experiment that would test it. The figures below are explicit targets, not observed results.

    Leading indicators (adoption, activation, completion)

    • Waitlist conversion from the concept page: target 25% of visitors who join early access. Method: a future concept-page experiment.
    • Account-connection rate during onboarding: target 60% of signups who connect at least one real source. Method: instrumented onboarding in a v1 prototype. This is the make-or-break activation metric, because consent-first onboarding is the model's biggest friction point.
    • Fingerprint approval rate: target 70% of users who review and approve their generated fingerprint without abandoning. Method: prototype funnel.

    Lagging indicators (retention, conversion to dates, satisfaction)

    • Introduction-to-date conversion: target that resonance-led introductions convert to real dates at least 1.5x a swipe-led control (target, not observed). Method: a held-out comparison once there is liquidity in one city. This is the central thesis test.
    • Paid conversion to Social Dating Plus: target 10% within the first cohort. Method: subscription analytics.
    • Member-reported "this felt different / better" satisfaction: target 4 of 5. Method: post-intro surveys.

    The three riskiest assumptions, stated plainly (carried from the concept page)

    1. Signal. Does taste similarity actually predict chemistry? Relationship science is skeptical that similarity-matching beats chance, so we deliberately frame taste as a high-quality filter and conversation starter, and we measure conversion-to-date against a control rather than claiming a compatibility score.
    2. Access. Will people connect their accounts? The consent-first model is cleaner than scraping but adds friction, and willingness to link a music account or photo library in exchange for fewer, better introductions is unproven.
    3. Cold start. Can we seed enough density to matter? Match quality needs liquidity in a city and a real graph, which is why launch is narrow by design.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The concept page leans on a single, contestable assumption (taste predicts chemistry) that relationship science does not clearly support, and it is easy to oversell it as a compatibility engine. The honest reframe is that Social Dating's defensible claim is "a more honest, less exhausting way to meet," not "a better predictor of love," and the validation plan above is built around proving the former, not the latter. If I rebuilt the page today, I would lead with the burnout-and-consent hypothesis (which needs sourced research) and treat the chemistry claim as an open question to be tested, not a promise. I would also be wary of the cited industry statistics: the earlier figures had no sources, not Social Dating's own data, and should never be presented as validation.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Does similarity-led matching beat a swipe-led control on conversion-to-date? (Owner: data) This is the thesis.
    • What is the minimum number of connectable sources that produces a fingerprint users find accurate? (Owner: data + design)
    • Will users actually grant the integrations, and which one is the lowest-friction first connection? (Owner: design + user research)
    • What is the legal and compliance footprint of consent-based data portability across jurisdictions? (Owner: legal)
    • What density (members and graph overlap) does a single city need before introductions feel high quality? (Owner: data)

    Path to a buildable v1 A single-city, single-community pilot with one or two connectable sources, a manually reviewable fingerprint, and a weekly batch of curated introductions. Success is defined as a measurable lift in introduction-to-date conversion over a swipe-led control, not raw engagement.

    Market positioning

    Swipe appsAlgorithm/quiz appsMatchmakersSocial Dating
    Matches onPhotos, bioSelf-reportHuman judgmentRevealed taste + graph
    Optimizes forTime-on-appRetentionOutcomesOutcomes
    Privacy stanceWeakMixedN/AConsent as product
    ScalableHighHighLowMedium to high

    GTM hypothesis. Launch where the graph is dense (one city, one community), let the "3 mutuals" trust signal drive word of mouth, and convert burned-out swipers who want depth. Defensibility comes from the consent-based data relationship and the network effects of a real shared graph, both of which compound and neither of which a cold-stranger swipe app can replicate.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: reframes a profitable but unhappy market around the right unit of analysis (the matching signal itself), not surface features.
    • Trade-off reasoning: deliberately accepts lower engagement metrics and slower geographic growth in exchange for healthier incentives and trust, and says so out loud.
    • Technical fluency: grounds the model in a concrete, current enabler (EU data-portability rules plus cheap-enough AI inference) rather than hand-waving.
    • Validation discipline: separates a hypothesis requiring sources (burnout, consent demand) from a contestable one (taste predicts chemistry) and builds the test plan around proving the contestable claim with a control, not asserting it.
    • Roadmap sense: sequences a narrow, density-first pilot before expansion, because the product literally does not work without local liquidity.
    • Communication: puts the riskiest assumptions on the marketing page itself, demonstrating that trust is treated as the product, not as copy.

    Research and access boundary

    Digital Markets Act, Article 6(9) is the specific portability provision; feasibility depends on gatekeeper coverage, permissions and actual integration access.

    Product requirements

    The Monthly

    V1 concept · V2 build

    DemoSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Period care is predictable, yet buying it is not.
    North star
    60% of subscribers retained past 3 cycles (target)

    Live today and next

    Live today

    • Landing page with the promise, a sample box and how it works in three steps.
    • Waitlist with Get early access and Give it as a gift.
    • Cycle date estimator, labeled as an estimate and not medical advice.
    • Planned price ($18 a month) and a founding round for the first 100 boxes.

    Next

    • Subscription checkout and cycle settings per subscriber.
    • Fulfillment partner and first shipped boxes for the founding round.
    • Measure waitlist conversion against the 8% target.

    Period care, timed to your cycle. A subscription box that ships pads, monthly chocolates, and weekly self care right before your period arrives. Landing page and waitlist built.

    Live V2 ↗

    The Monthly

    One-line summary: A period-care subscription box timed to each subscriber's cycle, shipping the right pads plus chocolates and rotating self-care items so support arrives exactly when it is needed.

    Live build: a landing page and waitlist to test demand for the timing promise. The subscription flow in P0 is specified, not built.

    Leading metric: waitlist conversion rate (target 8% of visitors; not observed).

    Problem

    Period care is predictable yet poorly served. People run out of supplies at the worst moment, generic subscription boxes ignore the actual timing of the cycle, and the experience feels transactional rather than caring. There is no offering that lands the right products in the right week, paired with the comfort items (chocolate, self-care) that make a tough few days a little better.

    Target users

    • Primary: People who menstruate, ages 16 to 40, who want reliable, well-timed supplies plus a bit of comfort and are open to subscriptions.
    • Secondary: Gift buyers (partners, parents, friends) wanting a thoughtful recurring gift.
    • Tertiary: People with predictable cycles who value convenience and surprise-and-delight.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Deliver the right supplies timed to arrive before each subscriber's period.
    • Pair essentials with comfort and self-care to make the box feel caring.
    • Build a sticky, low-churn subscription through reliability and delight.

    Non-goals

    • We are not offering medical or contraceptive advice or products.
    • We are not building a full cycle-tracking health platform; timing is a means, not the product.
    • We are not committing to fully custom product mixes at launch.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • Cycle-timed shipping: subscriber sets cycle start and length, and boxes are scheduled to arrive a few days before each period.
    • Core supplies: pads in selected absorbencies and counts.
    • Comfort add-ins: chocolates and a rotating self-care item each month.
    • Simple subscription management (skip, pause, adjust date, cancel).
    • Preference setup for absorbency, quantity, and dietary or allergy notes.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Cycle-length learning and adjustment based on feedback to improve timing accuracy.
    • Expanded product choices (tampons, liners, organic options).
    • Gift subscriptions with a personalized note and recipient setup.
    • Customizable self-care preferences (tea, skincare, comfort items).

    P2 (later)

    • Tiered boxes (essentials versus deluxe self-care).
    • Cycle reminders and light companion content.
    • Bundled one-off restock store for between-box needs.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Target: 60% of subscribers retained past 3 cycles (subscribers who keep receiving boxes through at least their third delivery, indicating the timing and contents deliver real value).

    Supporting metrics

    • On-time delivery accuracy: target 95% of shipments arrive before day 1.
    • Monthly churn: target under 7%; track average subscription lifespan.
    • Self-care item satisfaction and box rating per shipment.
    • Skip-and-pause versus cancel ratio, and gift conversion.

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Timing accuracy: cycle variability can make boxes arrive late. Mitigate with conservative lead times, easy date adjustment, and cycle-length learning over time.
    • Subscription fatigue: boxes can feel repetitive or like clutter. Mitigate with rotating, genuinely useful self-care items and easy skip and pause to prevent unwanted shipments.
    • Unit economics: shipping plus comfort items can squeeze margins. Mitigate with sensible box composition, tiered pricing, and supplier negotiation as volume grows.
    • Supply and fulfillment reliability: stockouts break the core promise. Mitigate with buffer inventory on essentials and a vetted fulfillment partner.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Weeks 1 to 6): MVP subscription: cycle-timed scheduling, core pads, chocolates, and one rotating self-care item, with basic subscription management. Launch to a waitlist cohort.
    • Phase 2 (Weeks 7 to 14): Personalization: add cycle-length learning, expanded product choices, and customizable self-care preferences. Tune timing accuracy and box ratings.
    • Phase 3 (Weeks 15 to 26): Growth and tiers: introduce gift subscriptions, tiered boxes, and a restock store, optimizing for subscribers retained past 3 cycles.

    Unit economics

    Proposed price: $18 to $22 a month. Illustrative planning assumptions, not supplier quotes: $9 COGS per box including pads, chocolates and one self-care item, plus $5 shipping. At $20, that leaves $6 (30%) before payment fees, overhead and acquisition. Weekly physical self-care shipments are not affordable in that model; the proposed weekly drops are digital content, with physical extras bundled monthly. Supplier and fulfillment quotes must validate these assumptions.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • Pads only in P0. Rejected alternative: tampons and cups at launch. Cost: narrower choice; simpler inventory and sizing prove timing first.
    • Cycle input instead of app integration. Rejected alternative: Clue or Apple Health sync. Cost: manual updates; lower integration and health-data exposure at launch.
    • Weekly self-care drops versus one monthly box. Rejected alternative: physical weekly shipping. Cost: less tactile weekly delight; digital drops plus one monthly shipment protect margin.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Without checkout, the waitlist shows interest, not willingness to pay.

    What I would do next

    A paid preorder for the founding 100 boxes, to test price before building the subscription.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Tested demand with a landing page before building fulfillment.
    • Ran the unit economics before choosing a price.
    • Kept scope honest: the subscription is specified, not built.

    Product requirements

    Pivot

    A foot-pedal, touch-free toilet seat that lifts and lowers hands-free.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    The toilet seat is a daily friction and hygiene point in shared bathrooms.
    North star
    70% of pilot households still use it at 90 days (target)

    Pivot

    One-line summary: A foot-pedal, touch-free toilet seat that lifts and lowers via a floor pedal, ending the seat-up-or-down standoff and keeping hands off a high-touch surface.

    Problem

    The toilet seat is a daily friction point in shared bathrooms: arguments over leaving it up or down, and a real hygiene concern because the seat is a high-touch surface people would rather not handle. Existing solutions are either expensive smart toilets, flimsy aftermarket gadgets, or motorized seats that are costly and failure-prone. There is no simple, durable, mechanical way to raise and lower the seat without touching it.

    Target users

    • Primary: Households with shared bathrooms who want to end the seat dispute and reduce touchpoints, especially mixed-gender homes and families with kids.
    • Secondary: Hygiene-conscious individuals and people with germ sensitivities.
    • Tertiary: Small commercial and semi-public restrooms (offices, cafes) wanting low-maintenance touch-free fixtures.

    Goals and Non-goals

    Goals

    • Let anyone raise and lower the seat hands-free with a foot pedal.
    • Deliver a durable, mechanical solution that needs no power or app.
    • Fit standard toilets with a straightforward install.

    Non-goals

    • We are not building a smart toilet, bidet, or electronics-laden seat in v1.
    • We are not motorizing the lid; the mechanism is mechanical and user-driven.
    • We are not targeting custom or non-standard toilet geometries initially.

    Key features

    P0 (launch)

    • Foot pedal mounted at the floor that mechanically raises and lowers the seat only in v1; lid lift is P1.
    • Smooth, controlled motion with a soft-close return to prevent slamming.
    • Fits standard round and elongated toilet bowls with included hardware.
    • Tool-light installation that a typical homeowner can complete.
    • Durable, water-resistant, easy-to-clean materials rated for daily use.

    P1 (fast follow)

    • Adjustable pedal placement and cable or linkage length for varied bathroom layouts.
    • Lid-and-seat combined lift option.
    • Color and finish variants to match common fixtures.
    • Replaceable wear parts and a simple maintenance kit.

    P2 (later)

    • Commercial-grade variant for higher-traffic restrooms.
    • Antimicrobial seat surface option.
    • Compatibility kits for non-standard bowls.

    Success metrics

    North-star metric: Target: 70% of pilot households report still using it at 90 days; not observed. Supporting commercial outcome: 30-day return rate under 10%.

    Supporting metrics

    • Successful self-install rate and median install time.
    • Mechanism reliability (cycles to failure) and warranty claim rate.
    • Customer satisfaction and Net Promoter Score.
    • Return rate and repeat or referral purchases.

    Key risks and mitigations

    • Mechanical durability: a bathroom mechanism cycled thousands of times must not fail. Mitigate with corrosion-resistant materials, high-cycle testing, and replaceable wear parts.
    • Install friction: a hard install kills adoption and drives returns. Mitigate with universal-fit hardware, clear instructions, and a fit-check guide before purchase.
    • Aesthetics and footprint: a visible pedal and linkage may feel intrusive. Mitigate with discreet, low-profile industrial design and finish options.
    • Fit variability: toilets vary in size and shape. Mitigate by designing to standard round and elongated bowls first and publishing clear compatibility guidance.

    Phased rollout

    • Phase 1 (Months 1 to 4): Prototype and durability: build working prototypes, run high-cycle reliability and soft-close testing, and validate fit on common standard bowls.
    • Phase 2 (Months 5 to 8): Pilot launch: small production run for standard bowls, direct-to-consumer launch, and measurement of self-install success and daily use.
    • Phase 3 (Months 9 to 15): Expand: add adjustable layouts, finishes, lid-lift option, and a commercial-grade variant, optimizing for active daily pedal use per unit.

    Price, materials and competition

    Proposed target price: $49, not a supplier-validated retail price. Illustrative bill of materials: $8 corrosion-resistant cable linkage, $6 pedal and housing, $5 universal brackets, $4 soft-close damper, $3 fasteners and packaging, totaling $26 before assembly, shipping and warranty. Existing aftermarket pedal seat lifters are the comparator category. Pivot's proposed difference is soft-close, adjustable standard-bowl fit and a finished low-profile pedal, not demonstrated superiority.

    Key decision

    Cable linkage over a rigid lever: flexible pedal placement wins, with stretch and corrosion testing as the cost. Seat only over seat-plus-lid at launch: simpler force and geometry win, at the cost of leaving lid lifting manual. Both rejected alternatives remain testable, not proven failures.

    Validation and next test

    Fit, cycle durability and continued use need a household pilot.

    Product requirements

    Verda Closet

    A dehumidifying closet that protects garments from moisture, mold, and odor.

    ConceptSolo: research and PRD
    Problem
    In humid climates and small apartments, closets quietly ruin clothes.
    North star
    500 refundable reservations within 8 weeks (target)

    Verda

    A closet that protects what you wear.

    The 30-second version. Verda is a consumer-hardware concept: a wardrobe system with a built-in dehumidifier that holds your clothes at the ideal 45 to 55% relative humidity, so leather does not crack, knits do not mildew, and your best pieces last for years. It treats the closet as a small sealed microclimate (the same idea proven in the dry cabinets that protect camera gear in the tropics) rather than fighting the whole room with a portable unit in the corner. Built as a concept landing page and a reserve-driven demand test, not a shipped product. The PM work here is hardware scoping (a buildable bill of materials and a three-SKU line), a demand-validation mechanism (refundable reservations gated on a real threshold), and honest non-goals about what the first build will and will not do.

    Timeline: Weekend concept artifact, in demand validation.

    1. Problem statement

    In humid climates and tight city apartments, your closet is quietly ruining your clothes. Ambient moisture sits inside the closet and goes to work: mildew on cotton, white bloom on leather, musty knits, warped shoes, corroded hardware. In tropical and coastal homes, closets can sit at 70 to 85% relative humidity for months, well above the roughly 50% ceiling that keeps fabrics safe. The damage is slow, invisible, and expensive, and it adds up to thousands of dollars of suits, bags, and coats degraded every rainy season.

    Existing solutions miss where the damage happens:

    ApproachWhat it doesWhy it falls short
    Portable room dehumidifierDries the whole roomNever reaches inside the closet where it matters, runs loud, holds no precise band, needs emptying
    Silica gel packs / charcoalPassive moisture absorptionTiny capacity, no regulation, no feedback, saturates and stops working
    Cedar blocks, garment bagsOdor and pest deterrenceDo nothing about ambient humidity
    Camera dry cabinetsPrecisely regulate a sealed volumeBuilt for lenses, sized and styled for gear, not a wardrobe

    The market gap: nobody has scaled the proven dry-cabinet approach up to a wardrobe and finished it like furniture.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • (User) Hold the closet interior at 45 to 55% relative humidity automatically, dry enough to stop mold but never so dry that leather cracks.
    • (User) Make protection effortless: no tanks to empty, no dials to babysit, set it and forget it.
    • (User) Protect the specific pieces worth protecting (leather, wool, silk, suede, sneakers) for the long run.
    • (Business) Validate real purchase intent through refundable reservations before committing capital to a production run.
    • (Business) Define a bill of materials and a SKU line buildable with off-the-shelf-adjacent parts, so the first prototype is fast and provable.

    Non-goals

    • Not a whole-room HVAC system. We regulate one sealed cabinet, not the apartment, because the closet is where the damage happens and a sealed volume is far cheaper to control precisely.
    • Not a maximum-drying box. We regulate to a band, not zero humidity, because over-drying cracks leather and embrittles fibers.
    • Not a connected-everything smart-home play in v1. The companion app does setpoint, logging, and drift alerts, nothing more, because feature creep delays a hardware build that has to prove the core climate claim first.
    • Not a custom-geometry product at launch. We ship defined SKUs, because bespoke closets multiply manufacturing complexity before demand is proven.
    • Not built before demand exists. We do not manufacture until reservations clear a real threshold, because building hardware on a hunch is how hardware startups die.

    3. The product

    A sealed wardrobe senses interior humidity, regulates a proposed 45 to 55% RH band, circulates air behind garments and manages condensate without claiming to dry the whole room. All engineering is concept scope, not a manufactured capability.

    Proposed target prices, not quotes: The Drop-In $600, The Wardrobe $1,500 and The Walk-In from $4,000. Illustrative Wardrobe materials budget: enclosure and seals $350, dehumidifier $120, sensing/control $60, fans and air plenum $70, cedar liner and water handling $100; total $700. At $1,500 the materials-only margin is about 53%; assembly, shipping, service and warranty reduce it. Supplier quotes and bench performance must validate the budget.

    4. User stories

    Humid-climate homeowner (primary persona)

    • As someone living in Singapore, Miami, or Mumbai, I want my closet held at a safe humidity automatically, so that my leather jackets and wool coats stop growing mold and musty smells every rainy season.
    • As an owner of expensive pieces, I want a system that protects bags and silk specifically, so that the items worth thousands do not degrade invisibly.

    Apartment renter

    • As a renter who cannot renovate, I want a sealed, plug-and-play wardrobe with no plumbing or installation, so that I get climate protection without touching the building.

    Set-it-and-forget-it user

    • As a busy person, I want fully automatic operation with no tank to empty and no dial to babysit, so that protection requires zero ongoing effort.

    Edge and error states

    • As a user, I want a drift alert when a zone leaves the safe band, so that I find out before damage starts rather than after.
    • As a user in a power outage or with a full water tray, I want a clear, non-silent failure state (an alert and a graceful hold), so that I am never falsely reassured that my clothes are protected when they are not.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Hold the sealed closet interior at 45 to 55% RH automatically.
      • Given the cabinet is sealed and powered, when interior humidity rises above the band, then the dehumidifier engages and returns the interior to setpoint without manual input.
    • Continuous interior sensing, not room sensing, with dual-zone coverage (main bay and vault).
      • Given the system is running, when humidity is read, then it is measured inside the cabinet in each zone, not in the room.
    • Even airflow so no damp pockets form (perforated back plenum plus circulation fans).
    • Whisper-quiet operation suitable for a bedroom.
    • Water handling with no bucket to empty (self-evaporating tray, optional gravity drain).
    • A failure/drift alert so the user is never silently unprotected.
      • Given a zone leaves the safe band or a fault occurs, when the condition persists, then the user receives an alert rather than a silent failure.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Companion app with humidity logging, trend history, and remote alerts.
    • A cedar vault liner: a naturally moisture- and moth-resistant zone for bags and silk.
    • Multiple finishes to match furniture.

    Future (P2)

    • The Walk-In flagship: zoned, ducted, whole-room control.
    • Smart-home integrations beyond the standalone app.
    • Higher-capacity hybrid engine for large walk-in volumes.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision: Seal and regulate the closet, do not dry the room. Alternatives considered: a better portable room dehumidifier (rejected, it never reaches inside the closet, runs loud, and holds no precise band); passive desiccant (rejected, tiny capacity, no regulation, no feedback). Why this won: a sealed volume is dramatically cheaper and more precise to control than a room, and the approach is already proven in camera dry cabinets. The trade-off: it requires gasketed doors and a genuinely sealed enclosure, which raises cabinet build cost versus a plastic box.

    Decision: Regulate to a 45 to 55% band, not maximum drying. Alternatives considered: drive humidity as low as possible (rejected, it cracks leather and embrittles fibers). Why this won: the goal is protection, not desiccation, and the band stops mold while keeping leather supple. This is the most common misconception the FAQ has to address, so it is a deliberate, stated design constraint.

    Decision: A three-SKU line (Drop-In, Wardrobe, Walk-In) instead of one product. Alternatives considered: a single flagship (rejected, it ignores that renters cannot install and renovators want millwork integration); fully bespoke (rejected, it multiplies manufacturing complexity before demand is proven). Why this won: three defined SKUs cover the three real living situations while keeping manufacturing bounded. The lead SKU, The Wardrobe, is deliberately the no-installation, no-plumbing unit because it has the widest addressable audience.

    Decision: Build the prototype from off-the-shelf-adjacent parts. Alternatives considered: custom-engineered compressor and enclosure from scratch (rejected, slow and capital-heavy before the climate claim is even proven). Why this won: a thermoelectric or desiccant module, standard humidistats, whisper-grade fans, and a gasketed shell make the first build fast and provable. The honest trade-off: thermoelectric modules are quiet and compressor-free but have limited capacity, which is exactly why the large Walk-In is deferred to a higher-capacity hybrid engine rather than promised in v1.

    Decision: Gate manufacturing on a real reservation threshold (500 refundable reservations). Alternatives considered: build first and sell later (rejected, the classic hardware failure mode); pure email waitlist (rejected, a free email is weak signal). Why this won: a refundable reservation is a stronger intent signal than an email but a lower barrier than a full pre-order, and a public threshold creates honest accountability. The unflattering trade-off: a low-friction refundable reservation still overstates true purchase intent, so it must be read as directional demand, not as committed revenue (see section 8).

    7. UX

    Concept descriptions, not screenshot links: open wardrobe with a protected interior; a visible humidity readout; a Sense, Regulate, Protect explanation; a labeled cutaway for sensing and airflow; and a refundable-reservation surface. The 312 / 500 figure is a design mock; no reservations have been collected. "Zero mildew" is not a verified promise.

    8. How I would validate this

    Because Verda is a hardware concept with no manufactured unit, this is a validation plan, not a record of results. The figures below are explicit targets, not observed results.

    Leading indicators (demand, intent)

    • Reservation rate against the public goal: target 500 of 500 refundable reservations within 8 weeks. Method: the reservation counter on the concept page. This is the primary go/no-go signal for committing to a build.
    • SKU preference split (Drop-In vs Wardrobe vs Walk-In): target a clear lead SKU to focus the first build. Method: the reservation form's SKU question.
    • Geographic concentration of reservations (which cities feel the humidity pain most): Method: the reservation form's city field, used to choose a launch market.

    Lagging indicators (build performance, satisfaction)

    • Prototype climate performance: target holding 45 to 55% RH in a sealed reach-in across a defined ambient range, whisper-quiet. Method: an instrumented bench prototype. This is the make-or-break engineering proof.
    • Reservation-to-purchase conversion once the unit is real: target 20%. Method: convert reservation-holders at founder pricing and measure follow-through.
    • Owner-reported protection satisfaction and "would recommend": target 4 of 5. Method: post-install survey.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The reservation mechanism is the right instinct but a weak proxy if read literally: a refundable $1 hold (or a free name on a list, in the longer page variant) captures curiosity as much as commitment, so the honest read is directional interest, not committed revenue. The two landing-page variants also tell slightly inconsistent stories (one frames a polished consumer product with a "Reserve for $1" hook, the other a build-it-if-500-want-it crowd-validation with a richer bill of materials and FAQ), and a real test should pick one narrative and one price point so the signal is clean. The hardest unvalidated assumption is engineering, not demand: thermoelectric dehumidification is quiet and compressor-free but capacity-limited, and whether it can hold the band in a large or frequently-opened closet is unproven. If I rebuilt this, I would lead the validation with a bench prototype that proves the climate claim in parallel with the demand test, rather than letting an attractive landing page imply a capability that has not yet been built. The displayed "312 / 500 reserved" figure is a design mock, not a real count.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    Open questions

    • Can a quiet thermoelectric or desiccant module actually hold 45 to 55% RH in a real wardrobe volume, including a frequently-opened or large closet? (Owner: eng) This is the central technical risk.
    • What is the bill-of-materials cost and resulting retail price, and at what price does demand hold? (Owner: eng + finance)
    • Which SKU and which launch city do reservations point to? (Owner: data)
    • How is condensate handled across renter installs with no drain access, at scale and reliably? (Owner: eng)
    • What is the energy draw, and does heat venting create any real conflict with home AC? (Owner: eng)

    Path to a buildable v1 Build the lead SKU first (likely The Wardrobe: sealed, plug-and-play, no plumbing) from the defined bill of materials: a thermoelectric dehumidifier module, dual-zone humidistat and temperature sensors, a whisper-grade circulation fan array, a gasketed cabinet shell, a perforated back plenum, a control board with companion app, a cedar vault liner, and a self-evaporating tray with optional drain. Gate the production run on clearing the reservation threshold, and prove the climate claim on a bench prototype in parallel.

    Market positioning. Verda sits between premium closet systems (California Closets, furniture-grade but climate-dumb) and appliances (room dehumidifiers, functional but ugly and imprecise). The wedge is "built like California Closets, smart like an appliance." Defensibility comes from being the first to package proven dry-cabinet climate control as furniture-grade wardrobe, plus the design, finish, and SKU breadth that a generic appliance maker would not invest in.

    GTM hypothesis. Start in the highest-humidity, highest-disposable-income markets (Singapore, Miami, Mumbai, coastal cities), convert reservation-holders at founder pricing, and let the protection of expensive pieces (the "it pays for itself" argument) drive word of mouth among people who have already lost a jacket to humidity.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: locates an invisible, slow, expensive problem (interior closet humidity) that adjacent products consistently miss because they treat the room, not the cabinet.
    • Trade-off reasoning: picks a sealed-volume thermoelectric approach with eyes open about its capacity limits, and defers the large Walk-In rather than overpromising.
    • Technical fluency: translates the concept into a concrete, buildable bill of materials and a coherent sense, regulate, distribute, protect architecture borrowed from proven camera dry cabinets.
    • Validation discipline: designs a demand gate (refundable reservations against a public threshold) and is honest that it overstates intent, while naming engineering, not demand, as the riskiest assumption.
    • Roadmap sense: sequences a single lead SKU and a parallel bench prototype before the flagship, matching build order to where risk and addressable audience actually sit.
    • Communication: makes an invisible benefit tangible (a glowing humidity readout, furniture-grade renders) so a skeptical buyer can feel the value before the product exists.

    Product requirements

    Para Papi

    A Spanish-language companion I built for my dad: I set one safe daily heart-health challenge and a note of pride, and he sees a single large-type task at a time.

    Live ↗
    In real useSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Health apps assume users are motivated, tech-comfortable and read English.
    North star
    A completed task on 5 of 7 days (target)

    Para Papi

    A Spanish-language companion that turns my dad's heart-health routine into a daily exchange of love: I set the challenge, he does one simple task, and a note of pride arrives with it.

    The 30-second version. Health apps assume the user is motivated, tech-comfortable, and reads English. My dad is none of those on a hard day. Proyecto Legado splits the product in two: a portal where I schedule one safe daily challenge and write a note of pride, and a view for him with one screen, large type, and one task at a time. Reminders arrive like letters from his daughter. It is not a fitness app; it is an emotional bridge. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live: https://papicarlos.lovable.app

    In use: Built for my father, Spanish first.

    Problem

    Older adults managing heart health are told to move more, but generic fitness apps add friction and guilt. Motivation comes from family, yet family is often far away.

    Who it is for

    An older Spanish-speaking parent who needs gentle daily movement, and the adult child who wants to support them from a distance.

    How it works

    ViewWhat happens
    Juliana's portalSchedule the day's challenge, write a note of pride, and send encouragement when a task is done
    Papá's viewOne screen, large type, one task at a time, with reminders that read like letters from his daughter

    Key decisions

    • Notes of pride instead of streaks. Rejected alternative: streaks and badges, which can turn missed days into guilt. Cost: less automated habit pressure.
    • One task per screen instead of a plan view. Rejected alternative: a weekly dashboard for the parent. Cost: less overview, accepted to keep the next action obvious.
    • Spanish-first instead of bilingual. Rejected alternative: split attention between two languages. Cost: a narrower audience, appropriate for the person this serves.
    • No clinician gating implemented yet. Rejected alternative: imply the build clinically approves challenges. Safe challenge means a short, gentle, optional activity within his existing care-team guidance, never a new intensity prescription. Cost: no broad-use launch before clinical review.

    Non-goals

    • Not a medical or rehab tool; no diagnosis, dosing or vital-sign tracking.
    • No streaks, scores or guilt when a day is missed.
    • Not built for a general audience; one user, one family.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: completed task on 5 of 7 days, completion within 3 hours of a reminder, and 4 notes of pride per week.

    What I would do next

    Voice notes from family members, a simple weekly summary for the caregiver, and clinician-approved challenge levels.

    Safety

    The app frames every challenge as a safe, gentle task (for example, a 10 minute walk) and says it is not a fitness app. It does not set exercise intensity or give medical advice.

    Risks

    • My dad overdoes a challenge. Mitigation: only gentle tasks, and the app says it is not a fitness app.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Reminders only work if he opens the app, so some days the note of pride arrives late. I would test a phone notification he can open with one tap.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Designing for a non-tech, non-English, low-motivation user.
    • Building a two-sided caregiver and parent product in one build.
    • Using encouragement rather than guilt while keeping a clear safety boundary.

    Product requirements

    Mary's Keepsake

    A keepsake journal gift.

    Live ↗
    In real useSolo: research, PRD and build, as a gift
    Problem
    Pregnancy apps get the tone wrong and harvest attention.
    North star
    A meaningful record kept by week 38

    Version history: this private gift is V1. Little Keepsake is V2, the public version.

    Mary

    A keepsake pregnancy journal, built as a gift for one person.

    The 30-second version. Mary is a private pregnancy companion and keepsake journal, built as a gift for a close friend. Each week it shows her what is growing, what her hormones are doing, what she might feel, what to eat, and a gentle movement and affirmation, then invites a sentence or two of journaling. When she nears the end, she can export the whole thing as a beautiful PDF keepsake of her pregnancy. It runs entirely in her browser with no account, no backend, and no tracking. Designed for an audience of one, it demonstrates real PM rigor: a deliberate data model, week-aware content, a hard privacy stance, and a clear-eyed view of the trade-offs that come from building for exactly one user.

    Timeline: Personal gift build, completed and delivered

    Demo: The app includes a "See a sample week" entry to the dashboard and keepsake without a due date.

    Content sourcing: Weekly growth and hormone text is informational.

    Results: The gift was delivered.

    1. Problem statement

    Pregnancy is forty-some weeks of profound bodily and emotional change, and the apps meant to accompany it mostly get the tone wrong: they are pastel, cartoonish, cliche-ridden ("mama," "mommy-to-be"), and built to harvest attention and data rather than to hold one person gently. My friend deserved something that felt like hers: a calm, adult, beautifully made space that explained what was happening in her body each week and let her keep a private record she could carry forever. The personal problem was specific and real. How do you give someone you love a companion for the most intimate stretch of her life that comforts rather than commodifies, and leaves her with a keepsake at the end? Mary is the answer to exactly that.

    2. Goals and non-goals

    Goals

    • Help her understand her body and her baby each week, in warm, direct, non-clinical language.
    • Make journaling the heart of the experience, so the app accrues into a personal record, not just a feed of facts.
    • Produce a genuinely beautiful PDF keepsake at the end, so the months become something she can hold and revisit.
    • Protect her completely: no account, no tracking, no data leaving her browser, because intimacy requires privacy.

    Non-goals

    • A commercial, multi-user product. Out of scope by design; this is a gift for one person, which sharpens rather than weakens the constraints.
    • Accounts, payments, or sharing. Out of scope because every one of those adds a privacy surface and friction my friend does not need.
    • Medical authority. Out of scope; the content is a warm companion, with a clear footer disclaimer to always talk to her provider.
    • Baby-app cliches (pastel pink, cartoon babies, "mama"). Explicitly out of scope; the tone is a Brooklyn stationery journal, not a nursery.

    3. The product

    Mary calculates the current pregnancy week from a due date, then serves week-aware content and a journaling space. Everything persists locally.

    ScreenWhat it does
    Onboarding (first load only)Soft welcome, due-date picker (with a current-week fallback), saves a local profile and routes to Home
    Home / Weekly DashboardGreeting and a current-week badge with soft progress, then six cards: What's growing, Your hormones, What you might feel, What to eat, Gentle movement, For today (affirmation), followed by the journal input and last three entries
    Journal HistoryAll entries grouped by month, each with date, week, and text, with inline edit and delete
    Download KeepsakeAvailable from week 38 (or a "baby has arrived" toggle): generates a PDF "my friend's Pregnancy Journey" with a cover page and one block per entry, each paired with that week's "what's growing" note

    The current week is derived as 40 minus weeks-until(dueDate), clamped to 1 to 42, so the right content always surfaces without her having to track anything. The journal input is the most important interaction: large, inviting, and auto-focused.

    4. User stories

    As the friend it was built for, opening the app mid-pregnancy, I want to see exactly what is happening this week in my body and my baby's, so that I feel informed and accompanied without searching.

    As the friend it was built for at the end of a long day, I want to write a sentence or two about how I am feeling, so that I capture this moment before it blurs into the next.

    As the friend it was built for nearing my due date, I want to download everything as a beautiful keepsake, so that I can hold the record of these months long after they pass.

    As the friend it was built for, who values her privacy deeply, I want nothing I write to leave my device, so that this space is entirely and only mine.

    As the friend it was built for returning to an empty journal (empty state), I want a warm, inviting prompt rather than a blank wall, so that the first entry feels easy to begin.

    As the friend it was built for if I go past my due date (edge case), I want the app to keep meeting me gently through weeks 41 and 42, so that an overdue week does not leave me staring at outdated content.

    5. Requirements

    Must-have (P0)

    • Local-only persistence, no account, no backend, no external calls.
      • Given my friend uses the app, when she enters anything, then it is stored only in her browser's localStorage and never transmitted.
    • Accurate week calculation driving content.
      • Given a saved due date, when she opens Home, then the correct pregnancy week (clamped 1 to 42) and its content are shown.
    • Journaling as the central interaction.
      • Given the Home screen, when she writes and saves, then the entry stores with today's date and the current week and appears in her history.
    • Beautiful PDF keepsake export from week 38.
      • Given week is 38+ (or the arrived toggle is on), when she taps Download Keepsake, then a serif-typeset PDF generates with a cover page and one block per entry plus that week's note.
    • Warm, adult, cliche-free tone with a medical disclaimer.
      • Given any screen, when it renders, then the language speaks directly to "you," avoids baby-app cliches, and the footer notes Mary is not medical advice.

    Nice-to-have (P1)

    • Subtle line-drawing illustrations per week.
    • Photo attachments on journal entries (kept local).
    • A gentle weekly "you reached a new week" moment.

    Future (P2)

    • A postpartum continuation companion.
    • Optional encrypted backup so the keepsake survives a lost device, without compromising the no-account principle.

    6. Key product decisions and trade-offs

    Decision: Build for an audience of one, on purpose. Alternatives considered: generalize it into a shippable app (rejected because the gift's whole value is that it is hers, named for her, tuned to her). Why this won: designing for one real, known user is a PM discipline, not a shortcut. It forces concrete choices (her name, her due date math, her tone) instead of hedged defaults, and it is the reason the product feels personal rather than generic.

    Decision: localStorage only, no account, no backend. Alternatives considered: a small backend for cross-device access and backup (rejected because it introduces an account, a privacy surface, and a dependency for a deeply private journal). Why this won: for an intimate record, the strongest privacy guarantee is that the data physically cannot leave the device. This is also the clearest unflattering trade-off in the build: it means the keepsake lives or dies with one browser, and a cleared cache or lost device loses everything. I accepted that risk for the privacy and simplicity it buys, and named encrypted backup as the future mitigation.

    Decision: Make the PDF keepsake genuinely beautiful, and gate it to the end. Alternatives considered: an always-available export, or a plain text dump (rejected because the keepsake is the emotional payoff and should arrive as a moment, beautifully). Why this won: gating it to week 38 turns the export into a milestone, and the serif-typeset, one-block-per-entry layout makes it an object worth keeping rather than a data file.

    Decision: Refuse baby-app conventions. Alternatives considered: the familiar pastel, cartoon, "mommy-to-be" idiom that the category defaults to (rejected because it is exactly the tone that made existing apps feel wrong for her). Why this won: tone is the product here. A warm, adult, stationery-journal aesthetic is what makes Mary feel like a gift and not an app.

    7. UX

    Even built for one person, the UX is held to a real standard. The palette is warm cream, deep brown, sage, terracotta, and dusty rose; headings are serif (Fraunces / Cormorant / DM Serif), body is clean sans (Inter / Geist); the layout is mobile-first at a 480px column with generous whitespace, 16px-plus rounded corners, and soft shadows. The microcopy speaks directly and warmly without ever tipping into saccharine, and there are no em-dashes anywhere, a small typographic discipline carried through the whole build. The journal input is deliberately the most prominent, auto-focused element, because writing is the behavior the product most wants to invite.

    8. Success metrics and validation

    For a gift of one, success is not adoption curves; it is whether the one person it was made for feels held and keeps something she treasures. Honest metrics for this build are qualitative and personal.

    Leading indicators (for this build)

    • Whether my friend returns to check in across weeks. Target: regular use across the trimester. Measured: by her own account (no tracking by design).
    • Whether the journal accrues entries. Target: a meaningful record by week 38. Measured: anecdotally.

    Lagging indicators

    • Whether she generates and keeps the PDF keepsake. Target: yes, the keepsake is the success condition.
    • Whether the experience felt warm and private rather than clinical. Target: her felt sense of being cared for.

    What didn't work, and what I'd change. The single hardest trade-off (localStorage only) is also the build's biggest fragility: a cleared cache or a switched device wipes an irreplaceable record. For a keepsake, that is a serious risk, and if I rebuilt I would add an opt-in encrypted local backup or an exportable data file early, so privacy and durability can coexist. I would also add periodic gentle nudges to export progress, rather than gating the keepsake entirely to week 38, so that an early arrival does not leave her with nothing. The content is warm but asserted; for anything beyond a private gift it would need clinical review before it could responsibly reach other people.

    9. Open questions and roadmap

    • How do we add durability (so the keepsake survives a lost device) without breaking the no-account, nothing-leaves-the-device promise? (Owner: eng + the recipient's preference.)
    • Should Mary continue into a postpartum companion, or is the keepsake the right place to end? (Owner: the recipient.)
    • If ever generalized, what clinical review and tone guardrails would the content need? (Owner: legal + a medical reviewer.)

    v2 scope (if ever extended): opt-in encrypted backup, per-week illustrations, and a postpartum continuation, always preserving the privacy-first core.

    Positioning note: Mary is intentionally not in the commercial pregnancy-app market (BabyCenter, What to Expect, Ovia), which competes on features, community, and monetized attention. Its differentiation is the opposite: a private, ad-free, beautifully made companion for one person. That is not a market position so much as a design philosophy, and it is exactly what makes the gift land.

    10. What this project demonstrates

    • Problem identification: named precisely why existing pregnancy apps felt wrong (tone, cliche, data harvesting) and built the deliberate inverse.
    • Trade-off reasoning: chose privacy over durability with eyes open, gated the keepsake to make it a moment, and refused category conventions on purpose.
    • Technical fluency: a clean local data model, derived week math, and a real PDF export pipeline, all with zero backend.
    • Validation discipline: honest that "success" for a gift is qualitative, and forthright about the fragility the privacy stance creates.
    • Roadmap sense: a credible path to durability and a postpartum continuation without compromising the core principle.
    • Communication: designing for one named, real user is a forcing function for clarity, and this spec shows the discipline that produced.

    Product requirements

    Little Keepsake

    A pregnancy journal that becomes a printable keepsake.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Pregnancy apps track symptoms and size, not the moments worth remembering.
    North star
    A note written in 3 of every 4 weeks (target)

    Version history: V1 was Mary's Keepsake, a private gift for a friend. Little Keepsake is V2, the public version.

    Little Keepsake

    The 30-second version. Enter a nickname and due date to start a private week by week journal. Write short notes for the baby, and at the end print everything as a keepsake. I first built it as a private gift for a friend, then made a public version with every personal detail removed. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Demo, no sign in: See a sample week opens a fictional mom's journal and saves nothing.

    Problem

    Pregnancy apps track symptoms and baby size, but few help you keep the small moments you will want to remember later.

    Who it is for

    Expecting parents who want a simple, private place to write a few lines each week.

    What it does

    • Nickname and due date setup.
    • A week by week view with short general notes.
    • Private journal entries tied to your account.
    • A printable keepsake PDF.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • A few lines a week, not a daily log. Rejected alternative: daily prompts. Cost: fewer entries, accepted because a small ask is one people keep.
    • Print as the payoff. The keepsake is the reason to keep writing, so it is built in from the start.
    • Separate the structure from the personal gift. The public version keeps what made the gift useful and drops everything about one person.

    Privacy

    Each journal is private to its account. The sample week uses a fictional person and is never saved. The public version was made from a copy with all data and personal details removed.

    Safety

    Every week view and the keepsake say: "General information, not medical advice. Ask your doctor or midwife about your pregnancy."

    Non-goals

    • No symptom tracking or medical guidance.
    • No social sharing or public profiles.
    • No ads or product recommendations.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed: a note written in 3 of every 4 weeks, and 30% of journals that reach the due date print a keepsake.

    What I learned

    A gift built for one person became a general tool once I separated the personal parts from the structure that made it useful.

    What I would do next

    Reminders to write each week, and a way to add photos to the keepsake.

    Product requirements

    Legacy Planner

    A will and estate planning workbook that turns "I keep putting this off" into one printable family packet.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    People know they should plan their estate but keep putting it off.
    North star
    40% of people who start a plan complete The Basics (target)

    Legacy Planner

    The 30-second version. Most people know they should plan their estate, and most keep putting it off. Legacy Planner breaks the job into six plain sections with a 10 minute first step, saves the plan as a private file, and prints a family packet to share with a spouse or partner. It prepares people for an attorney; it does not replace one. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Demo, no sign in: press Load a sample plan to see a full plan for a fictional family, then Clear to start your own.

    Problem

    Estate planning feels big, emotional and expensive, so it gets deferred. Nothing is written down, and when someone dies the family is left guessing where accounts, policies and documents are, and what that person wanted.

    Who it is for

    Adults, and especially business owners, who want their family to know where everything is and what they want, before they meet an attorney.

    What it does

    • Six sections: Start, The Basics (accounts, insurance, property, debts, digital accounts and where documents are kept), People (executor, guardians and advisors), Wishes, an optional Business succession section, and Next steps.
    • A 10 minute first step, so starting does not depend on having every answer.
    • Save my work and Load saved file, as a private file on the user's device.
    • Print family packet, plus a printable whole workbook and dark mode.
    • Next steps lists questions to bring to an estate attorney and a tax or financial advisor, and generates a summary.

    Origin

    It started as a private workbook built from one business owner's estate planning conversation. I generalized it into a tool anyone can use and removed every personal detail. All sample data is fictional.

    Key decisions and trade-offs

    • Private file save instead of accounts. Privacy over convenience: nothing is stored on a server. Cost: no sync across devices, and a lost file means starting again.
    • A workbook, not legal documents. It prepares people for an attorney rather than replacing one. Cost: the user still pays for legal help, accepted because will rules vary by state and country and a wrong document can cause harm.
    • Business succession is optional. Most users do not own a business, so the section stays out of their way.
    • A sample plan instead of a sign up wall. Visitors see a full plan in one click before typing anything personal.

    Non-goals

    • No legal or tax advice.
    • No will generation.
    • No storage of anyone's financial data.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed: 40% of visitors who start a plan complete The Basics, and 25% print a family packet.

    Risk

    Users may treat the workbook as legal advice. Mitigation: a visible disclaimer on Start and in the printed packet: "Not legal or tax advice. Laws vary by state and country. Review your plan with a licensed estate attorney."

    What I would do next

    State-specific checklists that point to the documents each state requires, and a read-only packet the executor can open.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    A private file means nothing is lost to a server, but people can forget to save and lose their work. I would add a reminder to save before closing and a one-click backup.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Turning a task people avoid into a small first step and plain sections.
    • Drawing a clear line between organizing information and giving legal advice.
    • Generalizing a personal tool into a product, with privacy built into the design.

    Product requirements

    Echoes of Life

    A Spanish voice-memory app I built so an older relative can record their life story in their own voice.

    Live ↗
    LiveSolo: research, PRD and build
    Problem
    Family stories live in one person's memory and are lost when no one records them.
    North star
    One new memory recorded each week (target)

    Echoes of Life

    Live as Nuestra Historia (Voces que perduran).

    The 30-second version. The stories that hold a family together usually live in one person's memory, and writing them down is hard for an older adult. Echoes of Life lets them speak instead: they enter a private key, record a memory in Spanish, and the recording is stored privately. It is built for one family, not a public product. Built solo with AI-assisted development.

    Live today and next

    Live today

    • A welcome screen in Spanish and entry with a private key.
    • Voice recording of memories, stored privately.

    Next

    • Save recordings to the family's Google Drive and log each one in a Google Sheet.
    • Simple prompts that suggest what to talk about next.

    Problem

    Older adults carry family history that no one has recorded. Typing a memoir is slow and tiring, and most recording apps are in English, ask for accounts and bury recordings in a phone's storage.

    Who it is for

    An older Spanish-speaking relative who wants to tell their story, and the family who wants to keep it.

    Key decisions

    • Voice instead of writing. Rejected alternative: a written journal. Cost: recordings are harder to search, accepted because speaking is the easiest way for this user to share.
    • A private key instead of an account. Rejected alternative: email sign up. Cost: no password recovery flow, accepted to keep entry to one step.
    • Spanish first. The app speaks the language the stories are told in.
    • Private by design. No sharing feed; the recordings belong to the family.

    Non-goals

    • Not a social or sharing app.
    • No transcription or AI rewriting of the stories at launch.
    • Not built for a general audience; one family.

    Success metrics

    Targets, not observed results: one new memory recorded each week, and recordings that the family replays.

    Risks

    • Recordings are deeply personal. Mitigation: private storage, key entry and no public listing of recordings.

    What did not work, and what I would change

    Automatic backup to the family's Google Drive is still waiting on setup, so recordings are not yet copied anywhere else. That is the first thing I would finish.

    What I would do next

    Finish the Drive and Sheets backup, then add gentle prompts for what to talk about next.

    What this project demonstrates

    • Designing for an older, non-English-speaking user: one step to enter, speak instead of type.
    • Treating privacy as the product: key entry, recordings stored privately.

    Case study

    The BEST Act: case study

    The BEST Act: case study

    From 50 interviews to a law. Product research applied to policy: founder interviews, a petition, a coalition and two testimonies before the DC Council, ending in a law that cut business licensing for every new business in Washington, DC.

    The 30-second version. Coaching founders only goes so far when the rules keep them out. The biggest barrier my founders hit was the cost and paperwork of getting licensed. I took their stories to the DC Council twice. Before the 2022 hearing I interviewed about 50 entrepreneurs, recruited other organizations to testify, emailed 5,000 people and ran a petition. The Business and Entrepreneurship Support to Thrive (BEST) Amendment Act became law on October 1, 2025. It consolidated more than 100 business license categories into 13 and lowered initial and renewal fees for every new business in the District.

    Case studyResearcher, organizer and witness
    Problem
    Licensing rules and fees kept DC micro-businesses informal.
    Outcome
    Law in effect October 2025: 100+ license categories cut to 13

    Problem

    A DC business license and its required fees were out of reach for micro-businesses earning under $100,000. Street vending licenses had been halted. Home occupancy permits were required before a business license. Founders with the greatest need could not grow their business legally.

    Who it is for

    Necessity-based entrepreneurs in Washington, DC, especially first-generation and immigrant founders, and the city agencies that license them.

    How it worked

    1. December 2018, first testimony. Weeks after the first Street Pitch, I testified before the Council's Committee of the Whole in support of the Micro-Business Startup Fee Relief Amendment Act, which would have cut basic license costs by 75% for businesses earning $100,000 or less, and asked for testing licenses so founders could try an idea before paying to make it official.
    2. 2021, research. With a Booz Allen Race and Social Equity grant, we surveyed vendors at Black Lives Matter Plaza and documented the barriers that keep necessity-based businesses informal.
    3. February 2022, second testimony. I interviewed about 50 entrepreneurs about their licensing experiences, recruited other organizations to testify, emailed 5,000 people and launched a petition with 196 signatures. I testified in support of the BEST Act and pushed past it: reinstate street vending licenses, drop occupancy permit requirements for low-risk businesses, and waive start-up fees for low-income entrepreneurs. The committee report records mine as the only testimony that addressed street vending.
    4. October 1, 2025, the result. The BEST Act took effect, consolidating 100+ license categories into 13 and lowering fees for every new business in DC. Separately, the Street Vendor Advancement Amendment Act passed unanimously in 2023.

    Key decisions

    • Lead with user research. Fifty interviews turned anecdotes into a pattern the Council could act on.
    • Build a coalition, not a solo ask. Other organizations testified alongside us.
    • Ask for more than the bill. Raising street vending when no one else did put it on the record.

    What I would do next

    Track license applications and fees paid by micro-businesses before and after the law, and publish the delta.

    Case study

    Kiya Tomlin Resort Partnership Strategy: case study

    Columbia brand partnership

    Case studyColumbia consulting team
    Problem
    A sustainable resort-wear brand needed its first resort partnerships.
    Outcome
    Recommended opening move: a flagship at The Breakers, Palm Beach

    A go-to-market strategy placing a sustainable resort-wear label into luxury wellness resorts.

    Deck ↗

    Kiya Tomlin Resort Partnership Strategy: case study

    A go-to-market strategy putting a sustainable resort-wear brand into luxury wellness resorts

    The 30-second version. A Columbia Business School brand-partnership engagement (co-prepared with a classmate) that built the go-to-market case for placing Kiya Tomlin, a sustainable bamboo resort-wear label, into luxury wellness resorts. The work delivered a competitive positioning memo and a ranked resort opportunity matrix, and recommended a concrete opening move: The Breakers, Palm Beach. This is strategy and market-research work, not a software build, and it demonstrates competitive analysis, customer segmentation, partnership structuring, and a defensible recommendation under a real deadline.

    Engagement: Columbia Business School brand-partnership project, with a classmate

    Timeline: Midterm deliverable, presentation March 3, 2026

    1. The strategic question

    Kiya Tomlin makes elevated, sustainable resort wear anchored by a signature sweatshirt dress: "polished ease," luxury comfort built on regenerated bamboo, fit-tested on real women. The brand sells direct but has limited presence where its ideal customer actually shops on vacation: the boutiques and gift shops of luxury wellness resorts. The question this engagement answered was where and how Kiya should enter resort retail to win the "daywear occasion" that no competitor currently owns.

    2. The market and the gap

    The customer is an affluent, wellness-conscious woman, 35 to 60, household income $250K and up, a frequent luxury-resort guest who values quality, sustainability, and understated elegance. The market is large: roughly $47B in global luxury wellness travel, with about 78% of resort guests buying in-stay retail. The gap is specific. Resort shops stock generic athleisure or formalwear, with nothing in the "polished ease" middle. No brand owns elevated bamboo resort sweatwear as a curated partner experience. Eberjey built a hotel business on sleepwear; the daywear occasion is wide open, and Kiya is positioned to own it.

    3. Approach

    The research was structured to mirror how resort partnerships are actually won, which is rarely on brand merit alone and usually on fit with what a resort already buys and how its boutique operates. The work covered:

    • A competitive set of premium resort, wellness, and athleisure brands that already appear in luxury hospitality retail and spa partnerships, mapped on positioning and price.
    • A shortlist of 10 to 12 luxury wellness resorts and resorts with high-activation spas and boutiques, scored as partnership targets.
    • A view of partnership mechanics: curated shop operators, pop-ups, collaborations, and the path to a buy.

    4. Findings and recommendation

    The competitive positioning memo placed Kiya in the open "polished ease" quadrant, distinct from both technical athleisure and resort formalwear, with sustainability and fit as the durable differentiators. The resort opportunity matrix ranked targets by customer overlap, boutique activation, and partnership feasibility. The recommended opening move is The Breakers in Palm Beach: a flagship that concentrates the target customer, runs a high-activation boutique, and gives Kiya a credible reference account to open the next set of doors.

    5. Deliverables

    • Competitive Positioning Memo: where Kiya sits in the landscape and why the "polished ease" position is defensible.
    • Resort Opportunity Matrix: 10 to 12 ranked targets with the scoring logic behind the ranking.
    • Opening-move recommendation: The Breakers, Palm Beach, with the rationale for leading there.

    6. What this project demonstrates

    • Competitive analysis. Mapped a crowded category and found the open position a brand could credibly own.
    • Customer segmentation. Defined a specific, high-value buyer and tied the recommendation to where she actually shops.
    • Partnership and GTM strategy. Structured the path to a retail partnership around how resorts really buy, not just brand fit.
    • Prioritization under constraint. Turned a broad market into a ranked, defensible shortlist and a single recommended first move, on a real deadline.
    • Executive communication. Packaged the analysis into a positioning memo, a decision matrix, and a presentation-ready deck.

    Case study

    Propio Google Ads Campaign

    Columbia Business School, Digital Marketing (B8680), October 2025

    Case studyColumbia Digital Marketing team
    Problem
    A non-alcoholic wine brand needed to know which audience to target.
    Outcome
    Sober-curious persona won (p = .025) on 28,552 impressions

    Context

    Propio is a woman-owned non-alcoholic wine brand launched in January 2025, made from real wine with the alcohol removed. Direct-to-consumer sales had slowed and the brand did not yet know who its core customer was. Our team ran a live Google Search campaign with a $150 budget to find out.

    The question

    Who converts: the sober-curious consumer who still wants to celebrate, or the wellness consumer who reads labels for sugar and calories?

    The test

    Two campaigns ran in parallel with separate keyword sets, headlines, and calls to action. "Mindful Socials" targeted urban adults 25 to 40 reducing alcohol with messages like "Celebrate Mindfully with Non-Alcoholic Rosé." "Performance and Recovery" targeted fitness-focused adults 25 to 45 with messages like "Low-Sugar White Wines That Won't Disrupt Your Training." Success metrics were click-through rate, time on site, and micro-conversions (email sign-ups and follows).

    What happened

    28,552 impressions, 178 clicks, and $125.74 spent across both campaigns. The sober-curious persona engaged at a significantly higher rate (p = .025). 90% of impressions came from mobile. About 55% of impressions landed on Friday and Saturday, with a spike at midnight. 92% of clicks came from the Display Network rather than Google Search, which told us the brand's reach problem is discovery, not intent. The top keyword in both groups was "alcohol alternative drinks," not "wine," which reshaped how Propio should describe itself.

    What I would do next

    Shift budget to the sober-curious persona and to mobile placements on Friday and Saturday evenings. Rewrite landing copy around "alcohol alternative" language. Add a sign-up incentive so the campaign produces owned audience data, not just clicks.

    Case study

    TikTok Youth Safety Redesign

    Columbia Business School, Tech Ethics, Team 6

    Case studyColumbia Tech Ethics team
    Problem
    TikTok's design rewards patterns that harm adolescents.
    Outcome
    A redesign with safer defaults for users under 18

    Context

    TikTok's algorithm rewards the same patterns that harm adolescents: infinite scroll, emotionally charged content, and attention treated as a commodity. The platform has faced COPPA lawsuits over age verification and parental consent, and tools like Family Pairing push the burden onto parents without real transparency. LGBTQ+ youth and Black teens face higher rates of cyberbullying and algorithmic harm.

    The method

    We evaluated the platform through three lenses. Consequentialism: the creative and community benefits are real, but the mental health harms to vulnerable users outweigh engagement gains. Duty-based ethics: TikTok owes minors informed consent, honest design, and protection it has not delivered. Virtue ethics: we built a team value card and tested each option against it.

    The recommendation

    Default well-being settings for users under 18, including time limits and a non-infinite feed. Family Pairing rebuilt so a parent can set it up in under two minutes and see what it does. Independent audits of algorithmic exposure for the groups most at risk, with results published. Age verification that does not rely on self-reporting alone.

    What it taught me

    Ethics work is product work. Every recommendation above is a feature decision with a tradeoff on engagement, and the job of a product manager is to make that tradeoff on purpose. I applied the same four-step method (facts, issues, stakeholders, frameworks) to the 23andMe genetic data case.

    Program record

    Street Pitch

    A program of Street Entrepreneurs, Washington, D.C., 2018 and 2020

    Technical.ly, People's Shark Tank ↗Full Street Entrepreneurs record ↗
    Program recordCreator and producer
    Problem
    Founders without wealthy networks had no friends-and-family round.
    Outcome
    300,000+ combined following reached through 50+ partners

    Overview

    Street Pitch was Street Entrepreneurs' annual founder showcase, built so that anyone could back a founder from home. I created and produced it as Founder of Street Entrepreneurs and ran two public editions: an in-person, livestreamed show in 2018 and a fully virtual one in 2020. From the 2018 press release: "If American Idol and Shark Tank had a baby, it would be called Street Pitch." My role: concept and format, partner and sponsor recruitment, the crossposting distribution model, judge recruitment, production with Hugo Creative and AXL, and fundraising for the finalists.

    2018: "Invest in Grit"

    More than 250 people packed the Inclusive Innovation Incubator on Georgia Avenue on November 27, 2018; seats ran out and guests stood along the walls. Eight founders pitched live, hosted by Brandon Andrews of ABC's Shark Tank casting team, to a panel of 21 investors, operators and D.C. officials, with American Sign Language interpretation from Gallaudet University. The show closed Global Entrepreneurship Week's FoundersRising awards for the D.C. region. Sponsors: Amazon Web Services, Make My Day CPA, Lyft, In3, Pernix Solutions and Georgetown McDonough Executive Education. More than 30 community organizations and city offices recruited and supported the founders.

    The distribution model

    Instead of buying reach, I recruited partners to stream and share the broadcast to their own followers. Partners were granted Facebook Live crosspost permission for the 7 to 9 pm window; others shared the stream. More than 50 organizations and supporters carried the show, including Global Entrepreneurship Week USA, Union Kitchen, DC Central Kitchen, Hispanic Heritage Foundation, Global Good Fund, Charlotte Clymer and Rantt Media, to a combined following of more than 300,000. The press release set a goal of one million virtual viewers; actual views were not measured.

    2019: pitching moves into the program

    With the accelerator organized into Founder Retreats, pitching moved into ward-level sessions where each cohort selected the founders who would go on to the next showcase. That peer selection became the backbone of the 2020 edition.

    2020: virtual, in lockdown

    On December 15, 2020, seven peer-selected finalists were filmed in advance, each with a mini-documentary shot at landmarks across all eight wards. The show premiered as a Facebook Live watch party, shared by partners and more than 100 community groups, with a panel of 20 judges from investors, companies and D.C. government. The broadcast reached 6,400 people and earned 2,489 engagements. Each finalist received $5,000 in Amazon Web Services credits, a seat in SEED SPOT's two-day accelerator, and a micro-grant funded by a board-run silent auction that also gave 29 more local entrepreneurs a marketing platform.

    Metrics at a glance (2018 / 2020)

    Founders featured: 8 / 7 (peer-selected). Judges: 21 / 20. In-person audience: 250+ / not applicable. Broadcast and promotion partners: 50+ / partners plus 100+ community groups. Combined partner following: 300,000+ / not measured. Broadcast reach: not measured / 6,400 people. Engagements: not measured / 2,489. Sponsors: 7 / AWS finalist credits. Every number is backed by a document in the Street Entrepreneurs archive.

    What I learned

    Distribution can be borrowed: a volunteer-run nonprofit with no ad budget reached a 300,000-plus audience by turning partners into broadcasters, and the same model carried the show online when the room closed. Peer selection builds buy-in: letting cohorts choose their own finalists gave the 2020 show its credibility. Measure what you can defend: in 2018 I tracked potential audience, not actual views; by 2020 I reported platform reach and engagement, which hold up.

    Written by Juliana Cardona Mejia. Every product in the portfolio has a PRD like this one.
